# Scheduled and Tribal Areas — Live Session Edition

India does not govern every tribal region through one template. Two constitutional systems sit side by side. One is protective: the President notifies the territory, the Governor reports and regulates, the Union directs, and an advisory council speaks. The other is self-governing: elected district councils legislate on land and custom, run courts, levy taxes and manage local services. On top of the first system Parliament later built a statutory layer of village self-rule through PESA and a statutory layer of forest-right recognition through the Forest Rights Act.

Most answers fail here for one reason: the learner memorises two lists and then swaps a verb. The President becomes the Governor, advice becomes legislation, consultation becomes consent, and a Governor's assent becomes a President's assent. This session is therefore built paragraph by paragraph. We start with the problem the Schedules were designed to solve, fix the Article 244 vocabulary, teach each Schedule in its own order, compare them only after both are fully understood, add the statutory rights layer, and finish with land conflict, accountability, extension debates and answer architecture.

## Lesson 1: Why Two Tribal-Area Systems Exist: Part X, Substantive Equality and the Colonial Inheritance

Progress: 1/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: The constitutional rationale for differentiated tribal-area administration and its historical sources

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — standard Indian Polity chapters on Scheduled and Tribal Areas, read against the current text of Part X because textbook summaries compress the Schedules into bullet lists
CA search: "new constitutional rationale, commission or Part X amendment on Scheduled Areas and tribal areas in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No amendment to Part X, the Fifth Schedule or the Sixth Schedule was located for this period, so the rationale taught here is the settled constitutional design as on 1 October 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one equality problem, two institutional answers

```text
ONE UNIFORM ADMINISTRATIVE MODEL FOR EVERY REGION
                     |
         what the model meets on the ground
                     |
  remote terrain  +  living customary institutions
  land and forest dependence  +  historical dispossession
  concentrated extractive pressure  +  thin political voice
                     |
        identical rules, unequal bargaining power
                     |
   SUBSTANTIVE EQUALITY NEEDS DIFFERENTIATED GOVERNANCE
                     |
         +-----------+-----------+
         |                       |
  FIFTH SCHEDULE           SIXTH SCHEDULE
  protective supervision   elected territorial autonomy
  President | Governor     District and Regional Councils
  advisory council         laws, courts, taxes, services
```

Caption: both Schedules answer the same question — how do you stop formally equal rules from producing substantively unequal outcomes — but they choose different institutions to do it.

### 1. Begin with the learner's objection

If Article 14 promises equality before the law, why does the Constitution carve out territories and administer them differently? The objection is worth stating in its strongest form, because a weak version of it produces a weak answer.

The reply is not "tribes need special privileges." It is that equality of rules and equality of outcome part company whenever the parties to a transaction start from radically different positions. A money-lending contract, a land sale, a mining lease and a development plan all look neutral on paper. Where one party holds written records, legal advice, capital and administrative access, and the other holds customary occupation, oral entitlement and no realistic forum, the neutral rule transfers the asset in one direction. Differentiated administration is therefore a device of substantive equality, not an exception to it.

Part X of the Constitution — containing Articles 244 and 244A — is the textual home of that device. It recognises that ordinary State administration may need constitutional modification in specified regions.

### 2. What actually makes these regions different

Four features recur, and each maps onto a specific institutional response later in the session.

| Feature of the region | Governance risk it creates | Where the Constitution or statute responds |
|---|---|---|
| Land and forest dependence for livelihood | Loss of land is loss of subsistence, not loss of an asset | Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2); PESA section 4(m)(iii); Forest Rights Act |
| Living customary institutions and dispute resolution | General law can displace a functioning local order | Sixth Schedule paragraphs 3 and 4; PESA sections 4(a), 4(b) and 4(d) |
| Remote terrain and thin administrative presence | Entitlements exist on paper but not in practice | Article 275(1) grants; Fifth Schedule paragraph 3 reporting |
| Concentrated extractive interest in minerals and forests | A single project can reshape an entire community's future | PESA sections 4(i), 4(k) and 4(l); Sixth Schedule paragraph 9 |

The point of the table is not the list. It is the discipline: every protective institution in this topic exists because of a named risk, and an answer that states the institution without the risk reads like recall rather than analysis.

### 3. The colonial inheritance — continuity of form, change of purpose

The idea of administratively separating tribal tracts did not begin in 1950.

```text
1874  Scheduled Districts Act
        "scheduled districts" set apart from ordinary courts and regulations
          |
1919  Government of India Act -> the category "backward tracts"
          |
1935  Government of India Act, ss. 91-92
        creates "EXCLUDED" and "PARTIALLY EXCLUDED" areas
        no federal or provincial law applies unless the GOVERNOR
          so directs by public notification
        Governor may make regulations for peace and good government
        administration insulated from representative provincial institutions
          |
1946-49  Constituent Assembly tribal-area subcommittees
          |
1950  Constitution retains differentiation, changes its purpose
```

Note the drafting that the 1935 Act bequeathed: laws apply only if the Governor directs by public notification, and the Governor may make regulations for peace and good government. Both devices survive in Fifth Schedule paragraph 5 and Sixth Schedule paragraph 19 — though, as Lesson 12 will show, the two constitutional paragraphs set the default in **opposite** directions. The machinery is inherited; the purpose is not.

The colonial design protected tribal tracts by keeping them out of politics. The constitutional design protects them by bringing rights, welfare and, in the north-east, elected self-government in. The form is continuous; the purpose is not. A good answer says exactly this and refuses to treat the Schedules as a surviving colonial relic.

### 4. Two subcommittees, two Schedules

The Constituent Assembly did not produce one scheme because its two advisory bodies were studying different realities.

| Subcommittee | Field examined | Constitutional outcome |
|---|---|---|
| Chaired by A.V. Thakkar | Excluded and partially excluded areas outside Assam | Fifth Schedule — protective administration under President, Governor and an advisory council |
| Chaired by Gopinath Bordoloi | Tribal and excluded areas of Assam and the north-eastern frontier | Sixth Schedule — autonomous districts and regions with elected councils |

The divergence was evidential, not arbitrary. In the north-eastern hill regions, communities had retained their own political institutions, land systems and customary law to a degree that made elected territorial self-government a workable institutional form. Elsewhere, tribal populations were more dispersed and more deeply interleaved with non-tribal settlement, so the Assembly chose protection of land, credit and administration over territorial self-rule.

### 5. The later statutory layer

The 1950 settlement left one gap that became visible once local government was constitutionalised in 1992: the Fifth Schedule had a guardian but no village-level democratic institution of its own.

```text
Fifth Schedule, 1950
  protective administration through President, Governor, State and TAC
          |
Part IX, 1992: Panchayats become constitutional
          |
Article 243M(1): Part IX does not apply to Scheduled Areas or tribal areas
          |
Article 243M(4)(b): Parliament may extend Part IX with exceptions and modifications
          |
PESA, 1996: habitation village + Gram Sabha powers in Fifth Schedule areas
          |
Forest Rights Act, 2006: recognition and vesting of forest rights, Gram Sabha-led
```

So the modern topic has three strata — constitutional protection, constitutional self-government, and statutory self-rule plus tenure. Keeping them apart is the whole examination skill.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the honest reply

**Objection.** Differentiated administration can become permanent tutelage. If the Governor must report, the Union must direct and an advisory council may only advise, the community remains an object of administration rather than its author.

**Reply.** The objection is accurate as a description of risk, not as a description of design. The Fifth Schedule's protective powers are real and bounded by purpose and law; PESA and the Forest Rights Act were enacted precisely to convert protection into participation; and the Sixth Schedule shows that the Constitution was willing to go all the way to elected territorial government where conditions allowed.

**Residual.** The reply does not close the argument. Protection that is never exercised, and participation that exists only in notified rules, both leave the community in the position the objection describes. That is why implementation evidence — reports, rules, records and remedies — carries so much weight in the later lessons.

### UPSC application

- Open any rationale answer with substantive equality, not with "tribes are backward"; the second framing is both inaccurate and unexaminable.
- Always name Part X and Article 244 before describing institutions.
- Use the colonial-continuity point as a qualification, never as the thesis.
- Thakkar and Bordoloi are the two safe subcommittee names; attach each to the correct field.
- Remember that PESA and the Forest Rights Act are statutes, not Schedules; this distinction earns marks in evaluative questions.

### Revision notes

1. Part X contains Articles 244 and 244A.
2. Article 244 is the gateway; the institutions live inside the Fifth and Sixth Schedules.
3. The Schedules are devices of substantive equality, not exceptions to equality.
4. Four recurring risks: land dependence, living custom, thin administration, extractive pressure.
5. Colonial route: Scheduled Districts Act, 1874 to excluded and partially excluded areas under the Government of India Act, 1935.
6. Colonial purpose was insulation from representative institutions; constitutional purpose is rights, welfare and self-government.
7. Thakkar subcommittee — areas outside Assam; Bordoloi subcommittee — Assam and the north-eastern tribal and excluded areas.
8. Fifth Schedule theory: protective supervision. Sixth Schedule theory: territorial self-government.
9. Article 243M(1) excluded Scheduled Areas and tribal areas from Part IX.
10. Article 243M(4)(b) is the enabling clause Parliament used to enact PESA in 1996.
11. The Forest Rights Act, 2006 added tenure recognition and community forest governance.
12. The paternalism objection is strongest where protective powers lie dormant.
13. Three strata to keep separate: constitutional protection, constitutional self-government, statutory self-rule and tenure.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did the Constituent Assembly adopt two different tribal-area systems rather than one uniform scheme?

**Model answer:** Because its two advisory subcommittees examined different institutional realities. In the Assam and north-eastern hill regions studied under Gopinath Bordoloi, communities retained functioning political institutions, land systems and customary law, so elected territorial self-government through autonomous districts was workable. In the areas outside Assam studied under A.V. Thakkar, tribal populations were more dispersed and interleaved with non-tribal settlement, so the Assembly chose protection of land, credit and administration under the President and Governor. The difference is evidential, not a ranking of communities.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Sixth Schedule as simply a "better" version of the Fifth. They embody different theories of safeguarding — guardianship and self-rule — and each has characteristic strengths and failures, which later lessons examine separately.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** Explain the constitutional rationale for separate administration of Scheduled Areas and tribal areas in India.

#### Model solution

Part X, through Article 244, authorises differentiated administration where uniform rules would reproduce unequal outcomes. Tribal regions combine land and forest dependence, living customary institutions, thin administrative reach and concentrated extractive pressure; formally neutral law in such conditions transfers land, credit and decision-making away from the community. Differentiation is therefore substantive equality, not privilege.

The design has two forms. Article 244(1) applies the Fifth Schedule: Presidential declaration of territory, continuing State administration under the Governor's reporting duty, Union directions, an advisory council and protective regulations on land and money-lending. Article 244(2) applies the Sixth Schedule: elected District and Regional Councils with specified legislative, executive, judicial and fiscal powers. The Thakkar and Bordoloi subcommittees produced this divergence from different field evidence.

PESA, 1996 and the Forest Rights Act, 2006 later converted protection into participation. Differentiation is legitimate only while it produces agency and accountability, not permanent tutelage.

**Rubric (10):** substantive-equality rationale 2; named risks of uniform administration 2; Article 244(1) and 244(2) institutional contrast 3; historical or statutory dimension 2; qualified normative conclusion 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

We now know why two systems exist. Before studying either in detail we must fix the provision map and the four nouns the examiner swaps most often — Scheduled Area, tribal area, Scheduled Tribe and autonomous district. That is the next lesson.

## Lesson 2: The Article 244 Master Map and the Vocabulary That Decides Close Options

Progress: 2/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Articles 244, 244A, 275, 339 and 342 and the exact meaning of the topic's core terms

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — standard Polity tables on Articles relating to Scheduled and Tribal Areas, corrected against the current constitutional text because textbook tables omit Article 275(1-A) and the clause-level structure of Article 244A
CA search: "amendment or official reinterpretation of Article 244, 244A, 275, 339 or 342 of the Constitution of India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No amendment or authoritative reinterpretation of these Articles was located, so the provision map below is the text in force on 1 October 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the provision spine

| Provision | Territorial reach | What it actually does |
|---|---|---|
| Article 244(1) | Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in any State other than Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram | Applies the Fifth Schedule |
| Article 244(2) | Tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram | Applies the Sixth Schedule |
| Article 244A | Assam only | Lets Parliament form an autonomous State within Assam, with a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both |
| Article 275(1), first proviso | States with Scheduled Areas or ST welfare needs | Grants-in-aid charged on the Consolidated Fund of India |
| Article 275(1), second proviso and 275(1-A) | Assam, and any autonomous State formed under 244A | Dedicated sums for Part I tribal areas, and their apportionment after an autonomous State is formed |
| Article 339(1) | Scheduled Areas and ST welfare across States | Presidential commission on administration and welfare |
| Article 339(2) | Any State | Union directions on drawing up and executing specified ST-welfare schemes |
| Article 342 | Any State or Union territory | President specifies Scheduled Tribes; Parliament alters the list by law |

Caption: Article 244 is only a switch. It decides which Schedule applies; everything institutional sits inside the Schedules, and the fiscal and supervisory support sits in Articles 275 and 339.

### 1. Article 244 read slowly

Article 244(1) says the provisions of the Fifth Schedule shall apply to the administration and control of the Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in any State other than the four named States. Two things follow immediately, and both are examined.

First, the Fifth Schedule governs **two objects**, not one: Scheduled Areas *and* Scheduled Tribes. That is why the Schedule can authorise a Tribes Advisory Council in a State that has Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas.

Second, the exclusion is **State-wise, not area-wise**. Paragraph 1 of the Fifth Schedule reinforces this by defining "State" in that Schedule so as to exclude Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. Those four States are simply outside the Fifth Schedule's field.

Article 244(2) then says the provisions of the Sixth Schedule shall apply to the administration of the tribal areas in those four States. Note what it omits: it does not speak of Scheduled Tribes generally, only of **tribal areas**. The Sixth Schedule is territorial in a way the Fifth is not.

A small but decisive drafting detail: the Sixth Schedule's own heading is referenced to **Articles 244(2) and 275(1)**. Its financial anchor is written into its title, which is why the Article 275 grant route belongs in this topic rather than in general federal finance.

### 2. Article 244A in full, because it is usually taught in half

Most summaries reduce Article 244A to "autonomous State within Assam." The clause structure carries more.

```text
Article 244A(1)
  Parliament may BY LAW form within the State of Assam
  an autonomous State comprising, wholly or in part,
  all or any of the tribal areas in PART I of the paragraph 20 table
  and may create for it
      (a) a Legislature, elected or partly nominated and partly elected
      (b) a Council of Ministers
      or both
            |
Article 244A(2) such a law may in particular
      (a) specify State List / Concurrent List matters for its legislature,
          with or without excluding the Assam Legislature
      (b) define the reach of its executive power
      (c) assign Assam's taxes attributable to it
      (d) make references to "a State" include the autonomous State
      (e) add supplemental, incidental and consequential provisions
            |
Article 244A(3) amendments touching (2)(a) or (2)(b) need
      a TWO-THIRDS majority of members present and voting in EACH House
            |
Article 244A(4) such a law is NOT an Article 368 amendment,
      even if it amends the Constitution in effect
```

Three examinable consequences. The autonomous State would be a *constitutionally distinct unit within Assam*, not a council. Its creation needs only ordinary parliamentary law, yet its core legislative and executive specification is entrenched by a special two-thirds requirement for later change. And Article 275(1-A) already provides for how Union grants would be apportioned between Assam and such an autonomous State — the Constitution has pre-wired the finance.

#### The part most summaries get wrong: Article 244A has been used

It is common to read that Article 244A "has never been used." That is inaccurate, and the correction is itself examinable, because the single use explains both why the clause is drafted as it is and why no autonomous State exists today.

✅ **Fact.** Article 244A was inserted by **section 2 of the Constitution (Twenty-second Amendment) Act, 1969**, which bears the date **25 September 1969** and contains no commencement provision beyond its short title, so the article took effect from that date. Two companion insertions in the same short Act are worth storing: **section 3** inserted **Article 275(1-A)**, the grants clause for such an autonomous State, and **section 4** inserted **Article 371B** for Assam. Parliament then acted on the new power almost at once. The **Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 (Act 55 of 1969)**, enacted on 29 December 1969, carries the long title "An Act to provide for the formation within the State of Assam of an autonomous State to be known as Meghalaya and for matters connected therewith." Section 3(1) provides that "on and from the appointed day, there shall be formed within the State of Assam an autonomous State to be known as Meghalaya", comprising the United Khasi-Jaintia Hills District and the Garo Hills District; section 3(2) allowed the North Cachar Hills or Mikir Hills districts to join if their District Councils so resolved by a two-thirds majority.

```text
            THE ONE USE OF ARTICLE 244A

25 Sep    Constitution (Twenty-second Amendment) Act, 1969, s. 2
1969      INSERTS ARTICLE 244A
               |   (s. 3 inserts Article 275(1-A); s. 4, Article 371B)
               |
29 Dec    ASSAM REORGANISATION (MEGHALAYA) ACT, 1969 (55 of 1969)
1969      enacted -- an autonomous State to be known as Meghalaya,
          formed WITHIN the State of Assam
               |
2 Apr     THE APPOINTED DAY. The autonomous State exists, with
1970         a Legislative Assembly            (ss. 11 to 32)
             a Council of Ministers            (ss. 6 and 7)
             its own extent of legislative power   (s. 33)
             its own Consolidated Fund         (s. 51)
             its own Advocate-General          (s. 8)
          and the SAME Act, by s. 74 and its FOURTH SCHEDULE,
          amends the SIXTH SCHEDULE itself
               |
21 Jan    NORTH-EASTERN AREAS (REORGANISATION) ACT, 1971 makes
1972      Meghalaya a FULL STATE. The autonomous State ends --
          by succeeding, not by failing
               |
as on     NO autonomous State exists under Article 244A.
1 Oct     The power is DORMANT, not untried.
2026
```

Caption: the clause was used once, for a little over twenty-one months, and was overtaken by full Statehood for the same territory.

Three details repay attention. First, the Act was brought into force in stages from 12 January 1970, and the **appointed day for the formation itself was 2 April 1970** — the date from which the Sixth Schedule's own amendment footnotes record section 74 and the Fourth Schedule as taking effect. Second, the structure the Act created is exactly what Article 244A(1) and (2) describe on paper: a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, a defined legislative field and a separate Consolidated Fund. The clause is therefore not an abstraction; there is a worked example of what it produces. Third, the autonomous State ceased on 21 January 1972, not because the device failed but because the same territory was raised to full Statehood by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 — the Act that, on the same day, rewrote Article 244(2) to name Meghalaya in its own right.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** That trajectory is the honest answer to "why has Article 244A not been used since?" In the one case where the political demand was strong enough to trigger it, the autonomous State proved to be a staging post rather than a destination. The clause survives as a standing option for a half-way house between a district council and a State, and it is precisely that half-way character that makes it relevant whenever an existing Assam council presses for more than the Sixth Schedule gives.

> 🔑 Say "used once, for Meghalaya, between 1970 and 1972, and dormant since", never "never used" and never "currently in operation."

### 3. The four nouns examiners swap

| Term | Legal source | Who creates or organises it | What it is not |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scheduled Area | Fifth Schedule, paragraph 6 | President, by order; consultation with the Governor where paragraph 6 requires it | Not every area with a Scheduled Tribe population; not a whole State |
| Tribal area | Sixth Schedule, paragraph 20 and its table | Constitutionally listed; the Governor organises and reorganises districts and regions under paragraph 1(3) | Not a Fifth Schedule label; not declared by the President |
| Scheduled Tribe | Article 342 | President by public notification for a State or Union territory, after consulting the Governor in the case of a State; Parliament includes or excludes by law | Not declared by the Governor; not uniform across States |
| Autonomous district or region | Sixth Schedule, paragraphs 1 and 2 | Governor, by public notification | Not a sovereign enclave; not outside State executive authority |

> **Memory rule:** the President schedules the **area** and specifies the **tribe**, by two different constitutional routes; the Governor organises the **district** and the **region**; Parliament alters the **tribe list** and may amend either Schedule by ordinary law.

### 4. Article 342 precisely

Article 342(1) empowers the President, with respect to any State or Union territory and — where it is a State — after consultation with the Governor, to specify by public notification the tribes, tribal communities, or parts of or groups within them, which shall for the purposes of the Constitution be deemed to be Scheduled Tribes in relation to that State or Union territory.

Article 342(2) then provides that Parliament may by law include in, or exclude from, that list; and that **save by such a law, a notification under clause (1) shall not be varied by any subsequent notification**.

That last limb is the sleeper. Once the first list is notified for a State, the President cannot simply re-notify a change; alteration runs through Parliament. The identical architecture — one originating order, then a statutory route for change — reappears in Fifth Schedule paragraph 6(2).

Three further precisions worth carrying:

- ST status is **State or Union-territory specific**. The same community may be a Scheduled Tribe in one State and not in another, because the notification is made in relation to a State or Union territory.
- Consultation with the Governor is required for a State; the clause does not impose that step for a Union territory.
- Article 342 specifies the **tribe**; it says nothing about territory. Scheduling an area and specifying a tribe are independent constitutional acts.

### 5. Articles 275 and 339 in their place

Article 275(1)'s first proviso creates a dedicated grant route: capital and recurring sums from the Consolidated Fund of India to enable a State to meet the costs of development schemes undertaken with the approval of the Government of India for promoting the welfare of Scheduled Tribes in that State, **or** for raising the level of administration of the Scheduled Areas in it to that of the rest of the State. The second proviso adds Assam-specific sums tied to the Part I tribal areas, and Article 275(1-A) deals with apportionment once an Article 244A autonomous State exists. Article 275(2) lets the President act by order until Parliament legislates, but once a Finance Commission has been constituted, no such order may be made except after considering its recommendations.

Article 339(1) empowers the President at any time, and required the President at the expiration of ten years from the commencement of the Constitution, to appoint by order a Commission to report on the administration of the Scheduled Areas and the welfare of the Scheduled Tribes in the States, defining its composition, powers and procedure. Article 339(2) extends Union executive power to giving directions to a State as to the drawing up and execution of schemes specified in the direction to be essential for ST welfare.

### 6. A comparison that prevents a classic error

| Direction given by the Union | Source | Subject of the direction |
|---|---|---|
| On the administration of Scheduled Areas | Fifth Schedule, paragraph 3 | How the Scheduled Area is administered |
| On drawing up and executing ST-welfare schemes | Article 339(2) | Specified welfare schemes, anywhere in a State |

They overlap in spirit and differ in text. Paragraph 3 attaches to scheduled territory; Article 339(2) attaches to scheme-specific welfare and is not territorially confined to Scheduled Areas. Writing one when the question asks for the other costs marks in both Prelims and Mains.

### UPSC application

- State Article 244(1) and 244(2) before naming any institution; it converts recall into a map.
- When Assam appears in a question, check whether the demand is the Sixth Schedule, paragraph 3A or 3B, or Article 244A — these are three different things.
- Article 244A(4), Fifth Schedule paragraph 7 and Sixth Schedule paragraph 21 share one idea: Parliament can do constitutionally significant work here by ordinary law.
- For any ST-status question, the triad is President notifies, Governor is consulted for a State, Parliament alters.
- Use Article 275(1) when a question asks how tribal administration is financed; use Article 339 when it asks how it is supervised.

### Revision notes

1. Article 244(1): Fifth Schedule applies to Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in States other than Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.
2. Article 244(2): Sixth Schedule applies to tribal areas in those four States.
3. Fifth Schedule paragraph 1 defines "State" in that Schedule to exclude the four Sixth Schedule States.
4. The Sixth Schedule's heading is referenced to Articles 244(2) and 275(1).
5. Article 244A: Parliament may by law form an autonomous State within Assam from Part I tribal areas.
6. Article 244A(1) allows a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both.
7. Article 244A(3) requires a two-thirds majority of members present and voting in each House to amend specified legislative or executive provisions.
8. Article 244A(4) says such a law is not an Article 368 amendment.
9. Article 244A has been used once: the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 formed the autonomous State of Meghalaya within Assam on the appointed day, 2 April 1970, and it ended on 21 January 1972 when Meghalaya became a full State. No autonomous State exists under Article 244A today.
10. Article 275(1) first proviso: grants for ST welfare schemes or for raising Scheduled Area administration to the State's general level.
11. Article 275(1-A) apportions those sums once an Article 244A autonomous State is formed.
12. Article 339(1): presidential commission, mandatory after ten years from commencement, permissible at any time.
13. Article 339(2): Union directions on specified essential ST-welfare schemes.
14. Article 342(1): President specifies STs for a State or Union territory, consulting the Governor for a State.
15. Article 342(2): Parliament alters the list; the original notification cannot otherwise be varied.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State claims that because Article 244A exists, its autonomous district council is already an "autonomous State." Why is that wrong, and what would actually be required?

**Model answer:** An autonomous district council exists under Sixth Schedule paragraphs 1 and 2 and exercises specified powers within the State. Article 244A is a separate enabling power under which **Parliament** may by law form an autonomous State within **Assam**, comprising Part I tribal areas, and create a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both, with a specified State or Concurrent List field. It applies only to Assam, requires an enacted parliamentary law, and carries its own entrenchment in Article 244A(3). It has been exercised once — the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 formed the autonomous State of Meghalaya on 2 April 1970, which lasted until full Statehood on 21 January 1972 — and no autonomous State exists under it now. Unless and until Parliament passes such a law again, a council remains a council.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading Article 244A as describing the current status of the Bodoland, Karbi Anglong or Dima Hasao councils. It describes a unit that must be separately enacted; it existed once, for Meghalaya, and does not exist today.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** Discuss Article 244A as a constitutional device, distinguishing it from the ordinary Sixth Schedule arrangement.

#### Model solution

Article 244A permits Parliament by law to form within Assam an autonomous State comprising, wholly or in part, tribal areas in Part I of the paragraph 20 table, with a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both.

Its reach exceeds a council's. Clause (2) allows Parliament to assign State List and Concurrent List matters to that legislature, define its executive power, assign attributable Assam taxes, and make references to "a State" include it. Clause (3) requires a two-thirds majority of members present and voting in each House to amend that specification; clause (4) declares the law is not an Article 368 amendment.

The power has been used once. The Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 created the autonomous State of Meghalaya on 2 April 1970; it ended with Statehood on 21 January 1972. A Sixth Schedule council legislates on paragraph 3 subjects within State authority. Article 244A is dormant, not untried.

**Rubric (10):** exact Article 244A(1) content 3; clause (2) enlargements 2; clause (3) entrenchment 1; clause (4) non-368 character 1; the single use for Meghalaya between 1970 and 1972 2; contrast with a Sixth Schedule council 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

The map is set. The first institution to build is territory itself: how an area becomes a Scheduled Area, who may change it, and what the official identification criteria can and cannot do.

---

## Lesson 3: Declaring a Scheduled Area: Paragraph 6, the Ten-State Map and the Administrative Criteria

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Fifth Schedule paragraph 6 declaration and alteration, paragraph 7 amendment, the State inventory and the identification criteria

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity chapter treatment of Scheduled Area declaration and the identification criteria, with the exact paragraph 6 clause structure taken from the constitutional text because textbooks compress it into one sentence
CA search: "new Presidential Order declaring or altering Scheduled Areas in India, or change in the number of Fifth Schedule States, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No fresh Scheduled Areas Order and no change to the officially listed ten-State inventory was located, so the map below stands as on 1 October 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: how territory acquires and loses Scheduled Area status

```text
      territorial facts | State reorganisation | review need
                             |
            +----------------+----------------+
            |                                 |
  administrative criteria                 constitutional power
  (guide identification)                  (creates legal status)
   tribal preponderance                   para 6(1): President declares
   compact, reasonable size               6(2)(a): cease to be scheduled
   viable unit: district/block/taluk      6(2)(aa): increase, after consulting
   comparative economic backwardness                the Governor
            |                             6(2)(b): alter ONLY by rectification
            |                                      of boundaries
            |                             6(2)(c): on boundary change, admission
            |                                      or new State, declare territory
            |                                      not previously in any State
            |                             6(2)(d): rescind and, consulting the
            |                                      Governor, redefine afresh
            +----------------+----------------+
                             |
                   PRESIDENTIAL ORDER
                             |
        only the notified territory is a Scheduled Area
                             |
   closing words of para 6(2): save as aforesaid, the order under
   6(1) shall not be varied by any subsequent order
```

Caption: criteria explain why an area is chosen; only the Presidential Order makes it a Scheduled Area; and the closing words of paragraph 6(2) stop the status from being changed by any route other than those listed.

### 1. The power, clause by clause

Paragraph 6(1) is a definition disguised as a power: "In this Constitution, the expression 'Scheduled Areas' means such areas as the President may by order declare to be Scheduled Areas." The constitutional meaning of the term is therefore whatever the operative orders say it is. There is no judicially discoverable "natural" Scheduled Area.

Paragraph 6(2) then lists what the President may do at any time by order:

- **6(2)(a)** direct that the whole or any specified part of a Scheduled Area shall cease to be one;
- **6(2)(aa)** increase the area of any Scheduled Area in a State **after consultation with the Governor** of that State;
- **6(2)(b)** alter a Scheduled Area, **but only by way of rectification of boundaries**;
- **6(2)(c)** on any alteration of the boundaries of a State, or on the admission into the Union or establishment of a new State, declare territory **not previously included in any State** to be, or form part of, a Scheduled Area;
- **6(2)(d)** rescind orders in relation to a State or States and, **in consultation with the Governor** concerned, make fresh orders redefining the Scheduled Areas.

Read the consultation requirement precisely. It is written into sub-clauses (aa) and (d). It is not written into (a), (b) or (c). Many summaries flatten this into "the President acts in consultation with the Governor" for every operation; the text is more differentiated.

Then come the closing words, easy to skip and genuinely examinable: any such order may contain incidental and consequential provisions, "but save as aforesaid, the order made under sub-paragraph (1) of this paragraph shall not be varied by any subsequent order." The founding declaration is stable except through the routes listed.

### 2. Consultation is not consent

This distinction decides close options and strengthens Mains analysis.

| Step | What it means | What it does not mean |
|---|---|---|
| Consultation with the Governor | The Governor's views must genuinely be sought and considered where the Schedule requires it | The Governor cannot veto, approve, or make the order |
| Presidential order | Creates, enlarges, rectifies, rescinds or redefines status | It is a Union executive instrument, not a State notification |
| Satisfaction of criteria | Explains and disciplines the choice | It does not, by itself, confer status |

### 3. The identification criteria

Official administrative practice identifies four criteria for declaring an area a Scheduled Area, and standard Polity sources reproduce them in the same form:

1. preponderance of tribal population;
2. compactness and reasonable size of the area;
3. a viable administrative entity, such as a district, block or taluk; and
4. economic backwardness of the area compared with neighbouring areas.

These are not spelt out in the Constitution. They became well established through administrative practice and the work of the commission on Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes. That origin matters: the criteria discipline executive choice, and a notification that ignored them entirely would be open to challenge as arbitrary — but satisfying them creates no entitlement to notification.

### 4. The ten-State map

```text
WEST AND CENTRAL BELT
Gujarat | Rajasthan | Maharashtra | Madhya Pradesh | Chhattisgarh

EASTERN BELT
Jharkhand | Odisha

SOUTH AND DECCAN
Andhra Pradesh | Telangana

HIMALAYAN
Himachal Pradesh
```

Ten States have notified Scheduled Areas, on official central listings and in the standard Polity sources. Two cautions ride with this list.

First, **inclusion of a State does not schedule the State**. Only the territory covered by operative Presidential Orders has the status. A statement that "Chhattisgarh is a Scheduled Area" is wrong; parts of Chhattisgarh are.

Second, district names and boundaries change through State reorganisation and administrative restructuring, and orders have been reissued accordingly — Telangana's coverage, for instance, derives from the application of pre-existing Andhra Pradesh orders after reorganisation. Memorise the State inventory and the declaration mechanism; verify a granular district list against the current order before quoting it.

### 5. Paragraph 7: amending the Schedule itself

Paragraph 7(1) allows Parliament from time to time by law to amend the Fifth Schedule by addition, variation or repeal. Paragraph 7(2) declares that no such law shall be deemed to be an amendment of the Constitution for the purposes of Article 368.

This is structural, not a footnote. The Fifth Schedule's own text can be changed by **ordinary legislation**. The Fifth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1976 did exactly that: it empowered the President to increase the area of a Scheduled Area after consulting the Governor, and to rescind an order and make fresh redefining orders in consultation with the Governor — which is why sub-clauses (aa) and (d) read as they do. The Sixth Schedule carries the identical device in paragraph 21, and Article 244A(4) repeats the idea for an autonomous-State law.

### 6. Objection and reply

**Objection.** If both the territory and the Schedule's own text can be changed without an Article 368 amendment, the protection looks fragile.

**Reply.** Flexibility is deliberate. Tribal demography, administrative boundaries and State reorganisation change; a protection frozen behind a constituent-power threshold would decay into inaccuracy. Parliament remains accountable, orders remain open to review for arbitrariness, and the closing words of paragraph 6(2) prevent casual variation of the founding order.

**Residual.** Flexibility cuts both ways. De-scheduling under paragraph 6(2)(a) is as available as scheduling, and the Constitution attaches no consultation requirement to it. The real safeguard there is political and evidentiary rather than textual.

### UPSC application

- The single most repeated proposition in this topic is "the President declares Scheduled Areas." Know it, and know that the Governor only advises where the paragraph says so.
- Attach consultation to paragraph 6(2)(aa) and (d) specifically, not to the whole paragraph.
- Quote the four criteria as administrative, not constitutional.
- Use paragraph 7 whenever a question asks about flexibility, amendment or the comparative rigidity of tribal safeguards.
- Never convert "ten States have Scheduled Areas" into "ten States are Scheduled Areas."

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 6(1): "Scheduled Areas" means areas the President declares by order.
2. Paragraph 6(2)(a): cease to be a Scheduled Area, in whole or specified part.
3. Paragraph 6(2)(aa): increase, after consultation with the Governor.
4. Paragraph 6(2)(b): alter only by rectification of boundaries.
5. Paragraph 6(2)(c): declare territory not previously in any State, on boundary change, admission or new-State establishment.
6. Paragraph 6(2)(d): rescind and, consulting the Governor, make fresh redefining orders.
7. Closing words: save as aforesaid, the paragraph 6(1) order shall not be varied by a later order.
8. Consultation is not consent, and is textually confined to (aa) and (d).
9. Four administrative criteria: tribal preponderance; compactness and reasonable size; viable unit such as district, block or taluk; comparative economic backwardness.
10. The criteria are practice-based, not constitutional text.
11. Ten States have notified Scheduled Areas: Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Odisha and Rajasthan.
12. Only notified territory is scheduled; a State is never wholly scheduled by the listing.
13. Paragraph 7: Parliament may amend the Fifth Schedule by ordinary law; it is not an Article 368 amendment.
14. The 1976 amending Act inserted the increase-and-redefine powers now in 6(2)(aa) and 6(2)(d).

### Concept check

**Question:** A State government issues a notification declaring three new blocks to be Scheduled Areas, citing their tribal majority and backwardness. Is the notification effective?

**Model answer:** No. Under Fifth Schedule paragraph 6(1), Scheduled Area status is created only by an order of the **President**; enlargement of an existing Scheduled Area under paragraph 6(2)(aa) is likewise a Presidential order, made after consultation with the Governor. Tribal preponderance and backwardness are administrative identification criteria that guide the choice; they do not confer status. A State notification can at most generate a proposal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the State administers a Scheduled Area, it also constitutes one. Administration and creation of status are separate functions held by different authorities.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** Examine the process by which an area is declared, altered or de-notified as a Scheduled Area, and assess whether that process adequately protects tribal interests.

#### Model solution

Fifth Schedule paragraph 6(1) makes Scheduled Area status a function of Presidential order. Paragraph 6(2) permits the President to direct that an area ceases to be scheduled, increase it after consulting the Governor, alter it only by rectification of boundaries, declare territory not previously in any State on reorganisation or admission, and rescind and, consulting the Governor, redefine afresh. The closing words bar variation of that order by any other route. Administrative criteria — tribal preponderance, compactness, a viable district, block or taluk unit and comparative backwardness — guide identification without conferring status.

The design resists casual change. Its weaknesses are real: consultation is textually required only for enlargement and redefinition, not for de-scheduling; consultation is not consent; no community hearing is prescribed; and paragraph 7 lets Parliament amend the Schedule by ordinary law. Protection therefore rests on political and judicial restraint rather than textual entrenchment.

**Rubric (10):** paragraph 6(1) declaration 2; accurate 6(2) sub-clause coverage 3; criteria correctly classified as administrative 2; evaluation of the consultation and de-scheduling asymmetry 2; paragraph 7 flexibility point 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

Territory now exists. The immediate question is who administers it, and what supervision the Constitution attaches to that administration. That is Fifth Schedule paragraphs 2 and 3.

---
## Lesson 4: The Fifth Schedule Executive Chain: State Power, Governor's Report and Union Directions

Progress: 4/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Fifth Schedule paragraphs 1 to 3 and the supervision loop they create

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity treatment of the Governor's "special responsibility" in Scheduled Areas, checked against paragraphs 2 and 3 because the textbook phrase is a gloss rather than the constitutional wording
CA search: "publication, tabling or review of Governor's annual report on administration of Scheduled Areas in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No official change to the reporting format, periodicity or publication practice was located, so the lesson teaches the textual duty and its known institutional gaps
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the supervision loop, and where it leaks

```text
STATE EXECUTIVE POWER CONTINUES  (para 2)
   subject to the provisions of the Fifth Schedule
                 |
   day-to-day administration of the Scheduled Area
   revenue | police | forests | welfare | development
                 |
   GOVERNOR'S REPORT  (para 3)
   annually, OR whenever so required by the President
                 |
                 v
             PRESIDENT
                 |
   UNION EXECUTIVE POWER extends to GIVING DIRECTIONS
   to the State as to the administration of the said areas  (para 3)
                 |
                 v
   scheme correction | administrative instruction | legislative follow-up

  WHERE THE LOOP LEAKS
  no prescribed report template
  no constitutional tabling deadline
  no stated consequence for a thin or late report
  no textual requirement of publication
```

Caption: the Constitution builds a complete circuit from local administration to Union direction, but it does not specify the quality, publicity or consequence of the report that carries the information.

### 1. Paragraph 2 settles the baseline

"Subject to the provisions of this Schedule, the executive power of a State extends to the Scheduled Areas therein."

Three words do the work. **Subject to** means the State's power is real but qualified by everything else in the Schedule — the Governor's paragraph 5 powers, the regulation route, the reporting duty. **Extends to** means there is no separate administering authority created by the Schedule. **Therein** means the power is ordinary State executive power operating in that territory, not a special grant.

This single sentence disposes of two frequently examined errors at once. Scheduling an area does **not** strip the State of executive power, and it does **not** vest administration in a local body, a council or the Union.

### 2. Paragraph 3 builds the supervision

Paragraph 3 has two limbs in one sentence, and both are tested.

First limb: the Governor of each State having Scheduled Areas "shall annually, or whenever so required by the President, make a report to the President regarding the administration of the Scheduled Areas in that State."

- The duty is **mandatory** — "shall."
- The periodicity is **annual**, with an additional on-demand trigger controlled by the President.
- The reporting officer is the **Governor**, and the recipient is the **President**. No Chief Minister, no Union ministry, no Parliament, is named in the text.
- The subject is the **administration** of Scheduled Areas, not tribal welfare in general.

Second limb: "the executive power of the Union shall extend to the giving of directions to the State as to the administration of the said areas."

- This is a **direction** power, not a takeover power.
- It operates on the **State**, telling it how to administer.
- It is executive, so it does not require a Proclamation, a parliamentary resolution or an emergency.

### 3. Why direction and takeover are not the same thing

The two paragraphs together settle five questions that are easy to answer loosely. State each one as a proposition about the text, and name the paragraph before the consequence.

| Question about the text | What paragraphs 2 and 3 provide |
|---|---|
| What happens to State executive power once an area is scheduled? | Paragraph 2 preserves it: the executive power of the State extends to the Scheduled Areas |
| Does the Fifth Schedule create a body to administer the area in the State's place? | It creates none. The Schedule establishes an advisory council under paragraph 4, not an administering authority |
| What does paragraph 3 give the Union? | Executive power to give **directions to the State** as to administration — a direction power operating on the State |
| Who reports, and to whom? | The **Governor** reports to the **President** |
| How often? | Annually, and whenever the President so requires |

The discipline the table builds is verbal precision. "Directs" is not "assumes"; "extends to" is not "replaces"; "advises" is not "administers". Carrying the Constitution's own verb into an answer is what separates a confident paragraph from a plausible-sounding one.

### 4. What the report could do, and what the text does not guarantee

The accountability value of paragraph 3 is that it converts local constitutional performance into an intergovernmental record.

```text
local evidence
  land transfer and restoration | money-lending | PESA conformity
  Forest Rights Act claims | services | displacement and R&R
          |
  Governor's Scheduled Area report
          |
  Presidential and Union scrutiny
          |
  direction under para 3 | scheme correction | Article 339(2) direction
          |
  administrative and political follow-up
```

But the Constitution prescribes none of the following: a standard reporting template; a date by which the report must be made; publication; tabling in the State legislature or Parliament; a reasoned response; or a consequence if the report is perfunctory. Those are institutional-design choices left to practice. This is the exact point at which a Mains answer should move from description to critique — and the critique must be stated as a gap in design and use, not as a constitutional defect in the power itself.

### 5. A note of discipline on the Governor's role

Textbooks call this the Governor's "special responsibility." The phrase is a useful gloss but not the constitutional wording, and it tempts two overstatements.

- *Overstatement one:* "The Governor acts in his personal discretion in all Fifth Schedule matters." The Schedule names the office that holds the power; it does not, in terms, convert every such function into unreviewable personal discretion. Asserting blanket discretion without a specific constitutional or judicial basis is unsafe.
- *Overstatement two:* "Ministerial advice is irrelevant here." Equally unsafe in the opposite direction.

The examinable criticism is sharper and safer than either: a protective constitutional power lodged in an office embedded in ordinary State executive arrangements can remain under-used when State revenue, forest and mining priorities point the other way. That is a claim about institutional incentives, and it is defensible.

### UPSC application

- Memorise paragraph 2 as a sentence; it answers the "executive power" family of questions by itself.
- Keep the report triad intact: **Governor reports, to the President, annually or whenever required**.
- Keep the direction triad intact: **Union executive power, directions, to the State**.
- In evaluative answers, distinguish "the power is weak" from "the power is unused" — only the second is accurate.
- Link paragraph 3 forward to Article 339(2), and distinguish them as in Lesson 2.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 2: subject to the Schedule, the State's executive power extends to Scheduled Areas therein.
2. Scheduling does not transfer administration to any local body.
3. Paragraph 3: the Governor shall report to the President annually, or whenever so required by the President.
4. The report concerns the administration of Scheduled Areas in that State.
5. The recipient is the President, not a Union ministry.
6. The reporting officer is the Governor, not the Chief Minister.
7. Union executive power extends to giving directions to the State on that administration.
8. Direction is not takeover; no Proclamation or emergency is involved.
9. The Constitution prescribes no template, deadline, publication, tabling or consequence for the report.
10. The report can convert local failures into an intergovernmental accountability record.
11. The "special responsibility" phrase is a textbook gloss, not constitutional wording.
12. Do not assert unqualified personal gubernatorial discretion without a specific basis.
13. The defensible criticism is institutional dormancy, not textual weakness.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Scheduled Area faces large-scale illegal land transfers. What does the Fifth Schedule allow the Union to do, and what does it not allow?

**Model answer:** The Governor must report on the administration of the Scheduled Area to the President annually or whenever the President requires it, and Union executive power extends under paragraph 3 to giving directions to the **State** on that administration. The Union may therefore direct the State to act, and may separately use Article 339(2) for essential ST-welfare schemes and Article 275(1) grants for capacity. It may not, under this Schedule, assume the administration of the area itself, displace State executive power preserved by paragraph 2, or vest administration in a local body.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading "control of the Union" in the marginal note to Article 339 as authority for Union administration. Control here operates through reports, directions and finance, not substitution.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** "The Fifth Schedule creates supervision without substitution." Examine with reference to paragraphs 2 and 3.

#### Model solution

Paragraph 2 preserves the baseline: subject to the Schedule, a State's executive power extends to the Scheduled Areas within it. No alternative administering authority is created. Paragraph 3 then layers supervision on that baseline. The Governor must report to the President annually, or whenever required, on the administration of those areas; and Union executive power extends to directions to the State on that administration.

The architecture is therefore corrective rather than substitutive. The Union informs itself through a constitutional reporting duty and acts through instruction to the responsible government, reinforced by Article 339(2) scheme directions and Article 275(1) grants-in-aid.

Its weakness lies in design detail, not in principle. No template, deadline, publication, tabling or consequence attaches to the report, so the quality of supervision depends on practice. Supervision without substitution is constitutionally sound; supervision without evidence is institutionally hollow, and reform should attack the second without disturbing the first.

**Rubric (10):** paragraph 2 baseline 2; paragraph 3 reporting duty stated exactly 2; direction power and its limits 2; link to Articles 339(2) and 275(1) 2; evaluative conclusion distinguishing design from use 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Supervision needs a voice from inside the State. The Fifth Schedule supplies one body for that purpose, and its powers are more precisely bounded than most summaries admit.

---

## Lesson 5: The Tribes Advisory Council: Paragraph 4 Composition, Duty and Ceiling

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Fifth Schedule paragraph 4 — establishment, membership, the shortfall rule, the advisory duty and rule-making

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity summary of the Tribes Advisory Council, corrected against paragraph 4 because the familiar "20 members, three-fourths ST MLAs" formula omits the "as nearly as may be" qualifier and the proviso
CA search: "Tribes Advisory Council meetings, reconstitution or reform in Indian States, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No nationally significant official development on Tribes Advisory Councils was located, so the lesson teaches the constitutional design and its structural limits
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the council and its ceiling

```text
WHERE IT MUST EXIST
  every State having Scheduled Areas
            +
  any State having Scheduled Tribes but NO Scheduled Areas,
  IF the President so directs
            |
            v
     TRIBES ADVISORY COUNCIL
     not more than TWENTY members
            |
   as nearly as may be, THREE-FOURTHS shall be
   representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the
   Legislative Assembly of the State
            |
   PROVISO: if ST representatives in the Assembly are fewer
   than the seats to be filled by them, the remaining seats
   are filled by OTHER MEMBERS OF THOSE TRIBES
            |
            v
   DUTY: advise on matters pertaining to the welfare and
   advancement of the Scheduled Tribes in the State
   AS MAY BE REFERRED TO THEM BY THE GOVERNOR
            |
   CEILING: advice.  Not a statute. Not a binding veto.
   Not an executive order. Not a judgment.
```

Caption: the composition links the council to elected ST representation; the duty clause ties its agenda to the Governor's reference; and the ceiling is advice.

### 1. Why this body exists at all

Paragraph 3 gives the Union information. Paragraph 5, which we study next, gives the Governor power. Neither gives tribal communities a seat. Paragraph 4 is the Fifth Schedule's answer to that gap: a standing council, overwhelmingly composed of elected Scheduled Tribe representatives, that advises on welfare and advancement.

Understand the design intention before judging it. The framers did not want a purely bureaucratic advisory committee; they wanted a body with a **legislative-representative connection**, which is why three-fourths of its seats are reserved for representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the State Legislative Assembly.

### 2. Paragraph 4(1) read exactly

Four precisions separate a correct answer from the usual summary.

| Familiar shorthand | The text |
|---|---|
| "Every Fifth Schedule State has a TAC" | A council shall be established in each State having Scheduled Areas **and**, if the President so directs, in any State having Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas |
| "It has 20 members" | **Not more than** twenty members — twenty is a ceiling, not a fixed strength |
| "Three-fourths are ST MLAs" | **As nearly as may be**, three-fourths shall be **representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the Legislative Assembly** |
| "If there aren't enough, seats stay vacant" | The proviso fills the remainder with **other members of those tribes** |

The second row matters for Prelims: "not more than twenty" is a different proposition from "twenty." The fourth row matters even more, because it shows the framers anticipated States where the Assembly would not supply enough ST representatives, and they solved it by widening the pool to the tribes themselves rather than to officials.

### 3. Paragraph 4(2): duty and agenda

"It shall be the duty of the Tribes Advisory Council to advise on such matters pertaining to the welfare and advancement of the Scheduled Tribes in the State as may be referred to them by the Governor."

Two limits are built into that sentence, and they are different from each other.

- **A subject limit.** The council advises on welfare and advancement of Scheduled Tribes. That is broad, but it is not a general power to review every administrative decision in a Scheduled Area.
- **An agenda limit.** It advises on matters "as may be referred to them by the Governor." The council does not set its own docket under the text.

The second limit is the sharper analytical point. A body that cannot choose what it examines cannot scrutinise what is never sent to it. This is not a criticism of the members; it is a feature of the clause. Reform proposals therefore target reference practice — a fixed meeting calendar, published agendas, reasoned disposal of advice — rather than the advisory character itself, which only a Schedule amendment could change.

### 4. Paragraph 4(3): the rule-making power

The Governor may make rules prescribing or regulating the number of members of the council, the mode of their appointment, the appointment of the Chairman and of its officers and servants, the conduct of its meetings and its procedure generally, and all other incidental matters.

That is why State practice varies: strength within the ceiling, chairmanship, secretariat support and meeting frequency are all rule-based rather than constitutionally fixed. It also means that much of what is criticised about councils in practice — irregular meetings, thin secretariats, weak follow-up — is remediable by rules, without touching the Constitution.

### 5. The comparison that must not collapse

| | Tribes Advisory Council | Autonomous District Council |
|---|---|---|
| Source | Fifth Schedule, paragraph 4 | Sixth Schedule, paragraphs 1 and 2 |
| Membership | Up to 20; mostly ST representatives in the Assembly | Up to 30; up to 4 nominated, the rest elected on adult suffrage — except the Bodoland Territorial Council, where the proviso to paragraph 2(1) sets a ceiling of not more than 46 |
| Core function | Advise on referred welfare and advancement matters | Make laws, administer services, run courts, levy taxes |
| Legal effect of output | Advice | Law, on the Governor's assent — or, for an Assam council law on a Concurrent List matter, on the President's |
| Agenda control | Governor's reference | Own legislative and executive initiative within the Schedule |

The row that matters most is the fourth. Writing that the Tribes Advisory Council "legislates," or that it is "the Fifth Schedule equivalent of a District Council," is a doctrinal error, not a slip of expression.

### 6. One more consultative function, easy to miss

Paragraph 4 is not the council's only appearance in the Schedule. Paragraph 5(5) makes consultation with the Tribes Advisory Council a **condition precedent** to the Governor making a regulation, in a State where such a council exists. So the council's advice, which is non-binding in content, becomes procedurally indispensable at one point in the Schedule. That asymmetry — non-binding advice, mandatory consultation — is worth stating precisely in any answer on the council's real weight.

### UPSC application

- Use the exact phrases "not more than twenty" and "as nearly as may be, three-fourths."
- Mention the proviso; very few answers do, and it demonstrates textual control.
- Always pair the advisory ceiling with the agenda limit; the two together make the critique precise.
- Pair paragraph 4 with paragraph 5(5) to show where advice becomes procedurally mandatory.
- Never equate the council with a District Council, and never describe its advice as binding.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 4(1): a council in each State having Scheduled Areas.
2. Also in a State having Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas, if the President so directs.
3. Strength: not more than twenty members.
4. Composition: as nearly as may be, three-fourths representatives of STs in the State Legislative Assembly.
5. Proviso: shortfall seats go to other members of those tribes.
6. Paragraph 4(2): duty to advise on welfare and advancement matters referred by the Governor.
7. Subject limit — welfare and advancement; agenda limit — Governor's reference.
8. Paragraph 4(3): the Governor may make rules on strength, appointment, Chairman, staff, meetings and procedure.
9. Much State-level variation arises from these rules, not from the Constitution.
10. The council advises; it does not legislate, veto or adjudicate.
11. Paragraph 5(5) makes consultation with the council mandatory before a Governor's regulation, where one exists.
12. Non-binding advice plus mandatory consultation is the council's exact legal weight.
13. It is not the Fifth Schedule counterpart of an Autonomous District Council.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State has Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas. Can it have a Tribes Advisory Council, and if so, how?

**Model answer:** Yes. Paragraph 4(1) requires a council in every State having Scheduled Areas, and permits one **in a State having Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas if the President so directs**. The composition rules are the same: not more than twenty members, with as nearly as may be three-fourths drawn from representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the State Legislative Assembly, and the proviso filling any shortfall with other members of those tribes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the council is tied to scheduled territory. Article 244(1) applies the Fifth Schedule to Scheduled Areas **and** Scheduled Tribes, and paragraph 4 uses that second object to reach States without any Scheduled Area.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "The Tribes Advisory Council is constitutionally guaranteed but institutionally marginal." Critically examine, and suggest reforms consistent with its constitutional character.

#### Model solution

Paragraph 4 of the Fifth Schedule guarantees the council's existence in every State with Scheduled Areas, and permits one by Presidential direction in a State with Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Areas. Its composition is deliberately representative: not more than twenty members, with as nearly as may be three-fourths drawn from representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the State Legislative Assembly, and a proviso filling any shortfall with other members of those tribes. Paragraph 5(5) further makes consultation with the council a condition of the Governor's regulation-making power.

Marginality arises from three features. The duty under paragraph 4(2) is to advise, so its output binds no one. The subject is confined to welfare and advancement. And the agenda is set by reference from the Governor, so the council cannot examine what is never sent to it. Paragraph 4(3) leaves strength, chairmanship, staffing, meetings and procedure to gubernatorial rules, which produces wide variation in practice.

Reform should therefore work on reference and process rather than legal effect, which only a paragraph 7 amendment could change. A fixed annual calendar; published agendas; mandatory reference of land-alienation, PESA conformity, forest-rights and displacement matters; evidence from affected Gram Sabhas; a reasoned action-taken statement on every recommendation; and an independent secretariat would convert advice into a record the Governor's paragraph 3 report could carry upward.

The council is thus not weak by accident. It was designed as a voice, and it becomes meaningful only when the system is obliged to answer that voice.

**Rubric (15):** exact paragraph 4(1) composition including the proviso 3; paragraph 4(2) subject and agenda limits 3; paragraph 4(3) and resulting State variation 2; paragraph 5(5) mandatory-consultation nuance 2; reform proposals tied to reference and process 3; conclusion respecting the advisory character 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

The council can only advise. The Fifth Schedule's actual protective firepower lies one paragraph further on, where the Governor may switch off or rewrite legislation and make land and credit regulations that outrank Acts of Parliament.

---

## Lesson 6: Paragraph 5: Filtering Law, Protective Regulations and Presidential Assent

Progress: 6/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Fifth Schedule paragraph 5 — non-application and modification of Acts, peace-and-good-government regulations, repeal power, assent and consultation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity account of the Governor's law-modification and regulation powers, expanded with the clause-by-clause text because the summary merges paragraph 5(1) and 5(2) into one sentence
CA search: "new Fifth Schedule regulation made by a Governor and assented to by the President in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No fresh paragraph 5 regulation with Presidential assent was located for this period; the lesson therefore teaches the power and its known pattern of use rather than a current instrument
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two different powers in one paragraph

```text
PARAGRAPH 5(1): THE FILTER                  PARAGRAPH 5(2)-(5): THE REGULATION
"Notwithstanding anything in this            "regulations for the peace and good
Constitution, the Governor may by            government of any area which is for
public notification direct that any          the time being a Scheduled Area"
particular Act of Parliament or of                      |
the State Legislature..."                     illustrative heads:
          |                                    (a) prohibit/restrict TRANSFER OF LAND
   +------+-------+                                by or among members of STs
   |              |                            (b) regulate ALLOTMENT OF LAND to
shall NOT      shall apply                         members of STs
 apply         subject to EXCEPTIONS           (c) regulate MONEY-LENDING business
               and MODIFICATIONS                   by persons lending to STs
          |                                              |
  may be given RETROSPECTIVE effect           5(3) may REPEAL or AMEND any Act of
                                                   Parliament / State Legislature /
  operates on a PARTICULAR Act                     existing law applicable to the area
  already in force                                        |
                                              5(5) consult the Tribes Advisory Council
                                                   where one exists
                                                        |
                                              5(4) submitted FORTHWITH to the President
                                                   NO EFFECT UNTIL HE ASSENTS
```

Caption: paragraph 5(1) adapts existing law to local conditions; paragraph 5(2) makes new law for the Scheduled Area. Only the second needs the President.

### 1. Paragraph 5(1): a constitutional filter on general legislation

The opening words are "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution." That is a strong non obstante clause, and it tells you the framers expected this power to override the ordinary scheme of legislative application.

What the Governor may do, by **public notification**, is direct that any particular Act of Parliament or of the State Legislature either:

- shall **not apply** to a Scheduled Area or any part of it in the State; or
- shall apply to it **subject to such exceptions and modifications** as he specifies.

And the clause adds: any such direction "may be given so as to have retrospective effect."

Four features deserve emphasis.

1. It operates on a **particular Act** — it is selective, not a wholesale suspension of the statute book.
2. It can reach **Acts of Parliament**, not merely State law. A State functionary disapplying a central Act is constitutionally remarkable, and it exists because local land, custom and institutional realities may make a general statute destructive in a Scheduled Area.
3. **Modification** is a middle path between full application and exclusion, which is usually the more sensible instrument.
4. **Retrospectivity** is expressly permitted, which lets the power cure past mismatch rather than only prevent future harm.

What it is not: it is not a power to suspend the Constitution, to displace Part III, or to create a law-free zone. It filters legislation; it does not disable constitutional rights.

### 2. Paragraph 5(2): the regulation power

Here the Governor may make regulations "for the peace and good government of any area in a State which is for the time being a Scheduled Area."

That is a wide general power, and the Schedule then adds, "in particular and without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing power," three illustrative heads:

| Head | Text | Risk it addresses |
|---|---|---|
| (a) | Prohibit or restrict the transfer of land **by or among members of the Scheduled Tribes** in such area | Distress sale, fraudulent transfer, benami acquisition |
| (b) | Regulate the **allotment of land** to members of the Scheduled Tribes in such area | Discretionary or inequitable allotment |
| (c) | Regulate the carrying on of business as **money-lender** by persons who lend money to members of the Scheduled Tribes | Debt bondage and interest-driven land loss |

Two textual points pay dividends. The heads are **illustrative**, introduced by "in particular and without prejudice to the generality" — so the peace-and-good-government power is not confined to land and credit. And head (a) covers transfer "by or among" members of Scheduled Tribes, which means intra-tribal transfers are within reach, not only tribal-to-non-tribal sales.

### 3. Paragraph 5(3): regulations that outrank statutes

"In making any such regulation... the Governor may repeal or amend any Act of Parliament or of the Legislature of the State or any existing law which is for the time being applicable to the area in question."

Pause on that. A regulation made by the Governor may **repeal or amend an Act of Parliament** in its application to the Scheduled Area. There are very few comparable provisions in the Constitution. It is the clearest textual evidence that the framers treated tribal land and credit protection as a subject requiring an instrument stronger than ordinary executive rule-making.

### 4. Paragraphs 5(4) and 5(5): the two conditions

The power is strong, so the Constitution conditions it twice, and the conditions operate at different stages.

```text
DRAFT REGULATION
      |
  [5(5)]  where there is a Tribes Advisory Council for the State,
          the Council MUST be consulted
          -> a precondition to MAKING the regulation
      |
  GOVERNOR MAKES THE REGULATION
      |
  [5(4)]  submitted FORTHWITH to the President
      |
  PRESIDENTIAL ASSENT
      |
  -> until assented to, the regulation HAS NO EFFECT
```

Two traps live here. The first is the direction of assent: Fifth Schedule regulations go to the **President**, whereas Sixth Schedule council laws go to the **Governor**. The second is the conditionality of consultation — paragraph 5(5) requires consultation only "in the case where there is a Tribes Advisory Council for the State," which is a conditional, not an absolute, requirement.

### 5. Comparing the two halves of paragraph 5

| | Paragraph 5(1) | Paragraph 5(2) with 5(3) |
|---|---|---|
| Instrument | Public notification | Regulation |
| Operates on | A particular existing Act | The area, by making fresh law |
| Effect | Non-application or modified application | Peace and good government, including land and money-lending |
| Can repeal or amend an Act | Only in effect, by disapplication or modification of that Act | Expressly, under 5(3) |
| Prior consultation required | Not stated in the paragraph | Tribes Advisory Council, where one exists |
| Assent required | Not stated in the paragraph | President; no effect until assent |
| Retrospective | Expressly permitted | Not expressly stated |

Students routinely attach the assent requirement to paragraph 5(1) as well. The text does not. Keeping the two columns distinct is one of the highest-yield precisions in this topic.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** This concentrates extraordinary legislative power in an unelected office, including power over Acts of Parliament.

**Reply.** The power is subject-bounded by the Schedule's protective purpose, procedurally conditioned by consultation and Presidential assent, territorially confined to Scheduled Areas, and — like any constitutional power — bounded by purpose and law rather than being at large.

**Residual.** The real-world complaint is the opposite of the objection.
