The criticism that survives scrutiny is not that the power is dangerous but that it is sparingly used, that the regulations in force are State-specific and uneven, and that a prohibition without updated land records, accessible forums and restoration machinery can freeze an illegality instead of reversing it. That thread continues in Lesson 17.

### UPSC application

- The single most examined proposition: Fifth Schedule regulations need the **President's** assent.
- Quote "peace and good government" verbatim; it is the operative phrase.
- Mention retrospectivity under 5(1) — few candidates do.
- Mention 5(3) repeal-or-amend power when asked how strong the Fifth Schedule really is.
- Keep 5(1) and 5(2) in separate sentences; merging them generates the assent error.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 5(1) opens with "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution."
2. By public notification the Governor may direct that a particular Act of Parliament or the State Legislature shall not apply, or shall apply with exceptions and modifications, to a Scheduled Area.
3. Such a direction may be given retrospective effect.
4. Paragraph 5(2): regulations for the peace and good government of a Scheduled Area.
5. Illustrative heads: transfer of land by or among STs; allotment of land to STs; money-lending to STs.
6. The heads are illustrative, not exhaustive.
7. Head (a) covers transfer "by or among" members of Scheduled Tribes.
8. Paragraph 5(3): a regulation may repeal or amend any Act of Parliament, State Act or existing law applicable to the area.
9. Paragraph 5(5): consult the Tribes Advisory Council where one exists, before making the regulation.
10. Paragraph 5(4): submit forthwith to the President; no effect until assent.
11. Assent under the Fifth Schedule is Presidential; Sixth Schedule council laws take the Governor's assent, except in Assam, where a council law relating to a Concurrent List matter is reserved for the President under paragraph 3A(2) or 3B(2).
12. Paragraph 5(1) carries no textual assent or consultation requirement.
13. The power filters and protects; it does not suspend the Constitution or Part III.
14. The durable criticism is under-use and uneven State-level regulations, not excess.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Governor issues a notification modifying the application of a central tenancy Act to a Scheduled Area, and in the same week makes a regulation restricting land transfers among Scheduled Tribes. Which instrument needs Presidential assent, and why?

**Model answer:** Only the regulation. Paragraph 5(4) requires every regulation made under paragraph 5 to be submitted forthwith to the President, and it has no effect until assented to; paragraph 5(5) additionally requires prior consultation with the Tribes Advisory Council where one exists. The notification modifying the application of a particular Act is made under paragraph 5(1), which contains no assent or consultation requirement, though it may be given retrospective effect. The difference reflects function: 5(1) adapts existing law, while 5(2) read with 5(3) makes new law that can repeal or amend statutes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Applying the assent condition to every Fifth Schedule action by the Governor. The condition attaches to regulations, not to law-application directions.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Analyse the Governor's powers under paragraph 5 of the Fifth Schedule, and assess why a constitutionally strong instrument has produced uneven protection.

#### Model solution

Paragraph 5 houses two different powers. Under 5(1), notwithstanding anything in the Constitution, the Governor may by public notification direct that a particular Act of Parliament or of the State Legislature shall not apply to a Scheduled Area, or shall apply subject to exceptions and modifications, with retrospective effect if specified. This is a filter that adapts general law to local land, custom and institutional realities.

Under 5(2) the Governor may make regulations for the peace and good government of a Scheduled Area, including, illustratively, prohibiting or restricting transfer of land by or among Scheduled Tribes, regulating allotment of land to them, and regulating money-lending to them. Paragraph 5(3) allows such a regulation to repeal or amend any Act of Parliament, State Act or existing law applicable to the area — an unusually strong instrument. Paragraph 5(5) requires consultation with the Tribes Advisory Council where one exists, and 5(4) makes the regulation ineffective until the President assents.

Unevenness arises downstream of the text. The instruments are State-specific, so remedies, exemptions and definitions differ across the ten States. Activation depends on gubernatorial initiative inside an executive system weighing revenue, forest and mining priorities. Above all, prohibition without updated land records, accessible forums, legal aid and restoration machinery converts an illegal transfer into an unremedied fact.

The reform agenda is therefore evidentiary: map and publish regulations in force, maintain geo-referenced records, resource restoration tribunals and track outcomes. The constitutional firepower already exists; what is missing is the machinery that fires it.

**Rubric (15):** paragraph 5(1) content including retrospectivity 3; paragraph 5(2) heads quoted accurately 3; paragraph 5(3) repeal-or-amend power 2; 5(4) assent and 5(5) consultation 3; causes of uneven protection 2; reform-oriented conclusion 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Protective powers need supervision and money. The Constitution supplies both outside the Schedule, in Articles 339 and 275, and together with paragraphs 2 to 5 they complete the Fifth Schedule's institutional picture.

---

## Lesson 7: Union Supervision and Money: Article 339, Article 275 and the Fifth Schedule Dashboard

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Article 339 commissions and directions, Article 275 grants, and a consolidated view of who does what in a Scheduled Area

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity treatment of the Dhebar and Bhuria commissions and of Article 275 grants, supplemented with the full Article 275 proviso structure and Article 275(1-A), which textbook tables omit
CA search: "new commission appointed under Article 339(1) or new Article 339(2) direction on Scheduled Tribe welfare schemes in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No fresh Article 339(1) commission or publicly reported Article 339(2) direction was located, so the lesson teaches the provisions and the two standard commissions
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: supervision and finance around the Schedule

```text
                      CONSTITUTIONAL SUPERVISION
   Article 339(1)                         Article 339(2)
   President MAY at any time, and         Union executive power extends to
   SHALL at the expiration of ten         DIRECTIONS to a State as to the
   years from commencement, appoint       drawing up and execution of SCHEMES
   a Commission to report on              specified in the direction to be
   administration of Scheduled Areas      ESSENTIAL for the welfare of
   and welfare of STs in the States       Scheduled Tribes in the State
            |                                        |
   order may define composition,          scheme-specific, not territorially
   powers and procedure                   confined to Scheduled Areas
            |
   Dhebar Commission 1960, reported 1961
   Bhuria Commission 2002, reported 2004

                      CONSTITUTIONAL FINANCE
   Article 275(1) first proviso  ->  capital and recurring grants for
      (i) development schemes approved by the Government of India for
          promoting ST welfare in the State, OR
      (ii) raising the level of administration of the Scheduled Areas
           to that of the rest of the State
   Article 275(1) second proviso ->  Assam-specific sums for Part I tribal areas
   Article 275(1-A)              ->  apportionment once an Article 244A
                                     autonomous State is formed
   Article 275(2)                ->  President may order until Parliament provides;
                                     after a Finance Commission exists, only after
                                     considering its recommendations
```

Caption: Article 339 supplies eyes and instructions; Article 275 supplies money. Neither is inside the Fifth Schedule, and both are indispensable to it.

### 1. Article 339(1): the commission power

The clause does two things in one sentence. It gives the President a **standing discretionary power** to appoint a Commission at any time, and it imposed a **one-time mandatory duty** to do so at the expiration of ten years from the commencement of the Constitution. The Commission reports on the administration of the Scheduled Areas **and** the welfare of the Scheduled Tribes in the States — again the Fifth Schedule's twin objects. The order appointing it may define its composition, powers and procedure, and may contain incidental or ancillary provisions.

Two commissions are the standard named evidence:

| Commission | Appointed | Chaired by | Reported |
|---|---|---|---|
| First Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes Commission | 1960 | U.N. Dhebar | 1961 |
| Second Commission | 2002 | Dilip Singh Bhuria | 2004 |

A naming caution worth internalising: the name "Bhuria" attaches to **two different bodies** in this topic. The 2002 Commission under Article 339(1) is one. The earlier committee that shaped the extension of Panchayat provisions to Scheduled Areas, and whose approach informed PESA in 1996, is another. Use the date and the function, not just the surname.

### 2. Article 339(2): scheme directions

"The executive power of the Union shall extend to the giving of directions to a State as to the drawing up and execution of schemes specified in the direction to be essential for the welfare of the Scheduled Tribes in the State."

Three precisions:

- It covers **drawing up** as well as **execution** — the Union may direct the design of the scheme, not only its implementation.
- The schemes must be **specified in the direction** and characterised as **essential** for ST welfare.
- It operates in relation to a **State**, and is not confined to Scheduled Areas. A State with Scheduled Tribes but no Scheduled Area is still within its reach.

Hold this against Fifth Schedule paragraph 3, which directs the State on the **administration of the Scheduled Areas**. The two directions are textually distinct and practically complementary: one is about territory, the other about schemes.

### 3. Article 275(1): the grant architecture

The main clause of Article 275(1) is the general grants-in-aid provision. The tribal content lies in its provisos.

**First proviso.** There shall be paid out of the Consolidated Fund of India, as grants-in-aid of the revenues of a State, such capital and recurring sums as may be necessary to enable that State to meet the costs of (i) such schemes of development as may be undertaken by the State with the approval of the Government of India for the purpose of promoting the welfare of the Scheduled Tribes in that State, or (ii) raising the level of administration of the Scheduled Areas in that State to that of the administration of the rest of the areas of that State.

Notice the second limb. It is an **administrative-capacity equalisation** clause, not merely a welfare-scheme clause. The Constitution anticipated that the gap in Scheduled Areas would be as much about the presence and quality of administration as about benefit transfers.

**Second proviso.** Assam receives capital and recurring sums equivalent to (a) the average excess of expenditure over revenues in the two years immediately preceding the commencement of the Constitution for the administration of the Part I tribal areas, and (b) the costs of development schemes undertaken with the approval of the Government of India to raise the level of administration of those areas to that of the rest of Assam.

**Article 275(1-A).** On the formation of an autonomous State under Article 244A, sums payable under the second proviso are paid to the autonomous State where it comprises all those tribal areas, or apportioned between Assam and the autonomous State as the President may by order specify; and the autonomous State receives grants for development schemes approved by the Government of India to raise its administration to the level of the rest of Assam.

**Article 275(2).** Until Parliament legislates under clause (1), the President may exercise the power by order; but after a Finance Commission has been constituted, no such order may be made except after considering its recommendations.

### 4. Why the fiscal clause carries analytical weight

```text
constitutional power   x   timely exercise
                       x   community participation
                       x   enforceable records
                       x   administrative and fiscal capacity
                       =   effective protection
```

The arithmetic is the point. If any multiplier approaches zero, the product does. A Scheduled Area with strong paragraph 5 regulations but no staff to enforce them, or with grants but no records, produces weak protection. Article 275(1)'s second limb exists precisely because formal autonomy without administrative capacity reproduces dependence.

The qualification must be stated in the same breath: a grant is evidence of fiscal support, not proof of devolution, rights recognition or outcomes. Quoting grant figures without outcome evidence is weak answer-writing.

### 5. The consolidated Fifth Schedule dashboard

| Actor | Core constitutional role | Characteristic failure mode |
|---|---|---|
| President and Union | Declare and alter areas under paragraph 6; assent to regulations under 5(4); receive reports under paragraph 3; direct the State under paragraph 3 and Article 339(2); appoint commissions under Article 339(1) | Supervision at a distance, dependent on the quality of reports |
| Governor | Report under paragraph 3; refer matters to the council under 4(2); filter law under 5(1); make regulations under 5(2); frame council rules under 4(3) | Opacity or dormancy; initiative is not legally triggered |
| State government | Day-to-day administration under paragraph 2; implementation and enforcement | Conflict with revenue, forest and mining priorities |
| Tribes Advisory Council | Advise on referred welfare and advancement matters; be consulted under 5(5) | Agenda controlled from above; advice not binding |
| Gram Sabha under PESA and the Forest Rights Act | Statutory participatory, resource and rights functions | Capacity, record-keeping and enforcement gaps |

This table is the Fifth Schedule in one view, and it is the structure to carry into any 15- or 20-mark answer on that Schedule.

### UPSC application

- For "how is tribal administration financed," lead with Article 275(1)'s first proviso and name both limbs.
- For "how is it supervised," lead with Article 339(1) and (2) plus paragraph 3, and distinguish them.
- Always attach Dhebar to 1960 and Bhuria to 2002, with reports in 1961 and 2004.
- Flag the two-Bhuria naming hazard in your own notes so you never attribute PESA to the 2002 Commission.
- Use the multiplier formulation to convert description into evaluation.

### Revision notes

1. Article 339(1): the President may at any time, and was required after ten years from commencement, to appoint a Commission by order.
2. Its remit: administration of Scheduled Areas and welfare of Scheduled Tribes in the States.
3. The order may define composition, powers and procedure.
4. Dhebar Commission: appointed 1960, reported 1961.
5. Bhuria Commission: appointed 2002, reported 2004.
6. The PESA-related Bhuria committee is a different, earlier body; distinguish by date and function.
7. Article 339(2): Union directions on drawing up and executing specified ST-welfare schemes.
8. Article 339(2) is scheme-based and not confined to Scheduled Areas.
9. Article 275(1) first proviso: grants for GoI-approved ST welfare schemes or for raising Scheduled Area administration to the State's general level.
10. Article 275(1) second proviso: Assam-specific sums tied to Part I tribal areas.
11. Article 275(1-A): apportionment on formation of an Article 244A autonomous State.
12. Article 275(2): President may order until Parliament provides; after a Finance Commission exists, only after considering its recommendations.
13. A grant is support for capacity, not proof of devolution or outcomes.
14. The dashboard: President and Union, Governor, State, council, Gram Sabha — each with a named role and a named failure mode.

### Concept check

**Question:** Distinguish the three Union powers to issue directions that appear in this topic.

**Model answer:** First, Fifth Schedule paragraph 3: Union executive power extends to directions to the **State** as to the **administration of Scheduled Areas**. Second, Article 339(2): Union executive power extends to directions to a State as to the **drawing up and execution of schemes** specified as **essential for ST welfare**, which is scheme-based and not territorially confined. Third, within the Sixth Schedule, the controls are different in kind — they run through the **Governor's** assent, inquiry, suspension and dissolution powers rather than Union direction. The first two are territorial and scheme-based respectively; the third is supervisory over an autonomous institution.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Article 339's marginal note, "Control of the Union," as authority for Union administration of Scheduled Areas. Control here operates through commissions, directions and finance.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** "Constitutional supervision of Scheduled Areas is informational and fiscal rather than administrative." Comment.

#### Model solution

The claim is largely accurate. Fifth Schedule paragraph 2 preserves State executive power, and paragraph 3 supplies the Union with information through the Governor's annual or on-demand report, plus a power to direct the State on administration. Article 339(1) adds a commission power — exercised through the Dhebar Commission of 1960 and the Bhuria Commission of 2002 — while Article 339(2) permits directions on drawing up and executing schemes specified as essential for ST welfare. Article 275(1)'s first proviso supplies capital and recurring grants both for approved ST welfare schemes and for raising Scheduled Area administration to the level of the rest of the State.

No clause authorises Union administration of a Scheduled Area. The design assumes that a well-informed and well-funded State will administer better than a distant substitute.

The qualification is that information without a prescribed reporting standard, and finance without outcome measurement, convert supervision into formality.

**Rubric (10):** paragraph 2 and 3 informational chain 2; Article 339(1) with commissions named 2; Article 339(2) scheme directions 2; Article 275(1) fiscal route including both limbs 2; evaluative qualification 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

The Fifth Schedule is now complete: territory, administration, voice, protective law and Union support. The Sixth Schedule answers the same problem with an entirely different institution — an elected government for the district — and it begins, as the Fifth did, with territory.

---
## Lesson 8: Sixth Schedule Territory: Four States, Autonomous Districts and Autonomous Regions

Progress: 8/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Sixth Schedule paragraphs 1 and 20 — the table of tribal areas, the Governor's organisation power and the district-region relationship

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity table of tribal areas at a glance for the four States, read against paragraph 1 and paragraph 20 because the textbook table omits the Governor's eight-clause reorganisation power and the Shillong carve-out
CA search: "creation of a new autonomous district or autonomous region under the Sixth Schedule in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura or Mizoram, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No new Sixth Schedule autonomous district or region was located; the ten tribal areas in the four States stood unchanged on 1 October 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the territorial nesting, and who controls each level

```text
                      THE FOUR-STATE FIELD  (Article 244(2))
   ASSAM            MEGHALAYA          TRIPURA           MIZORAM
   Part I           Part II            Part IIA          Part III
   of the table appended to PARAGRAPH 20
        |                |                 |                 |
        v                v                 v                 v
                   AUTONOMOUS DISTRICT  (para 1(1))
        still inside the State's executive authority
                           |
         if DIFFERENT Scheduled Tribes inhabit the district
                           |
                           v
                   AUTONOMOUS REGION  (para 1(2))
         carved out by the GOVERNOR by public notification
                           |
                           v
          DISTRICT COUNCIL  /  REGIONAL COUNCIL  (para 2)

   GOVERNOR'S PARAGRAPH 1(3) TOOLKIT -- EIGHT CLAUSES,
   each exercised by public notification
   (a) include an area        (b) exclude an area
   (c) create a NEW district  (d) increase a district's area
   (e) diminish it            (f) unite districts or parts
   (ff) alter the name        (g) define boundaries
   Proviso: (c), (d), (e) and (f) only AFTER CONSIDERING the report
            of a Commission appointed under paragraph 14(1)
   Second proviso: the order may carry incidental and consequential
            provisions, INCLUDING AMENDMENT OF PARAGRAPH 20 and its table
```

Caption: the Constitution lists the tribal areas; the Governor shapes the districts and regions within them — and may, by the second proviso, amend the very table that lists them.

### 1. Start with the question the Schedule is answering

In the Fifth Schedule the unit of protection is an **area** administered by the State. In the Sixth Schedule the unit is a **district that governs itself**. That change of unit explains everything that follows: you cannot give a territory a legislature, a court and a tax base unless the territory is defined, bounded and organised.

So paragraph 1 does the organising, and paragraph 20 supplies the list.

### 2. Paragraph 20 and the table

Paragraph 20(1) states that the areas specified in Parts I, II, IIA and III of the table are respectively the tribal areas within Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. The table currently names ten tribal areas:

| State | Part | Tribal areas listed |
|---|---|---|
| Assam | I | North Cachar Hills District; Karbi Anglong District; Bodoland Territorial Areas District |
| Meghalaya | II | Khasi Hills District; Jaintia Hills District; Garo Hills District |
| Tripura | IIA | Tripura Tribal Areas District |
| Mizoram | III | Chakma District; Mara District; Lai District |

Two drafting details reward attention.

- **Paragraph 20(3)** defines the Tripura Tribal Areas District by reference to the territory comprising the tribal areas specified in the First Schedule to the **Tripura Tribal Areas Autonomous District Council Act, 1979**. A constitutional category is thus delineated by a State statute — a neat illustration of how the Schedule borrows from ordinary law.
- **The proviso to paragraph 20(2)** excludes the area within the **municipality of Shillong** from being deemed within the Khasi Hills District for a specified list of purposes — certain paragraph 3(1) subjects, paragraphs 4, 5 and 6, parts of paragraph 8, and one clause of paragraph 10. This is the clearest textual proof that Sixth Schedule competence can be carved out within a listed tribal area rather than operating uniformly across it.

### 3. Paragraph 1(1): the district is created, but not detached

"Subject to the provisions of this paragraph, the tribal areas in each item of Parts I, II and IIA and in Part III of the table appended to paragraph 20 of this Schedule shall be an autonomous district."

The constitutional listing does the creation. But — and standard Polity texts are emphatic about this — autonomous districts "do not fall outside the executive authority of the State concerned." An autonomous district is a self-governing unit **within** a State, not an enclave outside it. Every later power in the Schedule must be read against that sentence.

### 4. Paragraph 1(2): when a district becomes plural

"If there are different Scheduled Tribes in an autonomous district, the Governor may, by public notification, divide the area or areas inhabited by them into autonomous regions."

This clause carries a political insight. A single district council elected across a plural district can reproduce, at the local level, exactly the majority-minority problem that the Sixth Schedule was meant to solve. The autonomous region is the Schedule's internal answer: a smaller unit with its own Regional Council for the area inhabited by a distinct Scheduled Tribe.

The consequence appears in paragraph 2(5): in an autonomous district that has Regional Councils, the District Council has only such powers over the Regional Council's areas as the Regional Council **delegates** to it, in addition to those the Schedule itself confers. The Regional Council is therefore not a sub-committee of the District Council.

✅ **One district is outside this clause entirely.** The 2003 amendment inserted a proviso after paragraph 1(2) for Assam: "Provided that nothing in this sub-paragraph shall apply to the **Bodoland Territorial Areas District**." No autonomous region can be created there, and therefore no Regional Council. That is a deliberate design choice, not an oversight: the Bodoland settlement built a single large council with a guaranteed internal composition under the proviso to paragraph 2(1) — thirty seats reserved for Scheduled Tribes, five for non-tribal communities, five open and six nominated — and the Schedule protects internal plurality there through that seat structure instead of through territorial sub-division.

### 5. Paragraph 1(3): the Governor's reorganisation toolkit

Paragraph 1(3) contains **eight** clauses, lettered (a) to (f), then (ff) and (g) — include, exclude, create, increase, diminish, unite, alter the name, define boundaries — each exercised by **public notification**. Count them from the lettering, not from the list: the interpolated clause (ff) is exactly where a careless count loses one. The two provisos are what make this examinable.

- The **first proviso** restrains the four most consequential limbs: no order creating a new autonomous district, increasing or diminishing a district's area, or uniting districts, may be made "except after consideration of the report of a Commission appointed under sub-paragraph (1) of paragraph 14." Reorganisation is thus evidence-led by constitutional design.
- The **second proviso** permits incidental and consequential provisions, "including any amendment of paragraph 20 and of any item in any of the Parts of the said table." So a gubernatorial order may, in consequence, alter the constitutional table itself.

Set that beside the Fifth Schedule and the contrast is instructive: there, territory is made and unmade by the **President**; here, the Constitution lists the territory and the **Governor** shapes it, under a commission-report discipline that the Fifth Schedule does not impose.

### 6. The boundary that must not be crossed

| Tempting claim | Correct position |
|---|---|
| The Sixth Schedule covers the whole north-east | It covers tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram only |
| Nagaland, Manipur and Arunachal Pradesh are Sixth Schedule States | They operate under other constitutional and statutory arrangements, including Article 371A for Nagaland and Article 371G for Mizoram's State-level protections |
| An autonomous district is outside State authority | It remains within the executive authority of the State |
| Governor's reorganisation equals creation of a State | Reorganisation under paragraph 1(3) is territorial administration, not Article 2 or 3 State-making |
| The President organises autonomous districts | The Governor does, by public notification |

Note the Mizoram row carefully. Mizoram has **both** three Sixth Schedule autonomous district councils **and** an Article 371G protection operating at State level. The two coexist; neither implies the other.

### UPSC application

- "A-M-T-M" — Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram — is the safest recall device in this topic.
- Learn the ten tribal areas by State; they are directly quotable.
- Mention the paragraph 14(1) Commission-report proviso when asked how autonomous districts are reorganised.
- Use the Shillong carve-out to show that competence inside a tribal area can be partial.
- Use the Mizoram example whenever a question tempts you to equate Article 371 protection with Sixth Schedule status.

### Revision notes

1. Article 244(2) applies the Sixth Schedule to tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.
2. Paragraph 20(1): Parts I, II, IIA and III of the table correspond to those four States.
3. Ten tribal areas are listed: three in Assam, three in Meghalaya, one in Tripura, three in Mizoram.
4. Assam: North Cachar Hills, Karbi Anglong, Bodoland Territorial Areas District.
5. Meghalaya: Khasi Hills, Jaintia Hills, Garo Hills.
6. Tripura: Tripura Tribal Areas District, defined by reference to the 1979 State Act's First Schedule.
7. Mizoram: Chakma, Mara, Lai.
8. The proviso to paragraph 20(2) excludes the Shillong municipality area from the Khasi Hills District for specified purposes.
9. Paragraph 1(1): listed tribal areas are autonomous districts, still within State executive authority.
10. Paragraph 1(2): the Governor may divide a district inhabited by different Scheduled Tribes into autonomous regions — except in the **Bodoland Territorial Areas District**, which a proviso inserted in 2003 takes outside the sub-paragraph altogether.
11. Paragraph 2(5): where Regional Councils exist, the District Council has only delegated powers over their areas.
12. Paragraph 1(3): eight reorganisation clauses — (a) to (f), (ff) and (g) — exercised by public notification.
13. First proviso: creation, increase, diminution and union require consideration of a paragraph 14(1) Commission report.
14. Second proviso: such an order may amend paragraph 20 and its table consequentially.
15. Nagaland, Manipur and Arunachal Pradesh are not Sixth Schedule States.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two Scheduled Tribes inhabit different portions of one autonomous district, and the smaller community fears domination in council elections. What does the Sixth Schedule provide, and what follows for the District Council's powers?

**Model answer:** Paragraph 1(2) allows the Governor, by public notification, to divide the areas inhabited by different Scheduled Tribes within an autonomous district into **autonomous regions**, each with its own Regional Council under paragraph 2(2). Paragraph 2(4) vests the administration of an autonomous region in its Regional Council, and paragraph 2(5) provides that in such a district the District Council has only those powers over the Regional Council's areas that the Regional Council delegates to it, in addition to powers the Schedule itself confers. The region is therefore a genuine internal safeguard, not a subordinate committee.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a Regional Council as a lower tier beneath the District Council. Within its area it is the primary administering authority under the Schedule. A second error is generalising the clause: the proviso inserted in 2003 excludes the Bodoland Territorial Areas District from paragraph 1(2), so no autonomous region can be formed there.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** Explain how territory is organised under the Sixth Schedule, and contrast it with the Fifth Schedule's territorial method.

#### Model solution

The Sixth Schedule itself lists the tribal areas in the table appended to paragraph 20, in Parts I to III for Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. Paragraph 1(1) constitutes each listed area an autonomous district, which stays within State executive authority. Where different Scheduled Tribes inhabit a district, paragraph 1(2) lets the Governor divide their areas into autonomous regions with Regional Councils. Paragraph 1(3) gives the Governor eight reorganisation clauses by public notification; creation, increase, diminution and union require prior consideration of a paragraph 14(1) Commission report, and consequential orders may amend paragraph 20 and its table.

The Fifth Schedule works differently. Paragraph 6 makes the **President** the author of territorial status, with gubernatorial consultation required only for enlargement and redefinition, and no commission-report condition.

So the Fifth centralises the creation of protected territory, while the Sixth constitutionalises it and devolves its shaping to the Governor under an evidentiary discipline.

**Rubric (10):** paragraph 20 listing and the four Parts 2; paragraph 1(1) district status within State authority 2; paragraph 1(2) autonomous regions 2; paragraph 1(3) powers and the Commission-report proviso 2; contrast with Fifth Schedule paragraph 6 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

A district exists. It now needs a government. Paragraph 2 builds the council — and Assam's special amendments immediately show that the ordinary model is not universal.

---

## Lesson 9: Building the Council: Composition, Term, Rules and the Amending Acts of 1988, 1995 and 2003

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Sixth Schedule paragraph 2 and the Assam-specific paragraphs 3A and 3B, with the current council map

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity account of the "30-member district council" and the table of parliamentary laws amending the Sixth Schedule, which supplies the 1995 and 2003 Assam amendments
CA search: "new Sixth Schedule amendment, council election or change in autonomous council composition in the four Sixth Schedule States, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No enacted change to Sixth Schedule council composition was located; an official reply of 4 August 2026 continued to identify ten autonomous district councils across the four States
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one ordinary model, one special design

```text
ORDINARY PARAGRAPH 2 MODEL                 BODOLAND SPECIAL DESIGN
District Council                            Bodoland Territorial Council
  NOT MORE THAN 30 members                    NOT MORE THAN 46 members
    not more than 4 NOMINATED by                40 ELECTED
      the Governor                                30 reserved for Scheduled Tribes
    the rest ELECTED on adult suffrage            5 reserved for non-tribal communities
  elected term: 5 years from the date           5 open to all communities
    appointed for the first meeting             6 NOMINATED by the Governor from
    after the general elections                   unrepresented communities,
  nominated members: during the                   including at least two women
    pleasure of the Governor
  extension: during a Proclamation of
    Emergency, or if the Governor
    considers elections impracticable,
    by not more than ONE YEAR AT A TIME
```

Caption: "up to 30" is the default, not a universal fact. Special constitutional amendments create different designs, and Bodoland is the standard example.

### Part A — Paragraph 2, clause by clause

**2(1).** There shall be a District Council for each autonomous district consisting of **not more than thirty members**, of whom **not more than four** shall be nominated by the Governor and the rest elected on the basis of adult suffrage.

Read "not more than" twice. The familiar formula "26 elected plus 4 nominated" is the standard textbook rendering of the maximum configuration, and it is useful recall — but the constitutional rule is a ceiling of thirty with a nomination ceiling of four.

**2(2).** There shall be a separate Regional Council for each autonomous region.

**2(3).** Each Council is a **body corporate** — perpetual succession, a common seal, and the capacity to sue and be sued in its own name. This is not decorative: corporate personality is what lets a council hold funds, contract and litigate.

**2(4).** Administration of an autonomous district vests in the District Council so far as it is not vested in a Regional Council; administration of an autonomous region vests in the Regional Council.

**2(5).** In a district with Regional Councils, the District Council has only delegated powers over their areas, besides those the Schedule confers.

**2(6).** The **Governor** makes rules for the **first constitution** of the Councils, in consultation with existing tribal councils or other representative tribal organisations in the district or region. Those rules cover composition and allocation of seats; delimitation of constituencies; voting qualifications and electoral rolls; qualifications for election; the term of office of Regional Council members; other election and nomination matters; procedure and conduct of business, including power to act notwithstanding a vacancy; and appointment of officers and staff.

Note what paragraph 2(6) implies: consultation with **existing tribal institutions** is written into the founding of these councils. The Sixth Schedule did not impose a design on the hills; it built one in conversation with the institutions already there.

**2(6A).** Elected members hold office for **five years from the date appointed for the first meeting** of the Council after the general elections, unless the Council is sooner dissolved under paragraph 16. Nominated members hold office **during the pleasure of the Governor**. Two provisos follow: the five-year period may be extended by the Governor while a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation, or where circumstances in the Governor's opinion make elections impracticable — by not more than one year at a time, and where an Emergency Proclamation is in operation, not beyond six months after it ceases; and a member elected to fill a casual vacancy serves only the remainder of the predecessor's term.

**2(7).** After its first constitution, the Council may itself make rules on the paragraph 2(6) matters **with the approval of the Governor**, and similarly on formation of subordinate local councils or boards and on transaction of business generally; until it does, the Governor's rules continue.

So rule-making migrates from the Governor to the Council, but never entirely out of the Governor's hands.

### Part B — The three amending Acts

Three ordinary laws have reshaped the Schedule since 1988, and they do not all point at the same State. The 1988 Act modified the Schedule for **Tripura and Mizoram**; the 1995 and 2003 Acts enlarged it for named councils in **Assam**, which is also the only State to which Article 244A applies. Hold the sequence as chronology, and keep the addressee of each Act in view.

| Instrument | What it did |
|---|---|
| Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1988 (67 of 1988) | Modified the Schedule for Tripura and Mizoram: inserted **paragraph 20BB**, under which the Governor acts **in his discretion** in a listed set of functions after consulting the Council of Ministers and, if he thinks it necessary, the District or Regional Council; provisions on application of Acts of Parliament and the State Legislature; a new paragraph 9(3) letting the Governor direct that the royalty share be made over within one year; paragraph 10 recast so that the licensing power reaches every **person resident in the district** rather than only non-tribals; paragraph 15 altered so that the **Governor**, not the State Legislature, revokes an annulment or suspension order, with the renewal proviso omitted; and paragraph 16 altered so that assumption of administration under 16(1)(b) no longer needs the State Legislature's previous approval, the second proviso requiring a hearing for the Council is omitted, and 16(3) becomes a bare duty to lay orders under 16(1) or 16(2) before that Legislature. The same Act also substituted serial numbers 2 and 3, and the entries relating to them, in Part III of the paragraph 20 table, which is how the **Mara** and **Lai** Districts came to carry the names they now bear. Lessons 11 and 12 work these through |
| Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1995 (42 of 1995) | Modified the Schedule for Assam: renamed the North Cachar Hills and Karbi Anglong District Councils as Autonomous Councils by a proviso to paragraph 2(3); inserted **paragraph 3A** with fifteen additional law-making heads for those two Councils and a duty on the Governor to reserve their Concurrent-List laws for the President; substituted paragraph 3(3) so that it opens "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A"; amended paragraph 12(1)(a) to cover paragraph 3A matters; omitted from paragraph 14(2) the words requiring the Governor's recommendations to accompany a Commission's report; and inserted **paragraph 20BA**, which makes consultation of the named Autonomous Council compulsory before the Governor acts in his discretion in a listed set of functions |
| Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 2003 (44 of 2003) | Modified the Schedule for Assam pursuant to the Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003: added the Bodoland Territorial Areas District as entry 3 in Part I of the paragraph 20 table; created the Bodoland Territorial Council by a further proviso to paragraph 2(3), with a composition ceiling in the proviso to paragraph 2(1); inserted **paragraph 3B** with forty additional heads and the same Presidential-reservation mechanism; substituted paragraph 3(3) again to save paragraph 3B(2); extended paragraph 12(1)(a) to paragraph 3B matters; inserted **paragraph 19(4)** for the interim period; and switched **four** provisions off for Bodoland |

✅ Those four switch-offs are worth isolating, because they are the clearest proof that the Schedule treats its councils individually rather than as a class.

```text
       WHAT THE 2003 AMENDMENT SWITCHED OFF FOR BODOLAND

  paragraph 1(2)   proviso: nothing in this sub-paragraph applies to
  autonomous        the BODOLAND TERRITORIAL AREAS DISTRICT
  regions           -> no autonomous region, therefore no Regional
                       Council, can be created there

  paragraph 4      4(6): "Nothing in this paragraph shall apply to
  justice               the Bodoland Territorial Council"
                   -> no village councils or courts, no council
                      appellate forum, no paragraph 4 rule-making

  paragraph 10     10(4): same formula
  money-lending    -> the special money-lending and trading
  and trading         regulation power is unavailable to the BTC

  paragraph 17     proviso: "nothing in this paragraph shall apply
  constituencies        to the Bodoland Territorial Areas District"
                   -> no exclusion of areas from reserved Assembly
                      constituencies inside the BTAD
```

Caption: one district, four express carve-outs — and in each case the thing removed was a general Schedule device that the Bodoland settlement replaced with something of its own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** Read the carve-outs against what the same Act gave. Internal plurality, elsewhere protected by autonomous regions under paragraph 1(2), is protected inside Bodoland by the seat structure in the proviso to paragraph 2(1) — thirty Scheduled Tribe seats, five non-tribal, five open and six nominated. The narrow money-lending and trading power is replaced by paragraph 3B's forty heads. The Schedule did not weaken the Bodoland Council by these exclusions; it substituted a different design and then removed the devices that would have duplicated or cut across it.

Three constitutional lessons follow.

1. **Paragraphs 3A and 3B** are where the enlarged subject-lists for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, and for the Bodoland Territorial Council, sit. They are additions to paragraph 3's ordinary field, not replacements of it — and each carries, in its own sub-paragraphs (2) and (3), a requirement that the Governor reserve for the **President** any law of that Council relating to a Concurrent List matter. Lesson 10 teaches the mechanism.
2. **The Sixth Schedule is asymmetric within its own four-State field.** It is not one uniform model lightly applied four times.
3. **All three amendments were ordinary parliamentary laws**, because paragraph 21 says a law amending the Schedule is not an Article 368 amendment. The most significant institutional changes in this topic have been made by ordinary legislation.

### Part C — The current council map

| State | Autonomous District Councils | Point to remember |
|---|---|---|
| Assam | Bodoland Territorial Council; Karbi Anglong Autonomous Council; Dima Hasao, formerly North Cachar Hills, Autonomous Council | Special amendments of 1995 and 2003 apply here |
| Meghalaya | Khasi Hills, Jaintia Hills and Garo Hills Autonomous District Councils | Paragraph 12A gives State law primacy in case of repugnancy |
| Tripura | Tripura Tribal Areas Autonomous District Council | Brought within the Sixth Schedule framework in the 1980s; its territory is defined through the 1979 State Act |
| Mizoram | Chakma, Lai and Mara Autonomous District Councils | Three councils coexist with Article 371G protection at State level |

An official reply dated 4 August 2026 identified ten Sixth Schedule Autonomous District Councils across these four States — three in Assam, three in Meghalaya, three in Mizoram and one in Tripura. Names, office-holders and election cycles change; the constitutional power map does not.

### Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** A council of up to thirty members for an entire district, with up to four members nominated by the Governor, is a thin form of representative government.

**Reply.** The council is small because its field is bounded: it legislates on the paragraph 3 subjects, administers listed services and levies specified taxes, rather than governing a State. Nomination exists to include communities that elections may leave out, which is exactly how the Bodoland design uses its six nominated seats.

**Residual.** Nomination at the Governor's pleasure under paragraph 2(6A) sits uneasily with elected accountability, and an elected majority community inside an autonomous district can marginalise smaller groups. Autonomous regions under paragraph 1(2) and nominated seats are partial answers, not complete ones — and in the Bodoland Territorial Areas District the first of those answers is unavailable, because the 2003 proviso excludes that district from paragraph 1(2) altogether, leaving the reserved-seat structure to do the whole of that work.

### UPSC application

- Say "not more than thirty" and "not more than four nominated" — then add that the textbook maximum configuration is 26 elected plus 4 nominated.
- Attach the five-year term to **elected** members only, and run it from the date appointed for the first meeting.
- Quote the Bodoland ceiling — **not more than forty-six** members, with its forty elected and 30/5/5 breakdown — as the exception that disproves universality.
- Attach 1988 to Tripura and Mizoram, 1995 to Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills, and 2003 to Bodoland.
- For Bodoland, remember what was taken away as well as what was given: paragraphs 1(2), 4, 10 and 17 do not apply there.
- Use paragraph 21 to explain how all of this was done without an Article 368 amendment.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 2(1): District Council of not more than thirty members, of whom not more than four are nominated by the Governor and the rest elected on adult suffrage; the standard maximum configuration is 26 elected plus 4 nominated.
2. Paragraph 2(2): a separate Regional Council for each autonomous region.
3. Paragraph 2(3): each Council is a body corporate.
4. Paragraph 2(4) and 2(5): administration vests in the Council; District Council powers over a Regional Council's area are delegated.
5. Paragraph 2(6): the Governor makes first-constitution rules in consultation with existing tribal councils or representative tribal organisations.
6. Paragraph 2(6A): elected members serve five years from the date appointed for the first meeting after general elections, while nominated members hold office during the Governor's pleasure.
7. Extension is possible during an Emergency Proclamation or where elections are impracticable, by not more than one year at a time.
8. Paragraph 2(7): after first constitution the Council may make rules with the Governor's approval.
9. 1988 amendment: Tripura and Mizoram — **paragraph 20BB** discretion in listed functions, application of Acts, the paragraph 9(3) one-year royalty time limit, paragraph 10 extended to **every person resident in the district**, and lighter supervision under paragraphs 15 and 16.
10. 1995 amendment: Assam — Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills renamed Autonomous Councils, paragraph 3A's additional law-making powers with reservation of their Concurrent-List laws for the President, the paragraph 14(2) omission of the Governor's recommendations, and **paragraph 20BA**'s compulsory consultation of those Councils.
11. 2003 amendment: Assam — Bodoland Territorial Areas District and Bodoland Territorial Council after the Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003, with paragraph 3B, paragraph 19(4), and four express switch-offs: no autonomous region under the proviso to paragraph 1(2), no paragraph 4 justice architecture under 4(6), no paragraph 10 regulation power under 10(4), and no paragraph 17 constituency exclusion under its proviso.
12. Bodoland Territorial Council, under the proviso to paragraph 2(1): **not more than forty-six** members — forty elected, of which thirty are reserved for Scheduled Tribes, five for non-tribal communities and five open to all communities; plus six nominated by the Governor from unrepresented communities of the Bodoland Territorial Areas District, of whom at least two shall be women, with the same rights and privileges including voting rights.
13. Paragraphs 3A and 3B carry the enlarged Assam subject-lists — fifteen heads for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, forty for the Bodoland Territorial Council — and each requires the Governor to reserve that Council's Concurrent-List laws for the President.
14. All these amendments were ordinary parliamentary laws under paragraph 21.
15. Ten Autonomous District Councils operated across the four States on official material dated 4 August 2026.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it unsafe to answer "every Autonomous District Council has 26 elected and 4 nominated members"?

**Model answer:** Because paragraph 2(1) sets ceilings, not a fixed strength: a District Council consists of **not more than thirty** members, of whom **not more than four** are nominated by the Governor. The 26-plus-4 figure describes the maximum ordinary configuration. Parliament has also created special designs by ordinary law under paragraph 21 — most prominently the Bodoland Territorial Council's ceiling of not more than forty-six members, comprising forty elected and six nominated by the Governor from unrepresented communities. Generalising one council's composition to all ten is therefore wrong on both the ordinary text and the special amendments.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming constitutional uniformity across the Sixth Schedule. Within one Schedule and four States, composition, subject-lists and the application of laws all vary by State-specific paragraphs.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "Asymmetry inside the Sixth Schedule is a feature of its design, not a defect." Discuss with reference to the Assam arrangements.

#### Model solution

The Sixth Schedule begins from a uniform template. Paragraph 2(1) provides a District Council of not more than thirty members, of whom not more than four are nominated by the Governor and the rest elected on adult suffrage; paragraph 3 supplies a common subject-list; paragraph 21 allows Parliament to amend the Schedule by ordinary law without engaging Article 368.

That clause is the engine of asymmetry, within the four-State field Article 244(2) fixes. The 1988 amendment gave Tripura and Mizoram paragraph 20BB discretion, State-specific application-of-laws rules, a royalty time limit, a paragraph 10 licensing power reaching every resident trader, and lighter supervision under paragraphs 15 and 16. The 1995 amendment renamed the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, inserted paragraph 3A, thinned paragraph 14(2), and by paragraph 20BA obliged the Governor to consult them. The 2003 amendment, following the Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003, created the Bodoland Territorial Areas District and a Council of not more than forty-six members with paragraph 3B's enlarged list, inserted paragraph 19(4), and switched paragraphs 1(2), 4, 10 and 17 off there. Assam alone also carries Article 244A, used once for Meghalaya between 1970 and 1972.

Asymmetry here is responsive rather than arbitrary: each enlargement answered a political settlement, demographic pattern or administrative need, by a transparent legislative route.

The qualification is that negotiated asymmetry can entrench inequality between councils and generate fresh demands for parity. Asymmetry is legitimate only when the differentiating reason is stated, revisable and accountable.

**Rubric (15):** paragraph 2(1) ordinary template 2; paragraph 21 ordinary-law amendment route and its four-State limit 3; 1988, 1995 and 2003 amendments accurately attributed 5; Article 244A as the Assam-only addition 2; evaluative treatment of asymmetry with qualification 3.

### Why the next lesson follows

The council is constituted. The reason it matters is that it can make law — and the exact boundaries of that law-making field are where most examination traps in this topic are set.

---

### Visual: the paragraph 3 field and its edges

| Head | Exact subject under paragraph 3(1) | Edge written into the text |
|---|---|---|
| (a) | Allotment, occupation or use, or setting apart, of land for agriculture, grazing, residential, other non-agricultural or community-interest purposes | **Other than any land which is a reserved forest**; and a proviso saves the State's power of compulsory acquisition for public purposes |
| (b) | Management of any forest | **Not being a reserved forest** |
| (c) | Use of any canal or water-course | For the purpose of **agriculture** |
| (d) | Regulation of the practice of jhum or other forms of shifting cultivation | Regulation, not prohibition by implication |
| (e) | Establishment of village or town committees or councils and their powers | Local institutional architecture |
| (f) | Any other matter relating to village or town administration, including village or town police and public health and sanitation | The widest head, but still local-administration anchored |
| (g) | Appointment or succession of Chiefs or Headmen | Customary leadership |
| (h) | Inheritance of property | Customary property devolution |
| (i) | Marriage and divorce | Personal law of the community |
| (j) | Social customs | Residual customary field |

Caption: the field is land, forest, water, cultivation, local administration, leadership and personal custom — precisely the subjects on which general law most often displaces a functioning local order.

### 1. Who holds the power, and over what

Paragraph 3(1) allocates the power with care. The **Regional Council** legislates for all areas within its autonomous region. The **District Council** legislates for all areas within the district **except** those under the authority of Regional Councils within it. Territory is divided first; competence follows territory.

### 2. The reserved-forest exclusion, twice over

The words "reserved forest" appear in heads (a) and (b), and paragraph 3(2) defines the term: a reserved forest means any area which is a reserved forest under the **Assam Forest Regulation, 1891**, or under any other law for the time being in force in the area in question.

This single adjective is probably the most frequently examined word in the Sixth Schedule. The council's land power under (a) does not extend to land which is a reserved forest; its forest-management power under (b) is confined to forests **not** being reserved forests. A statement that an autonomous district council controls all forests in its district is wrong on the face of the text.

### 3. The acquisition proviso

The proviso to head (a) says that nothing in council law "shall prevent the compulsory acquisition of any land, whether occupied or unoccupied, for public purposes by the Government of the State concerned in accordance with the law for the time being in force authorizing such acquisition."

So the council's land competence does not create immunity from acquisition. The State may still acquire for public purposes under the applicable acquisition law. This is the Sixth Schedule's own acknowledgement that autonomy sits inside a State.

### 4. Paragraph 3(3): assent — and the Assam exception most summaries omit

In its general form the clause is a single sentence:

"All laws made under this paragraph shall be submitted forthwith to the Governor and, until assented to by him, shall have no effect."

Compare with Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(4), where the regulation goes to the **President**. The mirror-image pairing is worth memorising as a single sentence: **Fifth Schedule regulation, President's assent; Sixth Schedule council law, Governor's assent.** Reversing them is a classic error.

But paragraph 3(3) no longer reads that way everywhere. **In its application to the State of Assam** it has been substituted twice — by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1995 and again by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 2003 — and the substituted text opens with a saving.

✅ **Fact — paragraph 3(3) as it stands for Assam:** "**Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B**, all laws made under this paragraph or sub-paragraph (1) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (1) of paragraph 3B shall be submitted forthwith to the Governor and, until assented to by him, shall have no effect."

Those opening words carry the whole exception. For Assam the Governor is the **default** assenting authority, not the only one, and the clause itself tells you where to look for the cases in which he is not.

```text
            A COUNCIL LAW IS PASSED UNDER PARAGRAPH 3
                              |
            +-----------------+------------------+
            |                                    |
   MEGHALAYA, TRIPURA,                        ASSAM
     MIZORAM                      (paragraph 3(3) as substituted
   paragraph 3(3) in its             in 1995 and again in 2003)
   ORIGINAL form                                |
            |                      does the law RELATE TO a matter
            v                      in LIST III, the Concurrent List?
   submitted forthwith to                       |
   the GOVERNOR; no effect           +----------+-----------+
   until he assents                  |                      |
                                    NO                     YES
                                     |                      |
                        GOVERNOR assents under     GOVERNOR SHALL RESERVE
                        paragraph 3(3)             it for the PRESIDENT
                                                   -- 3A(2) for the Karbi
                                                   Anglong and North Cachar
                                                   Hills Councils, 3B(2)
                                                   for the Bodoland Council
                                                            |
                                              PRESIDENT declares assent
                                              or withholds it -- 3A(3)/3B(3)
                                                            |
                                              proviso: he may direct return
                                              with a message; the Council
                                              reconsiders WITHIN SIX MONTHS;
                                              if passed again, with or
                                              without amendment, it is
                                              PRESENTED AGAIN to the President
```

Caption: one Schedule, two assent destinations, and the fork is cut by the content of the law rather than by the head it was made under.

#### What paragraphs 3A and 3B actually contain

✅ **Paragraph 3A**, inserted for Assam in 1995, gives the **North Cachar Hills Autonomous Council** and the **Karbi Anglong Autonomous Council**, within their respective districts, power to make laws on **fifteen further heads** lettered (a) to (o): industries; communications, including roads, bridges, ferries, municipal tramways, ropeways, inland waterways and non-mechanically-propelled vehicles; preservation and improvement of stock, prevention of animal diseases, veterinary training and practice and cattle pounds; primary and secondary education; agriculture, including agricultural education and research and plant protection; fisheries; water, including supplies, irrigation, canals, drainage, embankments, storage and water power; social security and social insurance, employment and unemployment; non-technical flood-control schemes for villages, paddy fields, markets and towns; theatre, dramatic performances, cinemas, sports and amusements; public health and sanitation, hospitals and dispensaries; minor irrigation; trade and commerce in, and the production, supply and distribution of, foodstuffs, cattle fodder, raw cotton and raw jute; libraries, museums and similar State-controlled institutions and non-national monuments and records; and **alienation of land**. Several heads are expressly made subject to named Union List entries.

✅ **Paragraph 3B**, inserted for Assam in 2003, gives the **Bodoland Territorial Council**, within its areas, power to make laws on a list of **forty heads** numbered (i) to (xl), running from agriculture and animal husbandry through co-operation, cultural affairs, education at every level, fisheries, flood control, food and civil supply, **forests other than reserved forests**, handloom and textile, health and family welfare, intoxicating liquors, irrigation, labour and employment, **land and revenue**, library services, lotteries and theatres and cinemas, markets and fairs, municipal and local authorities, museums and monuments, panchayat and rural development, planning and development, printing and stationery, public health engineering, public works, publicity, registration of births and deaths, relief and rehabilitation, sericulture, small and cottage industry, social welfare, soil conservation, sports and youth welfare, statistics, tourism, transport, tribal research institutes, urban development and town planning, weights and measures, to **welfare of plain tribes and backward classes**. A proviso protects existing land rights: nothing in such laws may extinguish or modify a citizen's existing rights and privileges in respect of his land at the commencement of the 2003 Act, or disallow an otherwise eligible citizen from acquiring land by inheritance, allotment, settlement or any other transfer within the Bodoland Territorial Areas District.

#### The reservation rule, which is the examinable part

Each of those paragraphs then carries the same two-step mechanism.

✅ **Sub-paragraph (2)** — of paragraph 3A for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, of paragraph 3B for the Bodoland Territorial Council — provides that all laws made by that Council **under paragraph 3 or under that paragraph**, "in so far as they relate to matters specified in **List III** of the Seventh Schedule", shall be submitted forthwith to the **Governor, who shall reserve the same for the consideration of the President**.

✅ **Sub-paragraph (3)** governs what follows. The President shall declare either that he assents to the law or that he withholds assent. Its proviso lets him direct the Governor to return the law to the Council with a message requesting reconsideration of the law or of specified provisions, and in particular consideration of any amendments he recommends; the Council shall then consider the law **within six months** of receiving the message, and if the law is again passed, with or without amendment, it shall be **presented again to the President**.

Two features repay close reading. The Governor has **no discretion** at that point — the text says he *shall* reserve. And the trigger is not the lettered head under which the Council legislated; it is whether the law "relates to matters specified in List III". A law made under an ordinary paragraph 3 head therefore goes to the President if its content falls in the Concurrent List.

That has a concrete illustration the examiner can build a question on. ✅ Paragraph 3(1)(h) gives councils **inheritance of property** and 3(1)(i) **marriage and divorce**. ✅ **Entry 5 of the Concurrent List** covers "marriage and divorce; infants and minors; adoption; wills, intestacy and succession; joint family and partition" and matters governed by personal law. ⚠️ The inference, stated as one: a council law on inheritance or on marriage and divorce relates to a List III matter, so in **Meghalaya, Tripura or Mizoram** it is completed by the **Governor's** assent under paragraph 3(3), while in **Assam** the same law made by the Karbi Anglong, North Cachar Hills or Bodoland Council is reserved by the Governor for the **President**. Whether a particular law "relates to" a List III matter is for the reserving authority to judge in the first instance, so state the rule and the trigger rather than predicting the outcome.

| | Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram | Assam |
|---|---|---|
| Text of paragraph 3(3) | Original | Substituted in 1995 and again in 2003, opening "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B" |
| Council law on a non-Concurrent paragraph 3 head | Governor's assent | Governor's assent |
| Council law relating to a List III matter | Governor's assent — these States have no 3A or 3B | Governor **shall reserve** for the President |
| Who may ask for reconsideration | No such mechanism in paragraph 3 | President, by message through the Governor; six months to reconsider; re-presentation |
| Councils affected | All | Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills (3A), Bodoland Territorial Council (3B) |

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** This is the Assam councils paying a price for their enlarged lists. Paragraphs 3A and 3B gave them a legislative field far beyond the ten general heads — including land and revenue, education and forests for Bodoland — and the Constitution balanced that enlargement by moving the Concurrent-List slice of their output up to the Union's assenting authority. Wider competence, higher supervision: the two were written into the same amendment.

A related point is taught in Lesson 12: paragraph 12A makes a Meghalaya council law or regulation void to the extent of repugnancy with a State law on the same matter. So assent is not the only constraint on council legislation.

### 5. The four traps, stated as propositions

| Tempting claim | Correct position |
|---|---|
| The council controls every forest in its district | Paragraph 3(1)(b) excludes reserved forests, as defined in paragraph 3(2) |
| The council owns the minerals in its district | Paragraph 9 provides for an agreed **share of royalties** from State-granted licences and leases, not subsoil ownership |
| The council may legislate on any State List subject | Competence is confined to the paragraph 3 heads, plus any enlargement under paragraphs 3A or 3B for named Assam councils |
| Council laws take the President's assent | Paragraph 3(3) requires the **Governor's** assent — except in **Assam**, where that clause opens "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B", so a council law relating to a Concurrent List matter is reserved by the Governor for the **President** |

UPSC regularly converts one omitted adjective — "reserved," "specified," "agreed" — into the decisive option. Reading the Schedule's qualifiers is therefore the exam skill, not a scholarly nicety.

### 6. Why these particular subjects

The ten heads are not a random devolution list. Trace them against the risks identified in Lesson 1.

```text
land allotment, occupation, use          -> land alienation risk
management of non-reserved forests       -> livelihood and resource risk
canal and watercourse use for agriculture-> subsistence agriculture
regulation of jhum                       -> customary cultivation practice
village/town committees and powers       -> local institutional continuity
village/town administration and police   -> everyday order and sanitation
Chiefs and Headmen                       -> customary political authority
inheritance                              -> intergenerational property transfer
marriage and divorce                     -> family and kinship order
social customs                           -> cultural self-definition
```

Each head corresponds to a point at which general law, applied without adjustment, would have displaced a working local institution. That is the analytical sentence to deploy in a Mains answer, and it differentiates a council from a mere municipal body.

### 7. Comparison: council law against Tribes Advisory Council advice

A council law, once assented to, is **law**: it binds, it is enforceable, and it is published under paragraph 11 in the State Official Gazette, on which publication it has the force of law. Advice under Fifth Schedule paragraph 4(2) binds no one. That difference — between an output that is law and an output that is counsel — is the real content of the phrase "greater autonomy" in the Sixth Schedule.

### UPSC application

- Learn the ten heads; they can be asked directly.
- Say "other than reserved forest" every time you mention council land or forest power.
- Mention the compulsory-acquisition proviso; it shows the limits of autonomy without needing a case.
- Fix the assent pairing in a single sentence and rehearse it — then add the Assam rider at once, because that is what separates a complete answer from a textbook one.
- If the State in the question is **Assam**, ask whether the council law relates to a Concurrent List matter before naming the assenting authority.
- When a question asks whether councils are "self-governing," answer through the subject list, the assent requirement and the repugnancy rule together.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 3(1): Regional Council legislates for its region; District Council for the rest of the district.
2. Head (a): allotment, occupation, use or setting apart of land for agriculture, grazing, residence, non-agricultural or community purposes.
3. Head (a) excludes land which is a reserved forest and is subject to a compulsory-acquisition proviso.
4. Head (b): management of any forest not being a reserved forest.
5. Head (c): use of any canal or watercourse for the purpose of agriculture.
6. Head (d): regulation of jhum or other shifting cultivation.
7. Head (e): establishment of village or town committees or councils and their powers.
8. Head (f): any other matter relating to village or town administration, including village or town police, public health and sanitation.
9. Heads (g) to (j): Chiefs or Headmen, inheritance of property, marriage and divorce, social customs.
10. Paragraph 3(2) defines reserved forest by reference to the Assam Forest Regulation, 1891 or other law in force.
11. Paragraph 3(3): council laws go to the Governor and have no effect until assented to. For **Assam** the clause was substituted in 1995 and again in 2003 and now opens "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B".
12. Fifth Schedule regulation takes the President's assent; Sixth Schedule council law takes the Governor's — except that in Assam a council law relating to a **List III** matter is reserved by the Governor for the **President** under paragraph 3A(2) or 3B(2), the Governor having no discretion to withhold the reservation.
13. Paragraph 9 gives a share of mineral royalties, not mineral ownership.
14. Competence is bounded by the paragraph 3 heads, plus paragraph 3A's fifteen heads (a) to (o) for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils and paragraph 3B's forty heads (i) to (xl) for the Bodoland Territorial Council, whose proviso protects existing land rights. Sub-paragraph (3) of each lets the President assent, withhold assent, or return the law with a message for reconsideration within six months and re-presentation.
15. Paragraph 11: council laws, rules and regulations are published in the State Official Gazette and have the force of law on publication.

### Concept check

**Question:** An autonomous district council enacts a law regulating felling and regeneration across all forests in its district, including reserved forests. Is the law valid?

**Model answer:** Not in its full sweep. Paragraph 3(1)(b) confines council law-making to the management of **any forest not being a reserved forest**, and paragraph 3(2) defines reserved forest by reference to the Assam Forest Regulation, 1891 or any other law in force in that area. Paragraph 3(1)(a) carries the same exclusion for land. The council may therefore legislate for non-reserved forests, but the portion purporting to cover reserved forests falls outside paragraph 3. Even the valid portion has no effect until it is assented to — by the **Governor** under paragraph 3(3), or, in **Assam**, by the **President**, to whom the Governor must reserve the law under paragraph 3A(2) or 3B(2) if it relates to a Concurrent List matter.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the council's forest power as plenary because forests are central to tribal livelihood. The Schedule protects the livelihood interest by a bounded competence, not an unlimited one.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Examine the legislative competence of Sixth Schedule councils and explain why the Schedule drew its boundaries where it did.

#### Model solution

Paragraph 3(1) confers law-making power on Regional Councils for their regions and on District Councils for the remainder of the district, over ten heads: allotment, occupation, use or setting apart of land; management of forests; use of canals or watercourses for agriculture; regulation of jhum; establishment of village or town committees and their powers; other village or town administration, including local police and public health and sanitation; appointment or succession of Chiefs or Headmen; inheritance; marriage and divorce; and social customs.

The boundaries are deliberate. Each head marks a point at which general law would displace a functioning customary institution — land tenure, resource management, cultivation practice, local authority, kinship and personal law. The list is a map of vulnerability converted into competence.

The exclusions are equally deliberate. Reserved-forest land and the management of reserved forests fall outside the power, as paragraph 3(2) defines them; the proviso to head (a) preserves compulsory acquisition; and paragraph 3(3) withholds effect until assent. That assent is not uniform. In Assam paragraph 3(3) is expressly subject to paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2), under which a council law relating to a List III matter must be reserved by the Governor for the President. For Meghalaya, paragraph 12A voids a repugnant council law against State law.

Councils are genuine legislatures within a bounded field, not miniature State legislatures. Autonomy here means authority over the subjects that constitute community life, exercised inside the State's constitutional order — both the design's strength and the source of demands for enlargement.

**Rubric (15):** accurate coverage of the ten heads 5; reserved-forest exclusion with paragraph 3(2) definition 2; compulsory-acquisition proviso 1; paragraph 3(3) assent with the Assam paragraph 3A(2) and 3B(2) reservation 3; the Meghalaya repugnancy rule 1; analytical explanation of why these boundaries 3.

### Why the next lesson follows

Legislative power alone does not make a government. The council also administers services, runs courts and raises revenue — three powers no Fifth Schedule institution possesses.

## Lesson 11: Executive, Judicial and Fiscal Powers of Sixth Schedule Councils

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Sixth Schedule paragraphs 4 to 11 and 13 — justice, services, funds, taxes, royalties, money-lending regulation and publication

Caption: four powers, each real and each bounded by an express condition — assent, previous approval, a tribal-party limit, or audit and State principles.

### Part A — Justice: paragraphs 4 and 5

**Paragraph 4(1).** The Council may constitute **village councils or courts** for the trial of suits and cases "between the parties all of whom belong to Scheduled Tribes within such areas," other than suits and cases to which paragraph 5(1) applies, to the exclusion of any court in the State. It may appoint members of those village councils and presiding officers of those courts, and such officers as are necessary to administer the laws made under paragraph 3.

Read the jurisdictional gate carefully: **all** parties must belong to Scheduled Tribes within the area. One non-tribal party takes the matter outside this jurisdiction.

**Paragraph 4(2).** The Regional Council — or a court constituted by it — or, where there is no Regional Council for the area, the District Council or its court, exercises **appellate** power over all such suits and cases. And then the striking words: "no other court except the High Court and the Supreme Court shall have jurisdiction over such suits or cases."

**Paragraph 4(3).** The High Court exercises such jurisdiction over those appellate matters "as the Governor may from time to time by order specify." So High Court reach into this system is defined by gubernatorial order.

**Paragraph 4(4).** The Council may, **with the previous approval of the Governor**, make rules on the constitution of village councils and courts and their powers; trial procedure; appellate procedure; enforcement of decisions and orders; and ancillary matters.

**Paragraph 4(5)** is the clause most summaries miss.
