From a date the President appoints, after consulting the State Government, paragraph 4 operates differently for a specified district or region: the tribal-party limitation is replaced by categories the **Governor** specifies; sub-paragraphs (2) and (3) are omitted; and rule-making shifts from the Council to the **Governor**, who also designates the courts to which appeals lie. The Constitution therefore built in a mechanism to convert a customary-court system into a more conventional one, district by district.

✅ **Paragraph 4(6)**, inserted for Assam by the 2003 amendment, is the clause that most changes the map: "Nothing in this paragraph shall apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council constituted under the proviso to sub-paragraph (3) of paragraph 2 of this Schedule." The whole of paragraph 4 — village councils and courts, the appellate structure, the High Court's specified jurisdiction, the rule-making power — is therefore **switched off for the Bodoland Territorial Council**. Nine of the ten listed tribal areas have the paragraph 4 justice architecture; the Bodoland Territorial Areas District does not. Never write "every Sixth Schedule council runs its own courts."

**Paragraph 5.** The Governor may confer on a Council, on courts constituted by it, or on an officer he appoints, powers under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 or the Code of Criminal Procedure, for the trial of suits or cases arising out of a law he specifies, or of offences punishable with death, transportation for life, or imprisonment for a term of **not less than five years**. He may withdraw or modify those powers. Where paragraph 5 applies, the Codes do not otherwise apply to such trials; and after a paragraph 4(5) appointed date, the Governor can no longer confer paragraph 5 powers on that Council or its courts.

So serious offences do not fall to village councils by default. They reach a council court only if the Governor confers the power, and the power is revocable.

### Part B — Executive functions: paragraph 6

**6(1).** The District Council for an autonomous district may **establish, construct or manage** primary schools, dispensaries, markets, cattle pounds, ferries, fisheries, roads, road transport and waterways in the district; and may, **with the previous approval of the Governor**, make regulations for their regulation and control — and in particular may prescribe the **language and the manner in which primary education shall be imparted** in the district's primary schools.

That last power is culturally significant and easy to overlook. A council may decide the medium of primary instruction in its district — a direct instrument for linguistic and cultural continuity.

**6(2).** The Governor may, **with the consent of any District Council**, entrust to it or its officers — conditionally or unconditionally — functions relating to agriculture, animal husbandry, community projects, co-operative societies, social welfare, village planning, "or any other matter to which the executive power of the State extends."

Note the architecture: paragraph 6(1) is an **own** function; paragraph 6(2) is an **entrusted** function requiring the Council's consent. Functional autonomy is therefore strongest where staff and funds actually accompany the subject — which is precisely where State-council practice varies most.

### Part C — Money: paragraphs 7, 8, 9, 10, 11 and 13

**Paragraph 7 — Funds.** A District Fund for each autonomous district and a Regional Fund for each autonomous region, crediting all moneys received by the Council in the course of administration. The Governor makes rules for management and procedure. Accounts are kept in such form as the **Comptroller and Auditor-General of India** may, with the President's approval, prescribe; the CAG causes them to be audited; and the reports go to the Governor, who causes them to be laid **before the Council**.

**Paragraph 8 — Revenue and taxes.**

| Power | Who holds it | Text |
|---|---|---|
| Assess and collect land revenue | Regional Council for its region; District Council for the rest of the district | In accordance with the principles for the time being followed by the State Government in assessing lands for land revenue generally |
| Taxes on lands and buildings; tolls on persons resident | Regional Council for its region; District Council for the rest | Within those areas |
| Taxes on professions, trades, callings and employments; on animals, vehicles and boats; on entry of goods into a market for sale and tolls on passengers and goods carried in ferries; for maintenance of schools, dispensaries or roads; and on **entertainment and amusements** | **District Council only** | Paragraph 8(3), clauses (a) to (e) — clause (e) inserted in 2016 |
| Regulations for levy and collection | Either Council | Submitted forthwith to the Governor; no effect until assented to — paragraph 8(4) |

The third row is a precision point, and it carries two of them.

First, paragraph 8(3) lists powers of the **District Council**, not of Regional Councils. Second, the list has **five** lettered clauses, not the four that most summaries print. ✅ Clause **(e), "taxes on entertainment and amusements"**, was inserted by **section 16 of the Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016** — the Goods and Services Tax amendment — with effect from **16 September 2016**. The same section tidied the punctuation of clauses (c) and (d) so that the new clause reads as the last member of the list.

✅ **Why a GST amendment reached into the Sixth Schedule.** Section 17 of the same Act rewrote **Entry 62 of the State List**, which until then covered taxes on luxuries including entertainments and amusements. As substituted, Entry 62 reads: "Taxes on entertainments and amusements **to the extent levied and collected by a Panchayat or a Municipality or a Regional Council or a District Council**." The State's own entertainment tax went into the GST pool; what was carved out and left standing was the **local-body layer**. Section 16 then gave the autonomous District Council an express head of its own inside this Schedule, so that the surviving layer had a textual home here and not only in the Seventh Schedule.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, and a precision trap worth storing.** The two texts are deliberately close but are **not co-extensive**. Entry 62 names a Regional Council *and* a District Council; Sixth Schedule paragraph 8(3) confers its taxes on the **District Council alone**, exactly as the first precision point says. An answer that reads the substituted Entry 62 as if it had enlarged paragraph 8(3) for Regional Councils has over-read it. Note also what clause (e) is: a **head of taxing power** within this Schedule. It settles that an autonomous District Council has the power; it does not by itself decide how that power and the GST regime interact in any particular case.

**Paragraph 9 — Mineral royalties.** Such share of the royalties accruing each year from licences or leases for prospecting for, or extraction of, minerals granted by the **State Government** in respect of an area within an autonomous district "as may be agreed upon between the Government of the State and the District Council" shall be made over to that Council. If a dispute arises on the share, it is referred to the **Governor for determination**, the amount he determines **in his discretion** is deemed payable, and his decision is **final**. ✅ As amended for **Tripura and Mizoram** in 1988, paragraph 9 carries a further sub-paragraph (3): the Governor may by order direct that the share be made over to the Council **within one year** of the agreement under 9(1) or the determination under 9(2). Assam and Meghalaya have no such time limit in the text.

Three consequences. The lease is granted by the **State**, not the council. The council receives an **agreed share**, which is neither ownership nor consent. And the dispute route ends with the Governor, not a court — one of the few places in the Schedule where finality is expressly conferred.

**Paragraph 10 — Money-lending and trading.** A District Council may make regulations regulating and controlling money-lending or trading within the district by persons other than Scheduled Tribes resident in the district. Such regulations may require a licence for money-lending; prescribe a maximum rate of interest; provide for maintenance and inspection of accounts; and prescribe that no person who is not a member of the Scheduled Tribes resident in the district shall carry on wholesale or retail business in any commodity except under a licence. Two provisos matter: the regulation must be passed by a majority of **not less than three-fourths of the total membership** of the Council; and it may not refuse a licence to a money-lender or trader who has been carrying on business in the district since before the regulation was made. All such regulations require the Governor's assent under 10(3).

That is the general form. Two State-specific departures change it materially, and both are examinable.

✅ **Tripura and Mizoram, 1988.** Paragraph 10 was amended in its application to those two States so that the words "**by non-tribals**" are omitted from the heading; the words "**other than Scheduled Tribes**" are omitted from sub-paragraph (1); and clause (d) of sub-paragraph (2) is replaced by "prescribe that **no person resident in the district** shall carry on any trade, whether wholesale or retail, except under a licence issued in that behalf by the District Council". There, the licensing power reaches **every resident trader**, tribal and non-tribal alike, exactly as the amended text provides. The heading of the paragraph, which most summaries quote, no longer describes the Tripura and Mizoram version at all.

✅ **Bodoland, 2003.** Paragraph 10(4), inserted for Assam, provides that "Nothing in this paragraph shall apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council constituted under the proviso to sub-paragraph (3) of paragraph 2 of this Schedule." The whole money-lending and trading regulation power is therefore **unavailable to the Bodoland Territorial Council**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** Read the two departures together and the design logic appears. Where a council's own enlarged legislative list already covers the ground — paragraph 3B gives the Bodoland Council trade and commerce-adjacent heads and land and revenue — the Schedule withdraws the narrower special regulation power. Where no such enlargement exists, as in Tripura and Mizoram, it widened the regulation power instead by removing the tribal/non-tribal line. The Schedule adjusts the instrument to the council, not the council to the instrument.

Set this beside Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2)(c). Both Schedules attack debt-driven dispossession; one does it through a Governor's regulation needing the President's assent, the other through a council regulation needing a three-fourths majority and the Governor's assent.

**Paragraph 11 — Publication.** All laws, rules and regulations made under the Schedule by a Council shall be published forthwith in the Official Gazette of the State and shall, on such publication, have the force of law.

**Paragraph 13 — Budget visibility.** The estimated receipts and expenditure pertaining to an autonomous district which are to be credited to, or made from, the Consolidated Fund of the State shall be **first placed before the District Council for discussion** and then shown **separately** in the State's annual financial statement laid before the State Legislature under Article 202.

Paragraph 13 is an underused answer point: it gives an autonomous district a guaranteed, visible line in the State budget and a prior right of discussion.

### Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Calling this "fiscal autonomy" overstates the position: the council assesses land revenue on the State's principles, its tax regulations need the Governor's assent, its accounts are prescribed and audited by the CAG, and its mineral royalty share is agreed with the State and settled finally by the Governor.

**Reply.** Autonomy in a federal constitutional order never means immunity. What the Schedule gives is a genuine own-revenue base, a dedicated fund, budget visibility under paragraph 13 and audited accounts laid before the Council itself — a package no Fifth Schedule institution possesses.

**Residual.** The gap that survives is capacity. A revenue power without trained assessors, maintained records and collection machinery yields little; and the Governor's final determination of the royalty share concentrates a significant fiscal decision in one office.

### UPSC application

- Use the four-power structure — legislative, executive, judicial, fiscal — as the skeleton of any Sixth Schedule answer, and attach one textual limit to each.
- Quote the tribal-party jurisdictional gate in paragraph 4(1) exactly — then add that paragraph 4 does not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council at all.
- Remember that only the **High Court and the Supreme Court** retain jurisdiction over paragraph 4(2) matters.
- Use paragraph 9 for the royalty-share point and never say councils own minerals.
- Use paragraph 13 when a question asks how council finances connect to State accountability.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 4(1): village councils and courts for suits and cases between parties all of whom belong to Scheduled Tribes within the area. Paragraph 4(6), inserted in 2003, switches the whole of paragraph 4 off for the **Bodoland Territorial Council**.
2. Paragraph 4(2): Council or its court is the appellate forum; no other court except the High Court and Supreme Court has jurisdiction.
3. Paragraph 4(3) and 4(4): the Governor specifies the High Court's jurisdiction by order, and Council rules on these matters need his previous approval.
4. Paragraph 4(5): from a Presidentially appointed date, the model can be recast, with rule-making shifting to the Governor.
5. Paragraph 5: the Governor may confer CPC or CrPC powers, including for offences punishable with death, transportation for life or imprisonment of not less than five years, and may withdraw them.
6. Paragraph 6(1): establish, construct or manage primary schools, dispensaries, markets, cattle pounds, ferries, fisheries, roads, road transport and waterways, and prescribe the language and manner of primary education.
7. Paragraph 6(2): the Governor may entrust further functions with the Council's consent.
8. Paragraph 7: District and Regional Funds; CAG-prescribed accounts, CAG audit, reports laid before the Council.
9. Paragraph 8(1) and 8(2): land revenue assessed on the State's general principles; taxes on lands and buildings and tolls on residents.
10. Paragraph 8(3): District Council only, and **five** lettered clauses — professions, trades, callings and employments; animals, vehicles and boats; entry of goods into a market and ferry tolls; taxes for maintenance of schools, dispensaries or roads; and, by clause (e) inserted by section 16 of the Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016 with effect from 16 September 2016, **entertainment and amusements**. The companion change is section 17, which left entertainment and amusement taxes in Entry 62 of the State List only to the extent levied and collected by a Panchayat, a Municipality, a Regional Council or a District Council.
11. Paragraph 8(4): levy regulations need the Governor's assent.
12. Paragraph 9: agreed share of State-granted mineral royalties; dispute determined by the Governor in his discretion, and his decision is final. For **Tripura and Mizoram**, 9(3) added in 1988 lets the Governor direct that the share be made over within one year.
13. Paragraph 10: money-lending and trading regulation; three-fourths total-membership majority; protection of pre-existing businesses; Governor's assent under 10(3). **Not uniform:** in Assam and Meghalaya it reaches persons "other than Scheduled Tribes"; as amended for **Tripura and Mizoram** in 1988 the words "by non-tribals" and "other than Scheduled Tribes" were dropped and clause (2)(d) now reaches **every person resident in the district**; and under 10(4), inserted in 2003, paragraph 10 does not apply to the **Bodoland Territorial Council** at all.
14. Paragraph 11: publication in the State Official Gazette gives force of law.
15. Paragraph 13: district estimates placed before the Council first and shown separately in the State budget under Article 202.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State grants a mining lease inside an autonomous district. What is the council's constitutional entitlement, and what is it not?

**Model answer:** Under paragraph 9(1) the council is entitled to **such share of the royalties** accruing each year from that licence or lease as is **agreed upon between the State Government and the District Council**, and that share must be made over to the Council. Under paragraph 9(2), a dispute about the share is referred to the Governor, whose determination in his discretion is final. The council is **not** the grantor of the lease, does not own the minerals, and has no consent power over the grant under this paragraph. Any participatory requirement must come from other law.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading royalty sharing as a form of community consent. Revenue sharing internalises part of the cost; it does not substitute for rights recognition, appraisal or rehabilitation.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "Sixth Schedule councils possess the form of government but a conditioned substance." Discuss with reference to their executive, judicial and fiscal powers.

#### Model solution

The form is genuine. Paragraph 6(1) lets a District Council establish, construct or manage primary schools, dispensaries, markets, cattle pounds, ferries, fisheries, roads, road transport and waterways, and even prescribe the language and manner of primary education; paragraph 6(2) allows the Governor to entrust further State functions with the Council's consent. Paragraph 4 permits village councils and courts for suits and cases where all parties belong to Scheduled Tribes within the area, with the Council as appellate forum and only the High Court and the Supreme Court retaining jurisdiction. Paragraphs 7 to 9 give District and Regional Funds, land-revenue assessment, specified taxes and an agreed share of mineral royalties.

The substance is conditioned at every point. Regulations under paragraph 6(1) require the Governor's previous approval; the High Court's jurisdiction is specified by gubernatorial order under paragraph 4(3); paragraph 4(5) lets the President trigger a recast in which rule-making passes to the Governor; paragraph 5 powers over serious offences are conferred and withdrawable; land revenue follows State principles; tax regulations need the Governor's assent under paragraph 8(4); accounts are prescribed and audited by the Comptroller and Auditor-General; and paragraph 9 makes the Governor's determination of the royalty share final.

These conditions are not defects. Autonomy inside a federal Constitution is always nested. The real deficit is capacity — trained assessors, maintained records, staff and funds that follow entrusted functions — which determines whether a constitutionally real power becomes a usable one.

**Rubric (15):** executive powers with paragraph 6(1) and 6(2) distinction 3; judicial powers with the tribal-party gate and appellate structure 4; fiscal powers across paragraphs 7 to 9 4; accurate statement of each conditioning requirement 2; capacity-focused evaluative conclusion 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Autonomy raises a question the Fifth Schedule never faces: which Acts of Parliament and of the State Legislature actually operate inside an autonomous district, and what happens when a council goes wrong. Both answers are State-specific.

---
## Lesson 12: Which Laws Apply, and the Constitutional Brakes on Autonomy

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Sixth Schedule paragraphs 12, 12A, 12AA, 12B, 14, 15, 16, 17, 19, 20BA, 20BB, 20C and 21

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity note that Acts "do not apply or apply with modifications," corrected by the four State-specific paragraphs and by the textbook's own footnote distinguishing who holds the direction power in each State
CA search: "suspension, supersession or dissolution of an autonomous district council, or Sixth Schedule amendment Bill in Parliament, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No supersession, dissolution or enacted Sixth Schedule amendment was located for this period
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four States, four different filters

| State | Paragraph | Who can switch off or modify a **State** Act | Who can switch off or modify an **Act of Parliament** | Distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Assam | 12 | For Acts on paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor Acts: the **District Council**, by public notification; for other Acts: the **Governor** | The **Governor** | Council itself controls application in its own legislative field |
| Meghalaya | 12A | No council switch; instead a **repugnancy rule** — State law prevails and the council law or regulation is void to the extent of repugnancy | The **President**, by notification | The only express repugnancy clause |
| Tripura | 12AA | For paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor Acts: the **District Council**; for other State Acts: the **Governor** | The **President** | Assam's council power plus Presidential control of central Acts |
| Mizoram | 12B | For paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor Acts: the **District Council**; for other State Acts: the **Governor** | The **President** | Same shape as Tripura |

Caption: there is no single rule. Assam concentrates the direction power in the Governor for Acts outside the council's field; Meghalaya replaces the council switch with a repugnancy rule; Tripura and Mizoram split the function between Council, Governor and President.

### 1. Why this paragraph family exists

A council that can legislate on land, forests, inheritance and custom will sooner or later collide with a State Act or an Act of Parliament on the same ground. Paragraphs 12 to 12B decide who resolves that collision, and they do it differently in each State because each State's arrangement was negotiated at a different time.

The practical answer-writing rule follows directly: **never write "Central and State laws do not apply in Sixth Schedule areas unless the council agrees."** That sentence is true of nothing. The correct formulation identifies the State, the type of Act and the subject.

### 2. Reading paragraph 12 (Assam) closely

Clause (a): no Act of the Assam Legislature on a paragraph 3 subject, and no Act of that Legislature prohibiting or restricting the consumption of any **non-distilled alcoholic liquor**, applies to an autonomous district or region unless the District Council for that district, or having jurisdiction over that region, so directs by public notification. In giving the direction, the Council may direct that the Act applies subject to such exceptions or modifications as it thinks fit.

Clause (b): for any other Act of Parliament or of the Assam Legislature, the **Governor** may by public notification direct non-application, or application with exceptions or modifications.

Clause (2): any such direction may be given retrospective effect.

Two observations. The council's power under clause (a) is a genuine **legislative-entry control** over its own field — it is one of the strongest devolved powers in the Constitution. And the non-distilled liquor carve-out is not quaint: regulation of local brews touches custom, ritual, livelihood and health simultaneously, which is why it was given to the council rather than left with the State.

### 3. Meghalaya's distinctive repugnancy rule

Paragraph 12A does something the other three do not. It says that where a provision of a council or Regional Council law on a paragraph 3(1) subject, or of a regulation made under paragraph 8 or paragraph 10, is repugnant to a provision of a law made by the Meghalaya Legislature on that matter, then the council law or regulation — **whether made before or after the State law** — is void to the extent of the repugnancy, and the State law prevails.

That is the reverse of the Assam arrangement. In Assam the council decides whether a State Act in its field enters; in Meghalaya the State law overrides the council's law where they conflict. Paragraph 12A(b) separately lets the **President** direct non-application or modified application of an Act of Parliament, with retrospective effect if specified.

### 4. The brakes: paragraphs 14, 15 and 16

```text
PARA 14  INQUIRY
  Governor may at any time appoint a Commission to examine and report on
  any specified matter relating to the administration of autonomous districts
  and regions, including the paragraph 1(3)(c)-(f) reorganisation matters,
  or generally on administration, and in particular on
    (a) educational and medical facilities and communications
    (b) the need for new or special legislation
    (c) administration of laws, rules and regulations made by the Councils
  Report + Governor's recommendations are LAID BEFORE THE STATE LEGISLATURE
  with an explanatory memorandum on proposed action
  ASSAM (1995): the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with
  respect thereto" are OMITTED -- the REPORT alone goes to the Legislature
  with the explanatory memorandum
  Governor may place one Minister specially in charge of the welfare of
  autonomous districts and regions
        |
PARA 15  ANNULMENT OR SUSPENSION
  Trigger: an act or resolution of a Council is LIKELY TO ENDANGER THE SAFETY
  OF INDIA or LIKELY TO BE PREJUDICIAL TO PUBLIC ORDER
  Governor may annul or suspend it, and may suspend the Council and assume
  its powers to prevent the act or the giving of effect to the resolution
  Order + reasons laid before the State Legislature as soon as possible
  ASSAM and MEGHALAYA: continues 12 months unless revoked by the STATE
  LEGISLATURE; the proviso allows renewal by a resolution of that Legislature
  for further 12-month periods unless cancelled by the Governor
  TRIPURA and MIZORAM (1988): "by the Legislature of the State" is replaced
  by "BY HIM", so the order continues 12 months unless revoked by the
  GOVERNOR, and the renewal PROVISO IS OMITTED -- no extension beyond
  the twelve months
        |
PARA 16  DISSOLUTION AND ASSUMPTION
  16(1) On the recommendation of a paragraph 14 Commission, the Governor may
        by public notification dissolve a Council and
          (a) order an immediate fresh general election, OR
          (b) with the PREVIOUS APPROVAL of the State Legislature, assume the
              administration himself or place it under the Commission or another
              suitable body for NOT MORE THAN TWELVE MONTHS
        Provisos: (b) may be used pending reconstitution after an (a) order; and
        no action under (b) without giving the Council an opportunity to place
        its views before the State Legislature
  16(2) If the Governor is satisfied that administration cannot be carried on
        in accordance with the Schedule, he may by public notification assume
        the Council's functions for NOT MORE THAN SIX MONTHS, extendable by
        further orders of up to six months each
  16(3) Such an order with reasons is laid before the State Legislature and
        CEASES at the expiration of THIRTY DAYS from the date the Legislature
        first sits after it is issued, unless approved before that expiry

  BUT paragraph 16 IS NOT UNIFORM ACROSS THE FOUR STATES. For TRIPURA and
  MIZORAM the 1988 amendment of the Schedule provides that --
    in 16(1)(b)  the words "subject to the previous approval of the
                 Legislature of the State" are OMITTED
    the SECOND PROVISO (opportunity to the Council to place its views
                 before the State Legislature) is OMITTED
    16(3) is SUBSTITUTED by a bare LAYING requirement covering orders
                 under BOTH 16(1) and 16(2): no thirty-day lapse,
                 no approval condition
```

Three features of this architecture deserve emphasis, and State-specific qualifications then cut across all three.

- **Evidence first.** Dissolution under 16(1) requires a Commission recommendation; reorganisation under paragraph 1(3) requires consideration of a Commission report. The Schedule repeatedly refuses to let territorial or institutional disruption proceed on bare satisfaction.
- **Legislature, not Parliament.** The accountability forum for paragraphs 14, 15 and 16 is the **State Legislature** — reports laid before it, approval for assumption under 16(1)(b), and the thirty-day approval rule under 16(3). The Union is largely absent from this circuit, which is a real structural contrast with the Fifth Schedule. Qualify that at once for Tripura and Mizoram, where the 1988 amendment moved two of those decisions from the Legislature to the Governor.
- **Time limits everywhere.** Twelve months under paragraph 15, twelve months under 16(1)(b), six months renewable under 16(2), and under 16(3) thirty days counted from the date the State Legislature first sits after the order is issued. Read that last limit carefully: it is thirty calendar days running from the first sitting, not thirty days on which the House actually sits. Emergency powers over an autonomous institution are bounded by clock as well as by procedure.

✅ **One more place where Assam is different, and it is inside paragraph 14 itself.** Paragraph 14(2) ordinarily requires that "the report of every such Commission **with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto**" be laid before the State Legislature by the Minister concerned, together with an explanatory memorandum on the action proposed. In its application to **Assam**, the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1995 omitted the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto". In Assam, therefore, what the Legislature receives is the **Commission's report and the Government's explanatory memorandum**; the Governor's own recommendations are no longer a required part of the laying.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** Set that beside the 1995 Act's other Assam work and a pattern appears. The same amendment that enlarged the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils' legislative field also thinned the Governor's personal footprint in the reporting chain. It is a small drafting change with a real consequence for answer-writing: a question about "the Governor's role in Sixth Schedule supervision" has a State-specific answer here too, and the safe formulation names the State before it names the duty.

#### The 1988 variation for Tripura and Mizoram: paragraphs 9, 10, 15 and 16

Nearly all of what the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1988 did, it did **in the Schedule's application to Tripura and Mizoram** rather than to the Schedule at large, and on that footing it touched four paragraphs. Two of them — 15 and 16 — are supervision clauses, and both move in the same direction. One change in that Act carries no application limit at all: it substituted serial numbers 2 and 3, and the entries relating to them, in Part III of the paragraph 20 table, which is where the **Mara** and **Lai** District names come from. Keep that one apart; the four below are the State-specific variations.

✅ **Paragraph 15, as amended for Tripura and Mizoram.** Two changes. In the opening portion the words "by the Legislature of the State" are replaced by the words "**by him**", so an annulment or suspension order continues in force for twelve months unless revoked by the **Governor** rather than by the State Legislature. And the **proviso is omitted** — the proviso that elsewhere lets the State Legislature, by resolution, keep the order alive for further twelve-month periods unless the Governor cancels it. The duty to lay the order and its reasons before the Legislature survives; the Legislature's power to end the order, and its power to prolong it, do not.

✅ **Paragraph 16, as amended for Tripura and Mizoram.** The words "subject to the previous approval of the Legislature of the State" in clause (b) of sub-paragraph (1) are **omitted**; the **second proviso** to sub-paragraph (1) — which elsewhere requires that the Council be given an opportunity of placing its views before the State Legislature — is **omitted**; and sub-paragraph (3) is **substituted** by a single sentence: every order made under sub-paragraph (1) **or** sub-paragraph (2), along with the reasons, shall be **laid before the Legislature of the State**.

The other two are not supervision clauses and run the other way, which is worth noticing.

✅ **Paragraph 9, as amended for Tripura and Mizoram.** A new sub-paragraph (3) lets the Governor by order direct that the royalty share due to a District Council be made over **within one year** of the agreement under 9(1) or the determination under 9(2). That is a protection for the council, not a restriction on it.

✅ **Paragraph 10, as amended for Tripura and Mizoram.** The words "by non-tribals" are omitted from the heading; the words "other than Scheduled Tribes" are omitted from sub-paragraph (1); and clause (d) of sub-paragraph (2) is replaced by "prescribe that **no person resident in the district** shall carry on any trade, whether wholesale or retail, except under a licence issued in that behalf by the District Council". The licensing reach therefore extends to every resident trader, not only to non-tribals. This too enlarges the council's regulatory power.

The consequences of the two supervision changes are exact, and they run in one direction.

| | Assam and Meghalaya | Tripura and Mizoram |
|---|---|---|
| 15: who may revoke an annulment or suspension order | The **State Legislature** | The **Governor** ("by him") |
| 15: renewal beyond twelve months | Proviso allows renewal by resolution of the Legislature, unless cancelled by the Governor | **Proviso omitted** — no renewal route |
| 16(1)(b) assumption of administration | Requires the **previous approval** of the State Legislature | **No previous approval** required |
| Council's right to be heard before 16(1)(b) action | Second proviso guarantees an opportunity to place its views before the State Legislature | **Omitted** |
| 16(2) order of assumption | Laid before the Legislature; **ceases after thirty days** from its first sitting unless approved | Laid before the Legislature; **no lapse, no approval condition** |
| 16(1) order of dissolution | No express laying requirement in 16(3), which speaks only of 16(2) orders | **Expressly laid**, along with reasons |

- **The qualification.** Do not state paragraphs 15 and 16 as uniform four-State rules. For Assam and Meghalaya the State Legislature is a genuine gatekeeper — it approves in advance under 16(1)(b), it can let a 16(2) order die by inaction, and under paragraph 15 it both revokes and renews. For Tripura and Mizoram the Governor acts first and the Legislature is **informed**, not asked; under paragraph 15 the Governor holds the revocation power himself, and the order simply lapses at twelve months because the renewal proviso is gone.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** This is the Schedule doing what it does everywhere else in Lesson 12 — refusing a single national rule and legislating State by State. The same instinct that produced four different application-of-laws regimes under paragraphs 12 to 12B produced two different supervision regimes here. Notice also that the same 1988 Act **widened** council power in paragraphs 9 and 10 while **narrowing** the legislative check in 15 and 16: a single amendment can move autonomy and accountability in opposite directions at once. When a question asks whether the Sixth Schedule is "one system", this is strong evidence that it is a family of systems sharing a chassis.

#### Where the Governor acts in his discretion: paragraphs 20BA and 20BB

One question follows naturally from everything above. When the Governor dissolves a Council, assumes its administration, annuls a resolution or assents to a council law, is he acting on the advice of his Council of Ministers, or on his own judgment? The Schedule answers it — but, again, State by State, in two short paragraphs that sit near the end and are easy to walk past.

✅ **Paragraph 20BA, inserted in its application to Assam by the 1995 Act.** In discharging his functions under a specified list of provisions — which includes paragraphs 1(2) and 1(3), 2(1), 2(6), 2(6A) other than its first proviso and 2(7), 3(3), 4(4), 5, 6(1), 7(2), 8(4), 10(3), 14(1), 15(1), and 16(1) and 16(2) — the Governor "shall, **after consulting the Council of Ministers and the North Cachar Hills Autonomous Council or the Karbi Anglong Autonomous Council**, as the case may be, take such action as he considers necessary **in his discretion**."

✅ **Paragraph 20BB, inserted in its application to Tripura and Mizoram by the 1988 Act.** In discharging his functions under a shorter list — paragraphs 1(2) and 1(3), 2(1) and 2(7), 3(3), 4(4), 5, 6(1), 7(2), 9(3), 14(1), 15(1), and 16(1) and 16(2) — the Governor "shall, **after consulting the Council of Ministers, and if he thinks it necessary, the District Council or the Regional Council concerned**, take such action as he considers necessary **in his discretion**."

What differs between them, and what does not, is worth isolating.

| | Assam, paragraph 20BA (1995) | Tripura and Mizoram, paragraph 20BB (1988) |
|---|---|---|
| Consultation of the Council of Ministers | Mandatory | Mandatory |
| Consultation of the autonomous council | **Mandatory** — the named Autonomous Council must be consulted | **Optional** — only "if he thinks it necessary" |
| Fiscal and regulatory assents inside the list | Includes 8(4) tax regulations and 10(3) money-lending and trading regulations | Does **not** include them |
| Council-constitution functions inside the list | Includes 2(6) first-constitution rules and 2(6A) term rules | Does **not** include them |
| Who finally decides | The Governor, in his discretion | The Governor, in his discretion |

✅ There is **no equivalent paragraph for Meghalaya**. The Schedule confers this textual discretion only in its application to Assam and to Tripura and Mizoram.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, and the discipline it imposes.** These two paragraphs settle less than they appear to, and more than most answers admit. They make the discretion **textual** rather than inferred, so it is safe to say that the Governor acts in his own discretion *in the listed functions in those three States* — and unsafe to convert that into unqualified personal discretion across the Schedule or across all four States, which is the standard overstatement. They also make consultation a **procedural** duty, not a consent requirement: a council that has been consulted has been heard, not obeyed. And the Assam text goes one step further than the Tripura and Mizoram text by making the council's own voice compulsory rather than optional, which is the sharpest textual reply available to the complaint that these councils are consulted only when it suits the Governor.

### 5. Paragraph 19: the condition the Schedule starts from

Everything taught so far assumes a District Council exists. Paragraph 19 answers the prior question: what governs an autonomous district **before** its Council has been constituted? The answer is startling if you have only read paragraphs 2 to 13, and it explains several features that otherwise look arbitrary.

```text
PARAGRAPH 19  TRANSITIONAL PROVISIONS
19(1)  "As soon as possible after the commencement of this Constitution"
       the GOVERNOR shall take steps to constitute a District Council
       for each autonomous district
         |
       UNTIL that Council is constituted:
         administration of the district VESTS IN THE GOVERNOR, and
         paragraph 19 applies INSTEAD OF the foregoing provisions
         |
         +-- 19(1)(a)  NO Act of Parliament and NO Act of the State
         |             Legislature applies to such an area unless the
         |             GOVERNOR so directs by PUBLIC NOTIFICATION;
         |             he may direct application subject to such
         |             exceptions or modifications as he thinks fit
         |
         +-- 19(1)(b)  The GOVERNOR may make REGULATIONS for the
                       PEACE AND GOOD GOVERNMENT of any such area, and
                       such regulations MAY REPEAL OR AMEND any Act of
                       Parliament, any Act of the State Legislature or
                       any existing law applicable to the area
         |
19(2)  A direction under 19(1)(a) may be given RETROSPECTIVE effect
19(3)  All regulations under 19(1)(b) are submitted FORTHWITH to the
       PRESIDENT and, until assented to by him, HAVE NO EFFECT

19(4)  [inserted 2003] INTERIM EXECUTIVE COUNCIL for the BODOLAND
       TERRITORIAL AREAS DISTRICT, formed by the GOVERNOR from
       leaders of the Bodo movement including signatories to the
       Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003, with adequate
       representation for NON-TRIBAL communities of the area;
       proviso: the Interim Council shall be for SIX MONTHS,
       during which ENDEAVOUR to hold the election to the
       Council shall be made
```

Read that beside Lesson 6, and then read it carefully, because the resemblance to Fifth Schedule paragraph 5 is real but **partial**, and the difference is the examinable part.

| Feature | Fifth Schedule, paragraph 5 | Sixth Schedule, paragraph 19(1) |
|---|---|---|
| Default position of a law | Acts **apply**; the Governor may direct that a particular Act shall **not** apply, or apply with exceptions or modifications | **No** Act of Parliament or of the State Legislature applies **unless** the Governor so directs |
| Direction operates as | A switch **off** | A switch **on** |
| Regulation power | Peace and good government of the area. Paragraph 5(2) then lists land transfer, land allotment and money-lending "in particular and without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing power" — illustrations, not a closed list | Peace and good government of the area, with no illustrative list at all |
| May repeal or amend an Act | Yes, under 5(3) | Yes, under 19(1)(b) |
| Retrospective effect | Expressly permitted for a notification under 5(1) | Expressly permitted for a direction under 19(1)(a), by 19(2) |
| Presidential assent | Required by 5(4) before a regulation has effect | Required by 19(3) before a regulation has effect |
| Tribes Advisory Council | 5(5) requires consultation where a Council exists | **No consultation requirement at all** |
| Duration | A standing feature of Fifth Schedule administration | **Transitional**, displaced once a District Council is constituted |

Caption: the two paragraphs share devices and reverse the default. Memorising them as "the same clause in two Schedules" is exactly how a question is lost.

Three consequences follow, and each is worth carrying into an answer.

- **The Sixth Schedule's default is gubernatorial, not democratic.** Elected self-government is what the Schedule *builds towards*; administration by the Governor under a protective regulation power is where it *starts*. The autonomy is constituted, not presumed.
- **Paragraph 19(3) is a President clause, but it is not the origin of the President's role in this Schedule.** It is easy to read it that way, and wrong. Paragraph 19(3) uses the President because, at that stage, the regulation-maker is the **Governor himself** — exactly as under Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(4), where the Governor's regulation also goes to the President. Supervisory assent follows the maker. Independently of paragraph 19, the President already appears in paragraph 4(5) for the date that recasts the justice system, in paragraph 7(3) for approving the form of council accounts, in paragraphs 12A(b), 12AA(c) and 12B(c) for the application of Acts of Parliament in Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram, and — most importantly for ordinary council legislation — in paragraphs **3A(2) and (3)** and **3B(2) and (3)** for Assam, where a council law relating to a Concurrent List matter must be reserved by the Governor for the President's consideration. The accurate sentence is that the Governor is the Schedule's working supervisor and the President appears at several specified structural points, one of which reaches ordinary council law-making in Assam.
- **Constituting the Council changes who holds the switch, not whether there is one.** Under 19(1)(a) the Governor alone decides what law applies. Once a Council exists, paragraph 12 and its State-specific variants redistribute that decision: in Assam the **Council** directs on paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor while the **Governor** directs on everything else; in Tripura and Mizoram the Council holds the same paragraph 3 switch, with the Governor for other State Acts and the **President** for Acts of Parliament; Meghalaya alone replaces the switch with a repugnancy rule, keeping the President for Acts of Parliament. Be precise about this. It is **not** true that once a Council is constituted laws simply apply unless someone directs otherwise — under paragraphs 12(a), 12AA and 12B, a State Act in the council field still does not apply to the district unless the competent authority so directs. The opt-in survives; the opt-in *holder* changes.

#### Paragraph 19(4): the Bodoland interim arrangement

The Sixth Schedule (Amendment) Act, 2003, which created the Bodoland Territorial Areas District, also added a fourth sub-paragraph to paragraph 19 — a second, much later use of the same transitional idea.

✅ Under paragraph 19(4), as soon as possible after the commencement of that Act an **Interim Executive Council** for the Bodoland Territorial Areas District in Assam was to be formed **by the Governor** from amongst **leaders of the Bodo movement, including the signatories to the Memorandum of Settlement**, and was to provide **adequate representation to the non-tribal communities** of that area. The proviso fixes its life: the Interim Council "shall be for a period of **six months** during which **endeavour to hold the election** to the Council shall be made." Read that proviso exactly as it stands. It states a period and an obligation of effort; it does not direct that the election be completed within the six months, and it does not say the Interim Council ends on the election. The Explanation defines "Memorandum of Settlement" as the Memorandum signed on **10 February 2003** between the Government of India, the Government of Assam and the Bodo Liberation Tigers.

Three points make this more than a footnote. It confirms that paragraph 19 is the Schedule's **general transitional technique**, not a 1950-only provision. It shows the Constitution absorbing a peace settlement directly into the Schedule, with the accord named in the text. And it is the one place in the Schedule where a nominated, non-elected body is given an express constitutional life, which is precisely why the text bounded it by a period and pointed it at an election.

⚠️ **A bounded reading, offered as such.** Paragraph 19(1) is phrased around the commencement of the Constitution — "as soon as possible after the commencement of this Constitution" — and 19(4) was drafted as its own dated provision for Bodoland. The text does not state a general rule that paragraph 19(1) revives automatically whenever a new autonomous district is created under paragraph 1(3)(c). The safer proposition, and the one the 2003 amendment supports, is that when Parliament creates a new unit it **supplies the interim arrangement expressly**, as it did for Bodoland. Do not assert automatic revival.

⚠️ **A second inference.** Paragraph 19 also exposes the inheritance traced in Lesson 1. Sections 91 and 92 of the Government of India Act, 1935 used the same two devices — no law applies unless the Governor notifies, and the Governor may make regulations for peace and good government. The Constitution kept the machinery for a transitional moment and placed an elected Council at the end of it. The continuity of form with a change of purpose is visible in a single paragraph.

### 6. Three more paragraphs that answer specific questions

**Paragraph 17 — constituencies.** For elections to the Legislative Assembly of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura or Mizoram, the Governor may by order declare that an area within an autonomous district shall not form part of a constituency filling a seat reserved for that district, but shall instead form part of a specified unreserved constituency. This is the Schedule's own tool for handling non-tribal pockets inside autonomous districts in Assembly delimitation. ✅ One exclusion was added in 2003: a proviso inserted for Assam provides that **nothing in paragraph 17 shall apply to the Bodoland Territorial Areas District**. The tool exists for nine of the ten listed tribal areas, not for all ten.

**Paragraph 20C — the Union territory adaptation.** The Schedule contains an interpretation clause under which, in its application to the **Union territory of Mizoram**, references to the Governor and Government of the State are read as references to the **Administrator appointed under Article 239**, references to the State are read as references to that Union territory, and references to the State Legislature as references to its Legislative Assembly, with three consequential modifications to paragraphs 4(5), 6(2) and 13. That Union territory was itself named in Article 244(2) between 1972 and 1987, so paragraph 20C was solving a drafting problem — how machinery written for a State reads when the unit is a Union territory — not a question of reach. Mizoram has long since become a State, so the clause is historical in operation; it remains constitutionally instructive, because it shows that the Sixth Schedule's machinery has been drafted, at least once, to work in a Union territory. Lesson 20 uses this point.

**Paragraph 21 — amendment.** Parliament may from time to time by law amend the Sixth Schedule by addition, variation or repeal, and no such law shall be deemed to be an amendment of the Constitution for the purposes of Article 368. This is the clause under which the 1988, 1995 and 2003 amendments were enacted. Note its boundary, which Lesson 20 relies on: paragraph 21's object is **this Schedule**. It does not reach **Article 244(2)**, which names the units to whose tribal areas the Schedule applies. That clause lies in Part X of the Constitution, and altering it is a different exercise with its own history — Lesson 20 sets it out.

### 7. Where the President actually appears in the Sixth Schedule

| Function | Primary actor |
|---|---|
| Assent to an ordinary council law under paragraph 3(3), in Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram | **Governor** |
| Assent to an ordinary council law in **Assam** where the law does **not** relate to a Concurrent List matter | **Governor**, under paragraph 3(3) as substituted |
| A council law of the Karbi Anglong or North Cachar Hills Council, under paragraph 3 or paragraph 3A, **relating to a List III matter** | Governor **shall reserve** it; **President** assents, withholds assent, or returns it with a message — paragraph 3A(2) and 3A(3) |
| A law of the Bodoland Territorial Council, under paragraph 3 or paragraph 3B, **relating to a List III matter** | Governor **shall reserve** it; **President** assents, withholds assent, or returns it with a message — paragraph 3B(2) and 3B(3) |
| Assent to council taxation regulations under paragraph 8(4) and money-lending or trading regulations under paragraph 10(3) | **Governor** |
| Previous approval for paragraph 4(4) and paragraph 6(1) rules and regulations | **Governor** |
| Non-application or modification of an Act of Parliament in Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram | **President** |
| Appointing the date under paragraph 4(5) to recast the justice system, after consulting the State Government | **President** |
| Assent to the Governor's transitional regulations under paragraph 19(3), before a District Council is constituted | **President** |
| Approving the form in which the CAG prescribes council accounts under paragraph 7(3) | **President** |
| Grants under Article 275(1) | Union and Parliament |
| Amendment of the Schedule under paragraph 21 | Parliament, by ordinary law |
| Routine approval of council decisions | **Not the President** |

The answer-writing rule is short, and it now has a rider: in the Sixth Schedule the Governor is the working supervisor and Parliament holds the amending power, while the President appears at specified structural points — including, in Assam alone, the Concurrent-List slice of ordinary council legislation.

### UPSC application

- Never generalise the application-of-laws rule; name the State and the paragraph.
- Use Assam's paragraph 12(a) council-notification power as the strongest single illustration of devolved authority in this Schedule.
- Use Meghalaya's paragraph 12A repugnancy rule as the counter-illustration.
- When asked whether councils are protected from arbitrary action, cite the Commission requirement, the State Legislature's role and the express time limits — then qualify at once with the 1988 Tripura and Mizoram variation of paragraphs 15 and 16, which is what separates a careful answer from a textbook one.
- Keep paragraph 15 and paragraph 16 apart: annulment or suspension of an act or resolution, versus dissolution or assumption of administration.
- Remember that the 2003 amendment carved the Bodoland Territorial Areas District out of four provisions — paragraph 1(2) autonomous regions, paragraph 4 justice, paragraph 10 money-lending and trading, and paragraph 17 constituencies.
- Use paragraph 19 to explain the baseline: before a District Council exists the Governor administers the district, no Act applies unless he notifies, and his peace-and-good-government regulations need the President's assent. Constituting the Council does not make laws apply automatically; it transfers the switch to the authority named for that State in paragraphs 12 to 12B.
- Use paragraph 19(4) when a question asks how the Constitution absorbed the Bodo settlement: a named accord, a nominated interim body, a stated six-month period and a duty to endeavour to hold the election, all inside the Schedule.

### Revision notes

1. Paragraph 12 (Assam): for paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor Acts of the State Legislature, the District Council directs application by public notification, with exceptions or modifications.
2. Paragraph 12(b) and 12(2): for other Acts of Parliament or the Assam Legislature the Governor directs, and such directions may be retrospective.
3. Paragraph 12A (Meghalaya): council law or paragraph 8 or 10 regulation is void to the extent of repugnancy with a State law; the State law prevails.
4. Paragraph 12A(b): the President may direct non-application or modified application of an Act of Parliament.
5. Paragraphs 12AA (Tripura) and 12B (Mizoram): Council for paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor; Governor for other State Acts; President for Acts of Parliament.
6. Paragraph 14: the Governor may appoint a Commission; its report and his recommendations are laid before the State Legislature with an explanatory memorandum, and he may place a Minister specially in charge of autonomous districts and regions. **Not uniform:** in its application to **Assam** the 1995 Act omitted the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto" from 14(2), so there the report and the memorandum are laid without those recommendations. Paragraphs **20BA** (Assam, 1995) and **20BB** (Tripura and Mizoram, 1988) then state that in listed functions the Governor acts **in his discretion** after consulting the Council of Ministers — compulsorily consulting the named Autonomous Council in Assam, optionally consulting the District or Regional Council in Tripura and Mizoram. There is no equivalent for Meghalaya.
7. Paragraph 15: annulment or suspension where an act or resolution is likely to endanger the safety of India or be prejudicial to public order; order and reasons before the State Legislature; twelve-month duration. **Not uniform:** in Assam and Meghalaya the order runs unless revoked by the State Legislature and the proviso allows renewal by its resolution, while as amended for **Tripura and Mizoram** in 1988 "by the Legislature of the State" became "by him", so the **Governor** revokes, and the renewal proviso was omitted altogether.
8. Paragraph 16(1), as it stands for **Assam and Meghalaya**: dissolution on a Commission's recommendation, with fresh elections or, with the State Legislature's previous approval, assumption of administration for not more than twelve months; the second proviso requires that the Council be given an opportunity to place its views before that Legislature.
9. Paragraph 16(2) and 16(3), again for **Assam and Meghalaya**: assumption of a Council's functions for not more than six months, extendable by six months at a time; the order with reasons is laid before the State Legislature and ceases thirty days from the date it first sits thereafter, unless approved before that expiry.
10. **Paragraph 16 is not uniform.** As amended in its application to **Tripura and Mizoram** by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1988: the words "subject to the previous approval of the Legislature of the State" in 16(1)(b) are omitted; the second proviso is omitted; and 16(3) is substituted by a requirement that every order under 16(1) **or** 16(2), with reasons, be laid before the State Legislature — no thirty-day lapse and no approval condition.
11. Paragraph 19(1) to 19(3): until a District Council is constituted the district is administered by the Governor; no Act of Parliament or of the State Legislature applies unless he directs by public notification, with retrospective effect if he chooses; his regulations for peace and good government may repeal or amend any applicable law and have no effect until the President assents. The default is the **mirror image** of Fifth Schedule paragraph 5, which switches laws off rather than on, and paragraph 19 carries no Tribes Advisory Council consultation requirement. Constituting the Council does not make laws apply automatically; it moves the switch to the authority named for that State in paragraphs 12 to 12B.
12. Paragraph 19(4), inserted in 2003: an Interim Executive Council for the Bodoland Territorial Areas District formed by the Governor from leaders of the Bodo movement, including signatories to the Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003, with adequate representation for non-tribal communities. Its proviso states that the Interim Council shall be for a period of six months during which endeavour to hold the election to the Council shall be made — a stated period and a duty of effort, not a completed-election deadline.
13. Paragraph 17: the Governor may exclude an area within an autonomous district from a reserved constituency for Assembly elections — but a proviso inserted in 2003 provides that paragraph 17 does **not** apply to the Bodoland Territorial Areas District. Paragraph 20C adapts the Schedule to the Union territory of Mizoram by reading Governor as Article 239 Administrator.
14. Paragraph 21: Parliament may amend the Sixth Schedule by ordinary law and such a law is not an Article 368 amendment — but its object is the Schedule, not Article 244(2), which names the units to whose tribal areas the Schedule applies. Article 244(2) has been altered both by constitutional amendment and, where the change was consequential on a reorganisation, by ordinary law under Articles 3 and 4. Lesson 20 works this through.
15. The President is not the routine assent authority for council laws — but he is not absent from them either. In **Assam**, paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) require the Governor to reserve for the President's consideration any council law relating to a Concurrent List matter, and 3A(3) and 3B(3) give the President power to assent, to withhold assent, or to return the law with a message for reconsideration within six months and re-presentation.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State Legislature passes an Act regulating inheritance among Scheduled Tribes. Does it apply inside an autonomous district in Assam, and would the answer differ in Meghalaya?

**Model answer:** Inheritance is a paragraph 3(1)(h) subject. In **Assam**, paragraph 12(a) provides that no Act of the State Legislature on a paragraph 3 matter applies to an autonomous district or region unless the District Council so directs by public notification, and the Council may direct application with exceptions or modifications. In **Meghalaya** the position is reversed: paragraph 12A(a) makes a council law or specified regulation void to the extent it is repugnant to a State law on that matter, whether the council law was made before or after, and the State law prevails. So the same Act faces a council gate in Assam and a repugnancy rule in Meghalaya.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming a single pan-Schedule rule on the application of laws. The Schedule deliberately uses four State-specific paragraphs.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "The Sixth Schedule protects autonomy with procedure rather than with immunity." Examine.

#### Model solution

The Schedule nowhere makes a council immune. It surrounds every intrusive power with procedure, evidence and time.

Paragraph 1(3) lets the Governor create, enlarge, diminish or unite autonomous districts, but its first proviso bars orders under clauses (c) to (f) without a paragraph 14(1) Commission report. Dissolution under 16(1) needs a Commission recommendation. In Assam and Meghalaya, assumption under 16(1)(b) needs the State Legislature's previous approval, is capped at twelve months, and the second proviso guarantees the Council a hearing. 16(2) assumption is capped at six months, extendable; 16(3) makes such an order lapse thirty days after the Legislature first sits unless approved.

Uniformity is apparent only. For Tripura and Mizoram the 1988 amendment omitted the previous-approval requirement and the second proviso, and replaced 16(3) with a bare laying duty — procedure thinned into reporting.

Paragraph 15 allows annulment or suspension only where an act or resolution is likely to endanger the safety of India or be prejudicial to public order, requires the order and reasons to be laid before the Legislature, and runs twelve months. Here too 1988 varied the rule: for those States the Governor, not the Legislature, revokes, and the renewal proviso was omitted. Paragraph 14 requires inquiry reports and a memorandum to be laid.

Protection is procedural, not absolute, and the forum is the State Legislature, not Parliament. It works only where that Legislature is attentive, and 1988 shows how readily the check can be lightened. Its strength is that no such power is exercised silently.

**Rubric (15):** paragraph 1(3) proviso and Commission requirement 2; paragraph 15 trigger, forum and duration 2; paragraph 16(1) and 16(2) conditions and caps 4; the 1988 Tripura and Mizoram variation of paragraphs 15 and 16 4; paragraph 14 reporting 1; evaluative conclusion on procedure versus immunity 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Both Schedules are now fully taught. Only at this point is a comparison honest — and it must be extended to the two systems that most often get confused with them.

---

## Lesson 13: Fifth versus Sixth, and the Four Systems That Must Not Be Collapsed

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The exact Fifth-Sixth comparison, Article 243M and 243ZC exclusions, and the distinction from Part IX Panchayats, PESA and the Article 371 family

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity comparison tables for the two Schedules, extended with Article 243M and the Article 371 family because textbook comparisons stop at "advisory versus self-governing"
CA search: "judicial or official clarification distinguishing Fifth Schedule, Sixth Schedule, PESA and Article 371 arrangements in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No new authoritative clarification was located; the distinctions below rest on the constitutional text and the statutes themselves
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the master comparison, axis by axis

| Dimension | Fifth Schedule | Sixth Schedule |
|---|---|---|
| Enabling Article | 244(1) | 244(2), with the Schedule also referenced to 275(1) |
| Territory | Notified Scheduled Areas in ten States | Tribal areas listed in the paragraph 20 table, in four States |
| Who makes the territory | President declares and alters under paragraph 6 | Constitution lists; Governor organises and reorganises under paragraph 1(3) |
| Governing theory | Protective supervision | Elected territorial self-government |
| Principal representative body | Tribes Advisory Council, up to 20, advisory | District and Regional Councils, up to 30, elected plus nominated; the Bodoland Territorial Council not more than 46 |
| Law-making | Governor filters Acts under 5(1) and makes regulations under 5(2) | Councils legislate on the ten paragraph 3 heads, plus 3A and 3B enlargements |
| Assent | **President**, under paragraph 5(4) | **Governor**, under paragraph 3(3) — except in Assam, where a council law relating to a List III matter is reserved for the **President** under paragraph 3A(2) or 3B(2) |
| Executive administration | State's executive power continues under paragraph 2; Union directions under paragraph 3 | State authority continues, but councils manage listed services under paragraph 6 |
| Courts | Ordinary judicial system; special laws may operate | Village councils and courts under paragraph 4 for cases where all parties are Scheduled Tribes — paragraph 4 does not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council |
| Taxation | No taxation power in the council | Land revenue and specified taxes under paragraph 8 |
| Minerals | No Schedule-level royalty entitlement | Agreed royalty share under paragraph 9 |
| Supervision forum | President and Union | Governor and the State Legislature, under paragraphs 14 to 16 |
| Statutory local-government layer | PESA applies | PESA does not apply |
| Amendment of the Schedule | Parliament by ordinary law, paragraph 7 | Parliament by ordinary law, paragraph 21 |

```text
FIFTH  = AREA + PRESIDENT/GOVERNOR + ADVISORY COUNCIL + PESA
SIXTH  = DISTRICT/REGION + ELECTED COUNCIL + LAWS, COURTS, TAXES
```

Caption: compare on identical axes or the comparison becomes assertion. Note especially the two assent rows and the two supervision-forum rows.

### 1. Read the comparison as an argument, not a table

The two Schedules disagree about **where safeguarding power should sit**.

The Fifth Schedule locates it in constitutional offices outside the community: the President makes the territory, the Governor filters law and makes regulations, the Union directs, and the community's representatives advise. Its strength is that protection does not depend on winning a local election; its weakness is that nothing in the text compels the protective powers to be used.

The Sixth Schedule locates it in the community's own elected institution: the council legislates, administers, adjudicates and taxes. Its strength is that power is continuous and locally accountable; its weaknesses are that competence is subject-bounded, that output needs the Governor's assent, that capacity and funds limit what the form delivers, and that an elected council can itself marginalise minorities inside its district.

Neither model is simply better. The defensible verdict is comparative and conditional: the Sixth Schedule's **agency** should be imported into Fifth Schedule practice without pretending that either model is institutionally complete.

### 2. Article 243M: the exclusion that created PESA

Part IX gave India a constitutional Panchayat system in 1992. Article 243M then withheld it from this topic's territory.

- **243M(1):** nothing in Part IX applies to the Scheduled Areas referred to in Article 244(1) or the tribal areas referred to in Article 244(2).
- **243M(2):** nothing in Part IX applies to (a) the States of **Nagaland, Meghalaya and Mizoram**, or (b) the hill areas of **Manipur** for which District Councils exist under any law in force.
- **243M(3):** this clause has **two limbs**, and they do different work. Under **(a)**, nothing in Part IX relating to Panchayats at the **district level** applies to the hill areas of the **Darjeeling** district of West Bengal for which the Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council exists under any law in force. Under **(b)**, nothing in Part IX is to be **construed to affect the functions and powers** of that Council. Keep them apart: limb (a) switches off one tier of Part IX in a defined territory, while limb (b) protects an existing statutory council from being read down by anything else in the Part.
- **243M(3A):** inserted later, by the Constitution (Eighty-third Amendment) Act, 2000 — nothing in **Article 243D**, which reserves Panchayat seats for the Scheduled Castes, applies to the State of **Arunachal Pradesh**. Notice how much narrower this is than the clauses above it. Clauses (1), (2) and (3)(a) withhold Part IX, or one tier of it, from a territory or a State; clause (3A) withholds the operation of a **single article inside** Part IX, and leaves the rest of the Part running in Arunachal Pradesh. The clause sits outside this topic's protective machinery, but it completes the article and shows the same instinct at work — a uniform template is relaxed, by exactly as much as is needed, wherever it would misfit the population it meets.
- **243M(4):** notwithstanding anything in the Constitution, (a) the Legislature of a State named in 243M(2)(a) may extend Part IX to that State — except areas under 243M(1) — if its Legislative Assembly passes a resolution by a majority of the total membership **and** by not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting; and (b) **Parliament** may by law extend Part IX to the Scheduled Areas **and the tribal areas** referred to in 243M(1), subject to exceptions and modifications, and no such law shall be deemed an Article 368 amendment.

Three analytical points come out of 243M(4)(b), and they are genuinely high-value.

1. The enabling clause permits extension to **both** Scheduled Areas and tribal areas. Parliament used it in 1996 only for **Scheduled Areas**. The Sixth Schedule areas were left outside PESA as a matter of legislative choice, not constitutional compulsion.
2. The extension may be made "subject to such exceptions and modifications as may be specified." PESA's whole structure — habitation village, Gram Sabha powers, one-half ST reservation — is the content of that phrase.
3. Such a law is not an Article 368 amendment. The same ordinary-law device recurs in Fifth Schedule paragraph 7, Sixth Schedule paragraph 21 and Article 244A(4).

Article **243ZC** performs the parallel function for municipalities under Part IXA, keeping Scheduled Areas and tribal areas outside it and allowing Parliament to extend that Part with exceptions and modifications. No equivalent of PESA has been enacted for municipalities, which is a live gap as Scheduled Areas urbanise.

### 3. Four systems side by side

| System | Legal source | Territorial unit | Core institution | One-line distinction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ordinary Panchayats | Part IX, Articles 243 to 243O | Rural local area | Gram Sabha and three-tier Panchayats | Constitutional local government of general application |
| PESA | 1996 Act, applying modified Part IX under Article 243M(4)(b) | Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas | Habitation-based Gram Sabha and Panchayats | Statutory self-rule layered on protective administration |
| Sixth Schedule | Constitution, Sixth Schedule | Autonomous district or region in four States | District and Regional Councils | Constitutional territorial self-government |
| Article 371 family | Part XXI, Articles 371 to 371J | A named State or region within it | Varies: Governor's special responsibility, legislature committees, development boards, consent requirements | State-specific asymmetric provisions, not a council system |

### 4. Why the Article 371 family is not a Sixth Schedule substitute

Article **371A** protects Naga religious or social practices, customary law and procedure, administration of civil and criminal justice involving decisions according to Naga customary law, and ownership and transfer of land and its resources — by providing that an Act of Parliament on those matters does not apply to Nagaland unless the Legislative Assembly so decides by resolution. Article **371G** makes equivalent provision for Mizoram.

These are powerful consent-based shields. But they operate at **State level**, through the **State Legislative Assembly**, and they do not create an elected territorial council with legislative, judicial and fiscal powers. Mizoram proves the point by holding both: Article 371G protection at State level **and** three Sixth Schedule autonomous district councils for the Chakma, Lai and Mara areas.

A related caution: a hill council created by ordinary State statute — the Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council is the Constitution's own example, named in Article 243M(3) — is not a Sixth Schedule Autonomous District Council. Constitutional status follows the Schedule, not the label on the institution.

### 5. The confusion map

```text
"Scheduled Area"  --X--  "tribal area"
   Fifth Schedule         Sixth Schedule
   President declares     Constitution lists, Governor organises

"Tribes Advisory Council" --X-- "Autonomous District Council"
   advises on referred matters    legislates, judges, taxes

"PESA Gram Sabha"  --X--  "ordinary Gram Sabha"
   habitation-based, Fifth        Part IX, general application
   Schedule areas only

"Article 371A/371G shield"  --X--  "Sixth Schedule council"
   State Assembly consent             elected district government

"statutory hill council"  --X--  "constitutional ADC"
   ordinary State or central law     Sixth Schedule paragraph 2
```

### UPSC application

- Build every comparison on identical axes: territory, body, law-making, assent, executive, courts, finance, supervision.
- Quote Article 243M(1) for the exclusion and 243M(4)(b) for the enabling power; the pair answers most PESA-origin questions by itself.
- Use the "Parliament could have extended Part IX to tribal areas too" point for analytical depth.
- Use Mizoram as the standing counter-example to "Article 371 equals Sixth Schedule."
- Mention Article 243ZC when a question raises urbanisation in Scheduled Areas.

### Revision notes

1. Fifth Schedule: Article 244(1), ten States, Presidential territory, advisory council, Governor's regulations with Presidential assent, PESA applies.
2. Sixth Schedule: Article 244(2), four States, constitutionally listed territory organised by the Governor, elected councils, council laws with the Governor's assent — in Assam, Concurrent-List council laws reserved for the President — PESA does not apply.
3. Assent pairing: President for Fifth Schedule regulations; Governor for Sixth Schedule council laws.
4. Supervision forum: President and Union for the Fifth; Governor and State Legislature for the Sixth.
5. Both Schedules are amendable by ordinary parliamentary law — paragraphs 7 and 21.
6. Article 243M(1): Part IX does not apply to Scheduled Areas or tribal areas.
7. Article 243M(2): Part IX does not apply to Nagaland, Meghalaya and Mizoram, or to Manipur's hill areas with District Councils.
8. Article 243M(3) has two limbs: (a) district-level Panchayat provisions do not apply to Darjeeling's hill areas with the Gorkha Hill Council, and (b) nothing in Part IX is to be construed to affect that Council's functions and powers. Article 243M(3A), inserted in 2000, withholds only Article 243D's Scheduled Caste reservation, and only from Arunachal Pradesh.
9. Article 243M(4)(a): a named State's Assembly may extend Part IX by a total-membership majority and a two-thirds majority of those present and voting.
10. Article 243M(4)(b): Parliament may extend Part IX to Scheduled Areas **and** tribal areas, with exceptions and modifications; not an Article 368 amendment.
11. PESA used 243M(4)(b) only for Scheduled Areas — a legislative choice, not a constitutional bar.
12. Article 243ZC performs the parallel exclusion and enabling function for municipalities under Part IXA.
13. Article 371A and 371G: State Assembly consent shields over custom, justice and land, operating at State level.
14. Mizoram holds Article 371G protection and three Sixth Schedule councils simultaneously.
15. A statutory hill council is not a constitutional Autonomous District Council.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does PESA not apply to Sixth Schedule areas, and could it have?

**Model answer:** PESA was enacted under Article 243M(4)(b), which empowers Parliament to extend Part IX to **the Scheduled Areas and the tribal areas** referred to in Article 243M(1), subject to exceptions and modifications. Parliament exercised that power only in respect of Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas. So the non-application to Sixth Schedule areas is a **legislative choice**, not a constitutional prohibition — and it is a defensible one, because those areas already possess elected District and Regional Councils with legislative, executive, judicial and fiscal powers, so a second statutory local-government layer would have raised difficult questions of overlap.

**Misconception to avoid:** Saying "PESA cannot apply to Sixth Schedule areas." The enabling clause plainly covers them; Parliament simply did not extend it.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Compare the Fifth and Sixth Schedules as competing models of tribal governance, and state a reasoned verdict on which design better secures tribal interests.

#### Model solution

The Schedules answer one question with two institutional theories.

The Fifth Schedule locates safeguarding power outside the community. Under Article 244(1) the President declares Scheduled Areas in ten States under paragraph 6; the State continues to administer under paragraph 2; the Governor reports to the President and the Union directs under paragraph 3; a Tribes Advisory Council of not more than twenty members advises on referred matters; and the Governor may disapply or modify Acts under paragraph 5(1) and make peace-and-good-government regulations under 5(2), whose land, allotment and money-lending heads are illustrative, effective only on Presidential assent.

The Sixth Schedule locates it inside the community. Under Article 244(2), tribal areas in four States become autonomous districts; elected District and Regional Councils legislate on ten paragraph 3 heads, run village courts under paragraph 4 where all parties are Scheduled Tribes, manage listed services under paragraph 6, assess land revenue and levy taxes under paragraph 8, and receive a royalty share under paragraph 9.

Each model's weakness mirrors its strength. Fifth Schedule powers are strong but discretionary, and dormancy is the recurring complaint; PESA and the Forest Rights Act supply agency.
