Sixth Schedule councils are continuously accountable but subject-bounded, assent-dependent, capacity-constrained and capable of marginalising minorities.

The verdict should be conditional, not hierarchical: the Sixth Schedule's elected agency is the better long-run design where territory is compact and customary institutions are intact, but the decisive variable in both is whether records, finance, participation and remedies convert formal power into usable power.

**Rubric (20):** identical-axis comparison 5; accurate Fifth Schedule institutional content 4; accurate Sixth Schedule institutional content 4; weaknesses of each model 3; conditional verdict with a stated decisive variable 3; precision on assent and territory 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

The Fifth Schedule's missing democratic institution was supplied by statute in 1996. The next two lessons teach PESA — first its foundations, then its exact power code.

---

## Lesson 14: PESA: Why It Was Enacted, the Habitation Village and the Gram Sabha

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The origin and foundational provisions of the Provisions of the Panchayats (Extension to the Scheduled Areas) Act, 1996 — sections 4(a) to 4(d)

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity chapter on PESA, read with the Act's own section 4 sequence so that objectives, village definition and Gram Sabha competence are taught in statutory order
CA search: "amendment to the PESA Act 1996 or new central guidance on Scheduled Area Gram Sabhas in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No amendment to the 1996 Act was located; dated State-level rule status is treated separately as an implementation status check in Lesson 19
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: from excluded territory to statutory self-rule

```text
1992  Part IX constitutionalises Panchayats
            |
Article 243M(1): Part IX shall NOT apply to Scheduled Areas or tribal areas
            |
Article 243M(4)(b): PARLIAMENT may extend Part IX to those areas
                    "subject to such exceptions and modifications
                     as may be specified in such law"
            |
committee work on how the extension should be designed
            |
1996  PROVISIONS OF THE PANCHAYATS (EXTENSION TO THE
      SCHEDULED AREAS) ACT  -- "PESA"
            |
   s.4(a)  State Panchayat law in Scheduled Areas SHALL BE IN CONSONANCE
           with customary law, social and religious practices and
           traditional management practices of community resources
   s.4(b)  a VILLAGE shall ordinarily consist of a habitation, a group of
           habitations, a hamlet or a group of hamlets comprising a community
           and managing its affairs in accordance with traditions and customs
   s.4(c)  every village shall have a GRAM SABHA of persons whose names are
           in the electoral rolls for the Panchayat at the village level
   s.4(d)  every Gram Sabha shall be COMPETENT to safeguard and preserve
           traditions and customs, cultural identity, community resources
           and the customary mode of dispute resolution
```

Caption: PESA does not create a new tier. It re-specifies what a village is, who constitutes its assembly and what that assembly is competent to protect — and then, in the rest of section 4, what it may decide.

### 1. The gap PESA was enacted to fill

By 1992 the Fifth Schedule had a guardian, a reporter, an adviser and a regulator, but no village-level democratic institution of its own. Part IX would have supplied one, except that Article 243M(1) deliberately kept it out — because applying a uniform Panchayat template to Scheduled Areas risked overwriting customary institutions with a standard administrative grid.

Article 243M(4)(b) resolved the tension: Parliament could extend Part IX **with exceptions and modifications**. PESA is the content of those words. Its objectives, as standard Polity sources summarise them, are to extend Part IX to Scheduled Areas with modifications; to provide self-rule for the bulk of the tribal population; to establish village governance with participatory democracy, making the Gram Sabha the nucleus of all activities; to evolve an administrative framework consistent with traditional practices; to safeguard and preserve the traditions and customs of tribal communities; to empower Panchayats at appropriate levels with powers suited to tribal requirements; and to prevent higher-level Panchayats from assuming the powers of lower levels or of the Gram Sabha.

Read the last objective carefully. **Subsidiarity is written into PESA's purpose**, and it reappears as a binding instruction in section 4(n).

### 2. Section 4(a): custom as a legislative constraint

A State's Panchayat legislation for Scheduled Areas "shall be in consonance with the customary law, social and religious practices and traditional management practices of community resources."

This is an unusual provision. It makes **custom a constraint on State legislation** in a defined territory. Three cautions follow, and all three belong in a good answer.

- "In consonance with" is a conformity standard, not an incorporation of custom as law.
- Custom here is plural and local; there is no single tribal custom to conform to, which is precisely why PESA then devolves the question to the village.
- Custom remains subject to the Constitution. Fundamental Rights, equality for women within the community, and valid statute law are not displaced by section 4(a). A practice that excluded women from a Gram Sabha, for instance, could not be defended by invoking tradition.

### 3. Section 4(b): why the village definition is the heart of PESA

"A village shall ordinarily consist of a habitation or a group of habitations or a hamlet or a group of hamlets comprising a community and managing its affairs in accordance with traditions and customs."

Compare that with the ordinary administrative reality. A Gram Panchayat in a Scheduled Area may cover several thousand people spread across widely separated hamlets, often of different communities. Decisions taken at that level are physically and socially distant from the hamlet whose forest, pond or burial ground is at stake.

PESA's definition moves the democratic unit **down** to the habitation, on two tests that must both be satisfied: it must comprise a **community**, and it must be **managing its affairs in accordance with traditions and customs**. The test is sociological, not cartographic.

This single definition carries most of PESA's practical consequence. If a State's rules treat the revenue village or the Gram Panchayat as the PESA village, the statute's entire architecture of proximity collapses, even though every other provision is faithfully reproduced.

### 4. Section 4(c) and 4(d): who constitutes the assembly, and what it protects

Section 4(c) makes the **Gram Sabha** consist of persons whose names are included in the electoral rolls for the Panchayat at the village level. Note what this does: it ties membership to the electoral roll, so the assembly is universal within the village rather than confined to a traditional council, a clan body or male heads of household. Custom shapes the unit; the electoral roll constitutes the membership.

Section 4(d) then states the Gram Sabha's foundational competence: to safeguard and preserve **the traditions and customs of the people, their cultural identity, community resources and the customary mode of dispute resolution**.

Four objects, and each is substantial. The last — customary dispute resolution — is the one most often forgotten, and it matters because it gives statutory recognition to local forums without converting them into courts.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Building governance on "traditions and customs" risks entrenching hierarchies inside the community, particularly against women and smaller groups.

**Reply.** Section 4(c) answers part of this directly by constituting the Gram Sabha from the electoral roll rather than from a traditional body, which makes it inclusive by law. Section 4(g), studied next, reserves not less than one-half of seats for Scheduled Tribes and all chairperson posts, and section 4(h) allows nomination of unrepresented Scheduled Tribes. And custom operates under the Constitution, not above it.

**Residual.** Formal inclusion and effective voice are different things. Meeting notice, quorum practice, timing, language, the physical accessibility of the venue, and the presence or absence of a reliable written record determine whether a legally inclusive assembly is a genuinely deliberative one. Those are the variables that Lesson 19 returns to.

### 6. A naming caution to carry

The committee work that shaped the extension of Panchayat provisions to Scheduled Areas is associated with Dilip Singh Bhuria, and so is the second Commission appointed under Article 339(1) in 2002. They are different bodies with different mandates and different dates. In an answer, write the function and the year — "the committee that shaped the 1996 extension" and "the 2002 Commission under Article 339(1)" — rather than relying on the surname alone.

### UPSC application

- Quote section 4(b) in full; it is the provision most likely to earn a distinguishing mark.
- Pair 243M(1) with 243M(4)(b) whenever the question asks why PESA exists.
- Use "custom as a constraint on State legislation, subject to the Constitution" as your section 4(a) sentence.
- Mention section 4(c)'s electoral-roll basis to pre-empt the exclusion objection.
- Do not call PESA a constitutional amendment; it is a parliamentary statute enacted under an enabling clause.

### Revision notes

1. Article 243M(1) excluded Part IX from Scheduled Areas and tribal areas.
2. Article 243M(4)(b) let Parliament extend Part IX with exceptions and modifications.
3. PESA, 1996 is that extension, and only for Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas.
4. PESA's objectives include self-rule, participatory village governance, an administrative framework consistent with traditional practices, preservation of custom, and subsidiarity.
5. Section 4(a): State Panchayat law in Scheduled Areas shall be in consonance with customary law, social and religious practices and traditional management of community resources.
6. "In consonance with" is a conformity standard; custom remains subject to the Constitution.
7. Section 4(b): a village is ordinarily a habitation, group of habitations, hamlet or group of hamlets comprising a community managing its affairs by traditions and customs.
8. The 4(b) test is sociological — a community, managing its own affairs — not an administrative boundary.
9. Section 4(c): the Gram Sabha consists of persons on the electoral rolls for the village-level Panchayat.
10. Membership is therefore universal within the village, not confined to a traditional council.
11. Section 4(d): the Gram Sabha is competent to safeguard traditions and customs, cultural identity, community resources and the customary mode of dispute resolution.
12. Mis-defining the PESA village in State rules defeats the statute even if all other provisions are copied.
13. The PESA-shaping committee and the 2002 Article 339(1) Commission share a surname; distinguish by function and date.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State's PESA rules define the "village" as the existing revenue village. Why is this a serious defect?

**Model answer:** Section 4(b) provides that a village shall ordinarily consist of a habitation, a group of habitations, a hamlet or a group of hamlets **comprising a community and managing its affairs in accordance with traditions and customs**. The statutory test is sociological — a community that already manages its own affairs — not administrative. Equating the PESA village with a revenue village or Gram Panchayat moves the Gram Sabha away from the habitation whose land, forest or water is at stake, dilutes smaller communities inside a larger unit, and hollows out every power that section 4 confers on the Gram Sabha, because the body exercising them is no longer the body the Act contemplated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the village definition as a drafting preamble. It is the operative provision that determines who exercises every later power in section 4.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt:** "PESA's most radical provision is its definition of a village." Comment.

#### Model solution

Section 4(b) provides that a PESA village shall ordinarily consist of a habitation, group of habitations, hamlet or group of hamlets comprising a community and managing its affairs by traditions and customs. The test is sociological, not administrative, and radical for three reasons.

First, it relocates the democratic unit to the level at which forests, water bodies, grazing land and burial grounds are used, so decisions are taken by those who bear the consequences. Second, it protects smaller communities otherwise outvoted inside a large multi-hamlet Gram Panchayat. Third, every later power in section 4 — approval of plans, certification, consultation on acquisition, recommendation on minor minerals, ownership of minor forest produce — is exercised by that village's Gram Sabha, so the definition determines who holds them all.

Its radicalism is its vulnerability: where State rules equate the PESA village with a revenue village, proximity collapses while the statute appears implemented.

**Rubric (10):** exact section 4(b) wording 3; sociological-versus-administrative contrast 2; consequences for proximity and minority voice 2; dependence of later section 4 powers on the definition 2; implementation vulnerability 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

The assembly now exists and knows what it protects. What it may actually decide is written in the rest of section 4, and each clause uses a deliberately different legal verb.

---

## Lesson 15: PESA Section 4: The Power-Verb Code and the Intensity Ladder

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: PESA sections 4(e) to 4(o) and section 5 — approval, identification, certification, consultation, mandatory recommendation, endowment and subsidiarity

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity enumeration of PESA's features, matched clause by clause to section 4 so that each power is attached to its exact statutory verb and its correct holder
CA search: "judicial ruling or central clarification on PESA gram sabha consent, consultation or minor mineral recommendation in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No new authoritative ruling or clarification on PESA verbs was located, so the lesson teaches the statutory text and the standard distinctions
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the intensity ladder, and where each clause sits

```text
   LOWEST INTENSITY
        |
   INFORMATION                 no statutory stage in PESA; the Act never
        |                      settles for mere notice
   CONSULTATION                s.4(i)  land acquisition in Scheduled Areas
   views must be sought                and resettlement or rehabilitation of
   and considered                      persons affected by such projects
        |                              -> Gram Sabha OR Panchayat at the
        |                                 appropriate level
   MANDATORY PRIOR             s.4(k)  grant of prospecting licence or mining
   RECOMMENDATION                      lease for MINOR MINERALS
   a specified recommendation  s.4(l)  grant of concession for exploitation
   is a precondition                   of minor minerals BY AUCTION
        |
   APPROVAL                    s.4(e)(i) plans, programmes and projects for
   the plan cannot proceed               social and economic development,
   without it                            BEFORE they are taken up for
        |                                 implementation by the village Panchayat
   SUBSTANTIVE ENDOWMENT       s.4(m)  State law SHALL ensure the Gram Sabha and
   ownership, control,                  Panchayats at the appropriate level are
   regulation, restoration              endowed with seven specified powers
        |
   HIGHEST INTENSITY
```

Caption: the Act assigns a different legal verb to each decision type. Reading one verb in place of another is the single most common PESA error in both Prelims and Mains.

### Part A — The verb-by-verb map

| Clause | Verb | Object | Holder, as the Act states it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4(e)(i) | **Approve** | Plans, programmes and projects for social and economic development, before implementation by the village Panchayat | Gram Sabha |
| 4(e)(ii) | **Identify or select** | Beneficiaries under poverty alleviation and other programmes | Gram Sabha |
| 4(f) | **Certify** | Utilisation of funds for those plans, programmes and projects | Village Panchayat must obtain the Gram Sabha's certification |
| 4(g) | **Reserve** | Seats at every Panchayat in Scheduled Areas | Reservation **in proportion to the population** of the communities for whom reservation is sought under Part IX; first proviso — not less than one-half of total seats for Scheduled Tribes; second proviso — all chairperson seats at all levels reserved for Scheduled Tribes |
| 4(h) | **Nominate** | Scheduled Tribes with no representation at the intermediate or district Panchayat | State Government, subject to a ceiling of one-tenth of the total members to be elected in that Panchayat |
| 4(i) | **Consult** | Land acquisition in Scheduled Areas for development projects, and resettlement or rehabilitation of affected persons | Gram Sabha or Panchayats at the appropriate level; actual planning and implementation of projects is coordinated at the State level |
| 4(j) | **Manage** | Planning and management of minor water bodies in Scheduled Areas | Panchayats at the appropriate level |
| 4(k) | **Recommend, mandatorily** | Grant of prospecting licence or mining lease for minor minerals | Gram Sabha or Panchayats at the appropriate level |
| 4(l) | **Recommend, prior and mandatory** | Grant of concession for exploitation of minor minerals by auction | Gram Sabha or Panchayats at the appropriate level |
| 4(m) | **Endow** | Seven substantive powers, set out below | State Legislature must ensure Gram Sabha and Panchayats at the appropriate level are endowed |
| 4(n) | **Safeguard subsidiarity** | Higher Panchayats must not assume the powers of a lower Panchayat or of the Gram Sabha | State legislation must contain the safeguard |
| 4(o) | **Endeavour** | To follow the Sixth Schedule pattern in designing administrative arrangements in district-level Panchayats in Scheduled Areas | State Legislature |

### Part B — Section 4(m): the substantive basket

The State Legislature, while endowing Panchayats in Scheduled Areas with powers to function as institutions of self-government, must ensure that the Gram Sabha and Panchayats at the appropriate level are endowed specifically with:

```text
(i)   power to enforce prohibition, or to regulate or restrict the sale
      and consumption of any intoxicant
(ii)  the OWNERSHIP of minor forest produce
(iii) power to PREVENT alienation of land in the Scheduled Areas and to
      take appropriate action to RESTORE any unlawfully alienated land
      of a Scheduled Tribe
(iv)  power to manage village markets, by whatever name called
(v)   power to exercise control over money-lending to the Scheduled Tribes
(vi)  power to exercise control over institutions and functionaries in
      all social sectors
(vii) power to control local plans and resources for such plans,
      including tribal sub-plans
```

Two features make this clause powerful. Head (ii) uses the word **ownership**, not access or usufruct. And head (iii) couples **prevention** with **restoration**, which matters because a prohibition that cannot reverse a completed transfer protects nobody who has already lost land.

Two features limit it. The powers arrive through **State law**: section 4(m) binds the State Legislature to endow, so the operative instrument is the State Panchayat Act and its rules. And several heads — intoxicants, markets, money-lending — intersect with separate State excise, market and money-lending statutes, so conformity has to be achieved across several laws, not just one.

### Part C — Why the ladder is the examination point

The decisive insight is that **PESA calibrated community power to the decision type**.

| Decision | Statutory intensity | Why the Act chose it |
|---|---|---|
| Village development plan | Approval | Entirely local in scope and effect; the village should decide |
| Beneficiary selection | Identification | Local knowledge is superior to administrative lists |
| Fund utilisation | Certification | Converts the Gram Sabha into an audit check |
| Land acquisition and resettlement | Consultation | Acquisition involves public purpose and compensation law; State-level coordination is expressly preserved |
| Minor-mineral licence, lease or auction concession | Mandatory recommendation | A specified recommendation becomes a precondition, without conferring an unconditional veto |
| Minor forest produce, markets, money-lending, intoxicants, alienation | Endowment of ownership and control | These define everyday economic life, so the Act requires substantive transfer |

The proposition to carry into an answer: **PESA is a calibrated code of community power, not a universal Gram Sabha veto**. That sentence is both the accurate legal position and the most defensible thesis for a Mains question on the subject.

Note also what the ladder leaves out. Section 4(k) and 4(l) speak of **minor** minerals. Major minerals are not within those clauses. Forest land and recognised forest rights run through the Forest Rights Act and forest and environment law, which the next two lessons take up. Writing "PESA requires Gram Sabha consent for all mining" is wrong three times over — wrong verb, wrong mineral category, wrong statute.

### Part D — Section 4(i) read against its own second limb

Section 4(i) is the clause most often misquoted, because it contains two sentences that point in different directions. The first requires consultation with the Gram Sabha or appropriate Panchayat before acquisition in Scheduled Areas and before resettling or rehabilitating affected persons. The second provides that the **actual planning and implementation of projects in the Scheduled Areas shall be coordinated at the State level**.

An honest answer states both. Parliament gave the community a guaranteed voice at the acquisition and rehabilitation stage while keeping project coordination with the State. Quoting only the first sentence overstates the power; quoting only the second understates it.

### Part E — Section 5 and the transition

PESA also provided that any provision of a law relating to Panchayats in Scheduled Areas which is inconsistent with the Act ceases to be in force at the expiry of **one year** from the date on which the Act received the President's assent; existing Panchayats continue until the expiry of their term unless the State Legislature dissolves them sooner. States were therefore given a defined conformity window, which is relevant to the implementation discussion in Lesson 19.

### Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Calling consultation a "power" is generous; a State can consult and then proceed exactly as planned.

**Reply.** Consultation in administrative law is not an empty formality — views must genuinely be sought and considered, and a decision taken without consultation where the statute requires it is vulnerable. More importantly, PESA did not stop at consultation: it used approval for plans, mandatory recommendation for minor-mineral decisions, and ownership and control under section 4(m).

**Residual.** The reply holds only where a record exists. Without minutes, attendance, resolutions and reasoned responses, the difference between genuine consultation and a signed register cannot be demonstrated — which is why record quality, not statutory language, is the real battleground.

### UPSC application

- Reproduce the verb for each clause; examiners reward "approve," "certify," "consult," "recommend," "own," "restore" used accurately.
- Quote section 4(g) in three steps — proportional reservation as the rule, the one-half Scheduled Tribe floor as the first proviso, all chairperson seats as the second — and add the one-tenth nomination ceiling under 4(h).
- Always pair section 4(i)'s consultation requirement with its State-level coordination sentence.
- Use section 4(o) when comparing PESA with the Sixth Schedule; Parliament itself pointed at that pattern.
- Never claim a universal veto; claim calibrated power, and prove it with three different verbs.

### Revision notes

1. Section 4(e)(i): Gram Sabha approves plans, programmes and projects for social and economic development before village-Panchayat implementation.
2. Section 4(e)(ii): Gram Sabha identifies or selects beneficiaries under poverty alleviation and other programmes.
3. Section 4(f): the village Panchayat must obtain the Gram Sabha's certification of utilisation of funds.
4. Section 4(g): reservation of seats at every Panchayat **in proportion to the population** of the communities for whom reservation is sought under Part IX; first proviso — ST reservation not less than one-half of total seats; second proviso — all chairperson seats at all levels reserved for STs.
5. Section 4(h): State may nominate unrepresented STs at intermediate or district level, not exceeding one-tenth of members to be elected.
6. Section 4(i): consultation before land acquisition and before resettlement or rehabilitation; actual planning and implementation coordinated at State level.
7. Section 4(j): planning and management of minor water bodies entrusted to Panchayats at the appropriate level.
8. Section 4(k): recommendation mandatory for grant of prospecting licence or mining lease for minor minerals.
9. Section 4(l): prior recommendation mandatory for concession for exploitation of minor minerals by auction.
10. Section 4(m): seven endowments — intoxicants; ownership of minor forest produce; prevention of alienation and restoration of unlawfully alienated land; village markets; control over money-lending to STs; control over institutions and functionaries in all social sectors; control over local plans and resources including tribal sub-plans.
11. Section 4(n): higher Panchayats must not assume the powers of lower Panchayats or the Gram Sabha.
12. Section 4(o): the State Legislature shall endeavour to follow the Sixth Schedule pattern at district level.
13. Section 5: inconsistent State provisions cease one year after Presidential assent; existing Panchayats continue till their term ends.
14. PESA verbs apply to "the Gram Sabha or the Panchayats at the appropriate level" where the Act says so.
15. Minor minerals only; major minerals, forest land and clearances run through other statutes.

### Concept check

**Question:** A company obtains a mining lease for a minor mineral in a Scheduled Area after the district administration held a public meeting and recorded "no objection." Does this satisfy PESA?

**Model answer:** Not as described. Section 4(k) makes the **recommendation** of the Gram Sabha or the Panchayats at the appropriate level **mandatory** for the grant of a prospecting licence or mining lease for minor minerals in Scheduled Areas, and section 4(l) requires a prior recommendation for a concession to exploit minor minerals by auction. A generic public meeting recording no objection is neither a Gram Sabha proceeding nor a recommendation by the competent body. The question is not whether people were heard but whether the statutory instrument — a recommendation by the specified holder — exists.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating consultation, no-objection and recommendation as interchangeable. Section 4(i) uses consultation for acquisition and rehabilitation; sections 4(k) and 4(l) require a mandatory recommendation; and section 4(e) requires approval. The verb determines the legal consequence.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "PESA is a calibrated code of community power, not a universal Gram Sabha veto." Discuss.

#### Model solution

Section 4 assigns a different legal verb to each class of decision, and the calibration is deliberate.

Where the decision is wholly local, the Act uses **approval**: under section 4(e)(i) the Gram Sabha approves plans, programmes and projects for social and economic development before the village Panchayat implements them, and under 4(e)(ii) it identifies beneficiaries. Section 4(f) adds a financial check by requiring the village Panchayat to obtain the Gram Sabha's certification of fund utilisation.

Where the decision engages public purpose and compensation law, the Act uses **consultation**: section 4(i) requires consultation with the Gram Sabha or appropriate Panchayat before land acquisition in Scheduled Areas and before resettlement or rehabilitation, while expressly keeping actual planning and implementation coordinated at the State level.

Where a resource decision is locally consequential but State-administered, the Act uses **mandatory recommendation**: sections 4(k) and 4(l) make a recommendation, and a prior recommendation respectively, mandatory for minor-mineral prospecting licences, mining leases and auction concessions.

Where everyday economic life is at stake, the Act requires **substantive endowment**: section 4(m) obliges State law to endow ownership of minor forest produce, control of village markets and money-lending, regulation of intoxicants, prevention of land alienation and restoration of unlawfully alienated land, and control over social-sector institutions and local plans.

PESA's strength is therefore legal precision, not a maximalist slogan. Courts and administrators should enforce the exact verb at each stage, while States strengthen Gram Sabha information, inclusion, records and remedies so that precision translates into power.

**Rubric (15):** approval and identification clauses 3; consultation clause with its State-coordination limb 3; mandatory recommendation for minor minerals 3; section 4(m) endowments 3; thesis proved rather than asserted 2; implementation-oriented conclusion 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

PESA settles who participates and with what local power. It does not settle who holds which forest right. That is the Forest Rights Act's question, and the two statutes must be read together without being merged.

## Lesson 16: The Forest Rights Act Interface: Claims, Community Forest Resource and Conservation Duties

Progress: 16/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: How the Forest Rights Act, 2006 interlocks with the Fifth Schedule and PESA — the Gram Sabha's statutory role, the claims chain and the conservation duties

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity and governance treatment of the Forest Rights Act as the companion rights statute; the full forest-law doctrine belongs with environment law, so this lesson stays on the governance interface
CA search: "new central rules, amendment or official instruction on Forest Rights Act community forest resource recognition in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No amendment or new central instrument was located for this period, so the lesson teaches the statutory architecture and its interface with PESA
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the rights-recognition chain, and what sits beside it

```text
   GRAM SABHA  (section 6(1))
   the authority to INITIATE the process of determining the nature and
   extent of individual or community forest rights, or both
     - receives and consolidates claims
     - verifies them
     - prepares a map delineating the area of each recommended claim
     - passes a resolution and forwards it
            |
            v
   SUB-DIVISIONAL LEVEL COMMITTEE
     - examines the Gram Sabha resolution
     - hears petitions from persons aggrieved
     - prepares the record of forest rights and forwards it
            |
            v
   DISTRICT LEVEL COMMITTEE
     - hears appeals
     - FINAL approval of the record of forest rights
            |
            v
   TITLE / RECOGNITION OF RIGHTS

   RUNNING ALONGSIDE, NOT INSIDE, THIS CHAIN
   section 4(5): no member of a forest-dwelling Scheduled Tribe or other
   traditional forest dweller shall be evicted or removed from forest land
   under his occupation till the recognition and verification procedure
   is complete
   section 5: holders of forest rights, the Gram Sabha and village-level
   institutions are empowered to protect wildlife, forest and biodiversity;
   protect catchments, water sources and ecologically sensitive areas;
   preserve the habitat from destructive practices affecting cultural and
   natural heritage; and ensure compliance with Gram Sabha decisions
   regulating access to community forest resources
```

Caption: the Gram Sabha initiates and verifies; the district committee finalises; and rights come attached to conservation duties rather than as unconditional entitlements.

### 1. Why this statute belongs in a Polity lesson at all

The full doctrine of the Forest Rights Act — eligibility, the categories of rights, the cut-off date, critical wildlife habitats — is forest and environment law. What belongs here is the **governance interface**, and it belongs here for a precise reason.

PESA answered the question *who participates, and with what local power*. It did not answer *who holds which right over which piece of forest*. Until that second question is answered, participation has nothing determinate to be about: a Gram Sabha cannot meaningfully be consulted on a project if nobody knows whose habitation, grazing route, sacred grove or produce-collection area the project will affect.

The Forest Rights Act supplies that missing determination, and it does so by making the **Gram Sabha** the initiating authority. That is the interface.

### 2. Section 6: the three-tier chain, read for its allocation of power

- **Section 6(1).** The Gram Sabha is the authority to initiate the process for determining the nature and extent of individual or community forest rights or both, by receiving and consolidating claims, verifying them, preparing a map delineating the area of each recommended claim, passing a resolution to that effect and forwarding a copy to the Sub-Divisional Level Committee.
- **Appeal.** A person aggrieved by a Gram Sabha resolution may petition the Sub-Divisional Level Committee within the statutory period.
- **Sub-Divisional Level Committee.** It examines the resolution, hears such petitions, prepares the record of forest rights and forwards it to the District Level Committee.
- **District Level Committee.** It hears appeals against the Sub-Divisional Committee's decisions and gives **final** approval to the record of forest rights.

Three governance consequences follow.

1. The Gram Sabha is not merely consulted; it is the **originating statutory authority**, and its map and resolution are the evidentiary foundation of everything above it.
2. The chain is **administrative with built-in appeals**, not judicial. Speed, record quality and committee composition therefore determine outcomes.
3. Finality rests at the district level. A Gram Sabha resolution is indispensable but not conclusive — a distinction that answers a great many examination propositions.

### 3. Community Forest Resource rights — the provision that changes governance

Among the forest rights recognised by the Act is the **right to protect, regenerate or conserve or manage any community forest resource which the community has been traditionally protecting and conserving for sustainable use**.

Three features make this a governance provision rather than only a tenure provision.

- It is a right of **management**, not merely of access or extraction. The holder becomes a decision-maker over the resource.
- The resource is the **customary common forest land** within the traditional or customary boundaries of the village, which can include areas traditionally accessed by the community.
- It is held by the **community through its Gram Sabha**, which means the Gram Sabha acquires a resource-management mandate independent of any Panchayat devolution.

This is why Community Forest Resource recognition is treated as the single most consequential element of the Act for self-governance, and why the distinction between an individual forest right, a community right and a Community Forest Resource right must never be blurred in an answer.

### 4. Section 5: rights with duties

The statute does not create an unconditional entitlement. The holders of forest rights, the Gram Sabha and village-level institutions are empowered and required to protect the wild life, forest and biodiversity; to ensure that adjoining catchment areas, water sources and other ecologically sensitive areas are adequately protected; to ensure that the habitat is preserved from destructive practices affecting cultural and natural heritage; and to ensure that decisions taken in the Gram Sabha regulating access to community forest resources, and stopping activity adversely affecting wildlife, forest and biodiversity, are complied with.

Two consequences. First, the standard objection that forest-rights recognition is inherently anti-conservation is answered **inside the statute**. Second, the duties are vested in the same body that holds the right, which is the classic design of common-property governance: the user is the regulator.

### 5. PESA and the Forest Rights Act: complementary, not interchangeable

| | PESA, 1996 | Forest Rights Act, 2006 |
|---|---|---|
| Central question | Who participates, and with what local power? | Who holds which forest right? |
| Territorial reach | Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas only | Forest land wherever eligible forest dwellers are found, not confined to Scheduled Areas |
| Instrument | Modified application of Part IX through State law | Direct statutory recognition and vesting of rights |
| Role of the Gram Sabha | Approval, identification, certification, consultation, recommendation, control | Initiation and verification of claims, mapping, resolution; management of community forest resource |
| Minor forest produce | State law must endow **ownership** under section 4(m)(ii) | Recognised as a forest right with rights of ownership, access, use and disposal under the Act's rights framework |
| Output | Local decisions and endowed powers | Recorded and vested rights, with titles |

```text
PESA  :  WHO decides locally, and with what power?
   +
FRA   :  WHO holds which forest right, over which area?
   +
forest, environment, mineral, acquisition and rehabilitation law
   |
   v
a decision that is lawful on every applicable count
```

The combined chain yields the sentence that most improves a Mains answer on this topic: **a project may satisfy one statute and still fail another**. Compliance here is cumulative, not alternative.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Layering two statutes over two Schedules produces procedural congestion, delaying both rights recognition and development.

**Reply.** The layers perform different functions and cannot substitute for one another. Determining rights before appraising a project is not duplication; it is the precondition of a rational appraisal, because without it the project's real social cost is unknown.

**Residual.** Congestion is nonetheless a genuine administrative problem where claims remain pending for years, where community and Community Forest Resource claims lag far behind individual claims, and where record-keeping is weak. The answer is sequencing and capacity — complete the rights determination first and resource the committees — not the removal of a layer.

### UPSC application

- Name the chain exactly: Gram Sabha, Sub-Divisional Level Committee, District Level Committee, with finality at the district level.
- Distinguish individual rights, community rights and Community Forest Resource rights in every answer that mentions the Act.
- Quote section 5 whenever conservation is raised as an objection.
- Use the "PESA asks who participates; the Act asks who holds which right" formulation as your interface sentence.
- Keep the detailed forest-law doctrine for environment answers; here, stay on governance.

### Revision notes

1. The Forest Rights Act, 2006 recognises and vests forest rights in forest-dwelling Scheduled Tribes and other traditional forest dwellers.
2. Section 6(1) makes the Gram Sabha the authority to initiate determination of individual or community forest rights.
3. The Gram Sabha receives, consolidates and verifies claims, prepares a map of each recommended claim and passes a resolution.
4. Petitions by aggrieved persons lie to the Sub-Divisional Level Committee.
5. The Sub-Divisional Level Committee examines the resolution, prepares the record of forest rights and forwards it.
6. The District Level Committee hears appeals and gives final approval to the record of forest rights.
7. The chain is administrative with statutory appeals; finality is at district level.
8. Community Forest Resource rights include the right to protect, regenerate, conserve or manage a traditionally protected and conserved community forest resource.
9. The community forest resource is customary common forest land within the village's traditional or customary boundaries.
10. Section 5 vests conservation duties in rights holders, the Gram Sabha and village-level institutions.
11. Section 4(5) bars eviction until the recognition and verification procedure is complete.
12. Individual rights, community rights and Community Forest Resource rights are three distinct categories.
13. PESA applies only to Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas; the Act is not territorially confined to them.
14. The Act does not transfer all forest administration or mineral ownership to the Gram Sabha.
15. Compliance across the Schedules, PESA, the Act and sectoral law is cumulative.

### Concept check

**Question:** A district administration says forest rights in a block are "settled" because individual titles have been issued to all claimants. What is missing?

**Model answer:** Individual forest rights are only one of the categories the Act recognises. **Community rights** over common uses and entitlements, and in particular **Community Forest Resource rights** — the right to protect, regenerate, conserve or manage the customary common forest land the community has traditionally protected — remain to be determined through the same section 6 chain, beginning with Gram Sabha claims, verification and mapping. Until those are recorded, the community's governance mandate over the resource is unrecognised, and any subsequent project appraisal will be working from an incomplete picture of affected rights.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating individual titles as the measure of implementation. Community and Community Forest Resource recognition is where the governance consequence of the statute actually lies.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Analyse how the Forest Rights Act, 2006 complements PESA in Scheduled Areas, and explain why neither can substitute for the other.

#### Model solution

PESA and the Forest Rights Act answer different questions, which is why they fit together.

PESA addresses the architecture of local decision-making in Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas. Section 4(b) defines the village as a habitation or group of habitations managing its affairs by traditions and customs; section 4(e) gives the Gram Sabha approval over local development plans; section 4(i) requires consultation before acquisition and rehabilitation; sections 4(k) and 4(l) make recommendations mandatory for minor-mineral licences, leases and auction concessions; and section 4(m) obliges State law to endow ownership of minor forest produce, control of markets and money-lending, and powers to prevent alienation and restore alienated land.

The Forest Rights Act addresses entitlement. Section 6 makes the Gram Sabha the authority to initiate determination of individual and community forest rights, consolidating and verifying claims and mapping each recommended claim, with the Sub-Divisional Level Committee preparing the record and the District Level Committee giving final approval. Community Forest Resource rights add a management mandate over customary common forest land, while section 5 attaches conservation duties and section 4(5) bars eviction before the procedure is complete.

Neither can substitute for the other. Participation without determined rights is procedurally real but substantively empty, because affected interests are unknown; determined rights without a functioning Gram Sabha leave titles unenforced. The Act is also not confined to Scheduled Areas, so PESA cannot carry its work everywhere.

Lawful decisions therefore require cumulative compliance: rights determination, statutory participation, appraisal, reasoned decision and monitored rehabilitation.

**Rubric (15):** accurate PESA functions 4; accurate section 6 chain 4; Community Forest Resource and section 5 duties 3; reasoned non-substitutability 2; sequencing conclusion 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Rights and participation exist on paper. The test of both is the one arena where tribal land has historically been lost — transfer, acquisition and mining — and that is where the courts have intervened.

## Lesson 17: Land Alienation, Mining and the Two Leading Judgments

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The alienation cycle, restoration machinery, *Samatha*, the Niyamgiri judgment and the cumulative-compliance chain

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity note on *Samatha* and the Fifth Schedule land-transfer bar, with the scope caution the same sources attach, since the judgment's reach beyond its State regulation has been debated
CA search: "new Supreme Court ruling on tribal land transfer, Scheduled Area mining leases or gram sabha consent in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No new authoritative ruling altering the position set out below was located, so the two leading judgments remain the operative case law for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: how land is lost, and where the law intervenes

```text
   weak or unrecorded title | debt | fraud | coercion
   benami purchase | administrative transfer | acquisition
                          |
                          v
          LOSS OF LAND  ->  LOSS OF LIVELIHOOD
                          |
   +----------------------+----------------------+
   |                      |                      |
 FIFTH SCHEDULE       PESA s.4(m)(iii)      FORMAL ACQUISITION
 para 5(2)(a)-(c)     prevent alienation    and rehabilitation law
 prohibit/restrict    AND restore           + PESA s.4(i) consultation
 transfer; regulate   unlawfully alienated
 allotment; regulate  land of a Scheduled
 money-lending        Tribe
   |                      |                      |
   +----------------------+----------------------+
                          |
          WHAT MAKES ANY OF THIS WORK
          updated and public land records
          an accessible forum and legal aid
          a restoration remedy that is actually tracked
                          |
                          v
          RESTORATION, not a paper prohibition
```

Caption: every protective provision in this topic converges on one test — can a completed wrongful transfer actually be reversed?

### 1. The mechanism of dispossession

Dispossession in Scheduled Areas is rarely a single illegal act. It is a sequence, and naming the sequence is what turns a descriptive answer into an analytical one.

1. **Record weakness.** Customary occupation is not always reflected in revenue or forest records, so the occupant cannot prove what they hold.
2. **Debt.** Credit at exploitative rates converts a seasonal shortfall into a permanent claim on land — which is exactly why Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2)(c) and PESA section 4(m)(v) target money-lending.
3. **Transfer.** Sale, mortgage, benami arrangement, long lease or "voluntary" surrender transfers possession, often in form rather than substance.
4. **Administrative conversion.** Land is re-classified, allotted or acquired, with compensation that reflects market value rather than livelihood value.
5. **Project change.** Forest diversion, submergence or exclusion alters the surrounding resource base even for those not directly displaced.

Each step has a legal answer somewhere in this topic. The failure is almost never the absence of a provision; it is the absence of a record, a forum and a remedy.

### 2. Prevention and restoration are different obligations

| Function | Instrument | Weakness if used alone |
|---|---|---|
| Prevent future transfer | Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2)(a) and (b); State land-transfer regulations | Does nothing about transfers already completed |
| Regulate credit | Paragraph 5(2)(c); PESA section 4(m)(v); Sixth Schedule paragraph 10 | Prevention without enforcement capacity |
| Restore alienated land | PESA section 4(m)(iii) | Needs records, a forum, legal aid and tracking |
| Compensate and rehabilitate | Acquisition and rehabilitation law, with PESA section 4(i) consultation | Compensation is not restoration |

PESA section 4(m)(iii) is doing something the Fifth Schedule's own regulation power does not expressly do: it requires State law to endow the power **to take appropriate action to restore any unlawfully alienated land of a Scheduled Tribe**. A prohibition tells the future what not to do; restoration addresses the past. Both are necessary.

A further caution for answers: **the operative land-transfer regulation is State-specific**. Definitions, presumptions, limitation periods, burden of proof and remedies differ across the ten States. Never assume one State's provisions describe another's.

### 3. *Samatha v. State of Andhra Pradesh* (1997)

**Setting.** The case arose under the Andhra Pradesh Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation, read with the Fifth Schedule.

**Majority holding.** State grants and leases of land in Scheduled Areas to non-tribal private mining companies were invalid in that regulatory setting; the protective regulation was read purposively so that the prohibition on transfer operated against such grants, and the permissible route lay with the State, its instrumentalities or cooperative societies of tribals.

**Constitutional significance.** The State was treated as holding land in a Scheduled Area in a trustee-like position, with the anti-alienation purpose of the Fifth Schedule informing the reading of the regulation.

**The boundary, which must always be stated.** The holding was built on a **specific State regulation**. Later treatment — including the context of the BALCO litigation — prevents presenting it as an automatic nationwide rule binding in every State irrespective of the applicable land-transfer law. The safe formulation is therefore: *Samatha* invalidated the impugned Scheduled-Area mining leases under the Andhra Pradesh regulation and gave the Fifth Schedule's protective purpose a strong reading, while its reach beyond that regulatory setting has been debated.

**What not to write.** "After *Samatha*, all mining by private companies in every Scheduled Area is constitutionally prohibited." That sentence over-claims and is the commonest error in answers on this case.

### 4. *Orissa Mining Corporation Ltd. v. Ministry of Environment and Forest* (2013) — Niyamgiri

**What the Court actually directed.**

```text
FRA claims of the affected communities -- individual, community,
cultural and religious, including the claim relating to worship
of the deity associated with the hill
            |
            v
GRAM SABHAS consider and decide those claims
with State and ministry assistance, and with a judicial officer
as observer
            |
            v
decisions communicated through the State to the Ministry of
Environment and Forest
            |
            v
THE MINISTRY takes the final Stage-II forest-clearance decision
in the light of the Gram Sabha decisions
```

**The holdings that matter for this topic.** The Gram Sabha has a statutory role in safeguarding customary and religious rights under the Forest Rights Act read with PESA. Religious and cultural claims associated with a sacred landscape are justiciable interests capable of statutory determination, not sentiment outside the law. And the determination of those claims had to precede the final clearance decision.

**The limit that must always be stated.** The judgment did **not** make the Gram Sabha the final clearance authority. The final Stage-II decision remained with the Ministry, to be taken in the light of the Gram Sabha decisions.

**The reported sequel**, which illustrates the mechanism rather than extending the ratio: twelve Gram Sabhas in the affected districts were held in the second half of 2013 and are reported to have rejected the proposal, after which the Ministry is reported to have refused Stage-II clearance in January 2014. These follow-up facts rest on contemporary reporting rather than on the judgment itself, and should be presented that way.

### 5. The cumulative-compliance chain

```text
TERRITORIAL STATUS
  is the land in a Scheduled Area or a tribal area?
        |
LAND-TRANSFER LAW
  what does the applicable State regulation prohibit, and whom does it bind?
        |
RIGHTS DETERMINATION
  have individual, community and community forest resource rights been
  determined under the Forest Rights Act section 6 chain?
        |
STATUTORY PARTICIPATION
  the exact PESA verb for this decision stage -- consult, recommend, approve
        |
SECTORAL APPRAISAL
  forest diversion, environmental appraisal, mineral law, acquisition
  and rehabilitation law
        |
REASONED DECISION
  avoid | redesign | condition | compensate and rehabilitate | refuse
        |
ENFORCEMENT AND MONITORING
  restoration tracking, rehabilitation delivery, grievance redress
```

The analytical claim this chain supports: **no single clearance, consultation or compensation substitutes for the others**. That is the thesis for almost any Mains question on tribal land and mining.

### 6. Answer-writing worked example

| Step | Content |
|---|---|
| Claim | Statutory participation changes what counts as a project's impact |
| Named evidence | The Niyamgiri judgment required Gram Sabhas to determine Forest Rights Act claims, including cultural and religious claims, before the final clearance decision |
| Analysis | A sacred landscape, a grazing route or a produce-collection area cannot be valued by market compensation; determination converts such interests into decision-relevant facts |
| Qualification | The Ministry retained the final Stage-II decision, and other statutory routes continued to apply |
| Mini-verdict | Participation improves the quality of the decision record; it does not displace the decision-maker |

That five-move structure — claim, named evidence, analysis, qualification, mini-verdict — is the paragraph template to reuse throughout this topic.

### UPSC application

- Always give *Samatha* its State-regulation setting and its scope qualification in the same breath.
- Always give the Niyamgiri judgment its Ministry-retains-final-decision limit in the same breath.
- Separate prevention from restoration; most answers mention only the first.
- Use the cumulative-compliance chain as your structure for any rights-versus-development question.
- Distinguish compensation, royalty sharing and consent; they are three different things.

### Revision notes

1. Dispossession is a sequence: record weakness, debt, transfer, administrative conversion, project change.
2. Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2)(a) to (c) illustrate the regulation power through transfer, allotment and money-lending.
3. PESA section 4(m)(iii) requires State law to endow powers both to prevent alienation and to restore unlawfully alienated land.
4. Sixth Schedule paragraph 10 regulates money-lending and trading in autonomous districts — by non-tribals in Assam and Meghalaya, and, as amended in 1988, by every person resident in the district in Tripura and Mizoram; under 10(4) it does not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council.
5. Land-transfer regulations are State-specific in wording and remedy.
6. *Samatha* (1997) arose under the Andhra Pradesh Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation read with the Fifth Schedule.
7. It invalidated the impugned grants and leases of Scheduled-Area land to non-tribal private mining companies in that setting.
8. It read the State as holding such land in a trustee-like position, with a purposive anti-alienation reading.
9. Its reach beyond that regulatory setting has been debated; do not state it as an automatic nationwide prohibition.
10. The Niyamgiri judgment (2013) required Gram Sabhas to decide Forest Rights Act claims, including cultural and religious claims.
11. A judicial officer acted as observer and the State and ministry were to assist.
12. The Ministry retained the final Stage-II forest-clearance decision, to be taken in the light of the Gram Sabha decisions.
13. Reported sequel: twelve Gram Sabhas rejected the proposal in 2013; the Ministry is reported to have refused clearance in January 2014.
14. Cumulative compliance: territorial status, land-transfer law, rights determination, PESA verb, sectoral appraisal, reasoned decision, enforcement.
15. Compensation is not restoration; royalty share is not consent; one clearance is not all clearances.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the holding and the limit of the Niyamgiri judgment in two sentences, without over-claiming.

**Model answer:** The Supreme Court held that the Gram Sabhas of the affected villages must consider and decide the communities' Forest Rights Act claims — including individual, community, cultural and religious claims associated with the sacred hill — with State and ministry assistance and a judicial officer as observer, and that those decisions were to be communicated through the State to the Ministry of Environment and Forest. The limit is that the **final Stage-II forest-clearance decision remained with the Ministry**, to be taken in the light of those Gram Sabha decisions, so the judgment did not confer a general or final clearance power on the Gram Sabha.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting the case into "Gram Sabha veto over all mining." It established a mandatory statutory determination before clearance in that proceeding, not a universal community veto over every project.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Analyse how the Fifth Schedule, PESA, the Forest Rights Act and judicial decisions together regulate land and mining conflicts in tribal areas, and evaluate why conflict persists.

#### Model solution

Regulation here is cumulative; conflict persists where one layer is completed and others are not.

The Fifth Schedule supplies the constitutional base. Paragraph 5(2) permits regulations restricting transfer of land by or among Scheduled Tribes, regulating allotment to them and regulating money-lending; paragraph 5(3) allows such a regulation to repeal or amend an applicable Act, and 5(4) conditions it on Presidential assent. The operative instrument, however, is State-specific.

PESA supplies calibrated participation. Section 4(i) requires consultation before acquisition and rehabilitation while preserving State-level coordination of project planning; sections 4(k) and 4(l) make recommendations mandatory for minor-mineral prospecting licences, leases and auction concessions; and section 4(m)(iii) obliges State law to endow powers to prevent alienation and to restore unlawfully alienated land.

The Forest Rights Act supplies entitlement. Section 6 makes the Gram Sabha the initiating authority for determining individual and community forest rights, with final approval at district level; section 5 attaches conservation duties; and section 4(5) bars eviction before the procedure is complete.

The courts supplied two bounded holdings. *Samatha* invalidated impugned Scheduled-Area mining leases to non-tribal private companies under the Andhra Pradesh land-transfer regulation, with a debated reach beyond that setting. The Niyamgiri judgment required Gram Sabha determination of forest-rights-linked cultural and religious claims while leaving the final Stage-II clearance with the Ministry.

Conflict persists because records are incomplete, restoration machinery is weak, PESA verbs are applied at the wrong stage, and compensation substitutes for recognition. Sequencing, records and enforceable remedies are the repair.

**Rubric (20):** Fifth Schedule paragraph 5 content 4; PESA verbs at correct stages 4; Forest Rights Act section 6 and section 5 4; both judgments with their exact limits 5; causal explanation of persistent conflict 2; sequencing-based conclusion 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

Land and mining expose the system under stress. The next step is to set that stress against the constitutional rights framework and then ask the institutional question directly: who is accountable to whom, and through what channel?
