## Lesson 18: Rights, Dignity and the Accountability Architecture of Tribal Governance

Progress: 18/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Part III and Directive Principles applied to Scheduled and tribal areas, the National Commission linkage, and the five channels of accountability

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity treatment of constitutional safeguards for Scheduled Tribes, used only for the rights interface; the commissions themselves are studied in the National Commissions topic
CA search: "official report or direction on accountability of Fifth and Sixth Schedule institutions or National Commission for Scheduled Tribes in India, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window. No nationally significant official accountability development was located for this period
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the rights web and the five accountability channels

| Provision | How it applies in a Scheduled or tribal area |
|---|---|
| Article 14 | Classification must be reasonable and administration non-arbitrary; differentiated treatment must serve the protective purpose |
| Article 19 | Movement, residence and occupation operate subject to valid protective restrictions, including land-transfer regulation |
| Article 21 | Dignity, livelihood, fair procedure and the cultural conditions of life |
| Articles 25 and 26 | Religious practice and sacred-landscape claims, as applied in the Niyamgiri proceedings |
| Article 29(1) | Right of a section of citizens to conserve its distinct language, script or culture |
| Article 46 | Directive Principle: promote with special care the educational and economic interests of the weaker sections, and in particular the Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes, and protect them from social injustice and all forms of exploitation |
| Article 300A | No person shall be deprived of property save by authority of law |
| Article 338A | A National Commission for Scheduled Tribes, with investigation, inquiry and advisory functions, and a duty on the Union and every State to consult it on all major policy matters affecting Scheduled Tribes |

```text
FIVE CHANNELS OF ACCOUNTABILITY

ELECTORAL        council members and Panchayat representatives face voters
LEGAL            Constitution, Schedules, PESA, Forest Rights Act, land,
                 forest and environment law, and judicial review
FISCAL           Article 275 grants, District and Regional Funds, CAG audit,
                 utilisation certification under PESA s.4(f)
ADMINISTRATIVE   Governor's para 3 report, Union directions, para 14 inquiry
                 commissions, reports laid before the State Legislature
SOCIAL           Gram Sabha proceedings, public records, grievance redress,
                 community monitoring
```

Caption: the Schedules are not a rights-free zone. Part III applies, Directive Principles orient policy, and five distinct channels can, in principle, hold the system to account.

### 1. Why Part III must be stated explicitly

A recurring error treats the Schedules as creating a separate legal order. They do not. The Fifth Schedule's paragraph 5(1) non obstante clause lets the Governor disapply or modify a **statute**; it does not disapply the Constitution. Council laws under Sixth Schedule paragraph 3 are laws, and laws are subject to Part III.

This matters in two directions, and a strong answer notices both.

- **Protection against the State.** Article 300A means deprivation of property requires authority of law; Article 21 brings livelihood, dignity and fair procedure into project decisions; Articles 25 and 26 gave the sacred-landscape claims in the Niyamgiri proceedings a constitutional footing.
- **Protection within the community.** Custom is constitutionally relevant under PESA section 4(a) and Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(1)(j), but it is not immune. A customary practice that excludes women from a Gram Sabha, or denies inheritance in a manner that violates equality, cannot be defended merely by invoking tradition. Autonomy protects a community from external domination; it does not license internal domination.

### 2. Article 46 and the orientation of policy

Article 46 directs the State to promote with special care the educational and economic interests of the weaker sections, and in particular of the Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes, and to protect them from social injustice and all forms of exploitation. It is not enforceable in itself, but it supplies the constitutional purpose against which protective regulations, grants under Article 275(1) and scheme directions under Article 339(2) are judged for reasonableness.

The analytical use is this: when a protective restriction under Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2) is challenged as an unreasonable restriction on a freedom, Article 46 and the Schedule's own purpose are what make the restriction defensible.

### 3. The commission linkage, kept in its place

Article 338A establishes the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes with investigative, inquiry, advisory and monitoring functions concerning safeguards for Scheduled Tribes, and it obliges the Union and every State Government to consult the Commission on all major policy matters affecting Scheduled Tribes. Its composition, powers, reporting obligations and institutional limits are studied in the topic on National Commissions and are not repeated here.

What belongs here is only the interface: the Commission is a **fifth watcher** alongside the Governor's report, the Union's directions, the State Legislature's scrutiny of Sixth Schedule inquiry reports, and the Gram Sabha's own record. Its recommendatory character means it strengthens the evidentiary record rather than compelling an outcome, which is exactly the pattern seen throughout this topic.

### 4. How accountability actually fails

| Failure | Institutional effect |
|---|---|
| The Tribes Advisory Council's agenda is set entirely from above | Advice becomes ceremonial, and the body cannot examine what is never referred |
| The Governor's report is not standardised or published | Union supervision proceeds without public evidence, and comparison across years is impossible |
| Council laws await assent without timelines | Autonomy becomes administrative delay, and the council loses legislative initiative in practice |
| Parallel departments retain staff and funds for entrusted functions | Councils and Panchayats hold responsibility without control |
| Gram Sabha records are poor, absent or manipulated | Participation becomes formal compliance; consultation and consent cannot be distinguished |
| Land and forest-rights records are incomplete | Rights cannot survive project appraisal, because the affected interest is undocumented |
| Restoration orders are not tracked | A prohibition becomes an unremedied illegality |

Read the table as a causal chain rather than a list. Each failure removes the **evidence** on which the next institution in the chain depends. That is why record quality is the recurring theme of this session's final lessons: in a system of reports, resolutions, assents and audits, the record *is* the accountability.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** With five channels of accountability, the real problem cannot be institutional design; it must be political will.

**Reply.** Design and will are not alternatives. Several of these channels are **discretionary in their trigger**: the Governor decides what to refer to the Tribes Advisory Council; the Union decides whether to direct; the Governor decides whether to appoint a paragraph 14 Commission. A channel that opens only when an actor chooses to open it will reflect that actor's priorities.

**Residual.** Converting discretionary channels into standing obligations — a publication requirement, a reference calendar, assent timelines, an action-taken statement — would change incentives without altering constitutional status. Whether the political system chooses to adopt such obligations is a separate question, and the honest answer is that this remains unresolved.

### UPSC application

- Name Articles 14, 19, 21, 25, 26, 29(1), 46 and 300A when asked about safeguards; do not restrict the answer to Article 46.
- Use the two-directional point — protection against the State and within the community — to avoid a one-sided answer on custom.
- Mention Article 338A as a linkage only; do not transplant the commissions topic into a Schedules answer.
- Use the five-channel framework as the skeleton for any accountability or reform question.
- Attribute failures to missing evidence rather than to general "lack of will"; it is more precise and more examinable.

### Revision notes

1. The Schedules operate inside the Constitution; Part III is not displaced.
2. Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(1) disapplies or modifies statutes, not constitutional rights.
3. Article 14: non-arbitrary administration and reasonable classification.
4. Article 19: protective restrictions on transfer, residence and occupation can be valid in Scheduled Areas.
5. Article 21: dignity, livelihood, fair procedure and the cultural conditions of life.
6. Articles 25 and 26: religious practice and sacred-landscape claims.
7. Article 29(1): conservation of distinct language, script or culture.
8. Article 46: special care for educational and economic interests of Scheduled Tribes and protection from exploitation.
9. Article 300A: deprivation of property only by authority of law.
10. Article 338A: National Commission for Scheduled Tribes, with a duty on the Union and States to consult it on major ST policy matters.
11. Custom under PESA section 4(a) and paragraph 3(1)(j) remains subject to constitutional rights and valid law.
12. Five accountability channels: electoral, legal, fiscal, administrative, social.
13. Common failures: controlled agendas, unpublished reports, assent delay, functions without staff and funds, weak Gram Sabha and land records, untracked restoration.
14. Several accountability channels are discretionary in their trigger, which is the design-level criticism.
15. In this system the record is the accountability.

### Concept check

**Question:** A council law regulating inheritance under Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(1)(h) is challenged as discriminatory against women. Can the council defend it by invoking customary practice?

**Model answer:** Not by invocation alone. A council law under paragraph 3 is a law and takes effect only on assent — the **Governor's** under paragraph 3(3), or, in Assam, the **President's**, because inheritance falls within Concurrent List Entry 5 and paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) require the Governor to reserve such a law for him. Either way it is law, and therefore subject to Part III, including Article 14 and Article 15. Paragraph 3(1)(h) and (j) make inheritance and social customs council subjects, and custom is a legitimate source of content, but the Schedule does not place council law outside constitutional rights. The defensible position is that the custom serves a legitimate protective purpose and does not deny equality; a defence resting on tradition as such will not meet the challenge.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating autonomy as immunity. Autonomy protects a community from external domination; it does not authorise discrimination within it.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "Tribal self-governance in India is rich in accountability channels and poor in accountability evidence." Examine.

#### Model solution

The channels exist. Electoral accountability runs through Sixth Schedule council elections and PESA-modified Panchayat elections. Legal accountability runs through the Constitution, the Schedules, PESA, the Forest Rights Act, land and environment law and judicial review. Fiscal accountability runs through Article 275(1) grants, District and Regional Funds, accounts prescribed and audited by the Comptroller and Auditor-General under paragraph 7, and Gram Sabha certification of fund utilisation under PESA section 4(f). Administrative accountability runs through the Governor's paragraph 3 report, Union directions, paragraph 14 inquiry commissions and reports laid before the State Legislature. Social accountability runs through Gram Sabha proceedings and community monitoring, with Article 338A adding a consultative watcher.

The evidence is the weak link. The Constitution prescribes no template, deadline or publication for the Governor's report. The Tribes Advisory Council can examine only what is referred to it. Council laws may await assent without stated timelines. Entrusted functions under paragraph 6(2) often arrive without staff or funds. Gram Sabha minutes, attendance and resolutions are often thin, so consultation cannot be distinguished from formality. Land and forest-rights records remain incomplete, so affected interests are invisible at appraisal, and restoration orders are rarely tracked.

Each failure disables the next institution, because every channel here runs on a record.

The reform agenda follows directly: standardise and publish the Governor's report; fix a reference calendar and action-taken statements for the Tribes Advisory Council; set assent timelines with reasons; map functions, staff and funds; and build privacy-sensitive, accessible land and community-forest records with tracked remedies.

**Rubric (15):** five channels named with accurate provisions 5; evidence failures identified specifically 4; causal link between missing evidence and disabled institutions 3; reform proposals matched to each failure 2; balanced conclusion 1.

### Why the next lesson follows

The diagnosis points at implementation. The next lesson moves from constitutional design to the dated status of State-level rules, capacity, convergence and delivery agencies.

---
## Lesson 19: Implementation Control: PESA Rules, Capacity, Convergence and Delivery Agencies

Progress: 19/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Dated State-rule status under PESA, the capacity and convergence agenda, and the constitutional status of delivery agencies

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity note that all ten Fifth Schedule States enacted compliance legislation by amending their Panchayati Raj Acts, which is distinct from notifying PESA Rules and is treated separately below
CA search: "status of State PESA Rules notification including Odisha and Jharkhand, Ministry of Panchayati Raj listing, 2026"
CA found: Treated as a dated implementation status check, not a current-affairs linkage. Official central material and parliamentary replies traced to 2026 show State PESA Rules notified in nine of the ten PESA States, with Odisha alone still at draft stage as recorded in a Rajya Sabha unstarred reply answered on 25 March 2026; the most recent addition is the Jharkhand PESA Rules, 2025, notified in the first week of January 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four different things that all get called "PESA implementation"

```text
LEVEL 1  CENTRAL STATUTE
         PESA, 1996 -- in force for all Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas
                |
LEVEL 2  STATE PANCHAYAT LAW CONFORMITY
         amendment of the State Panchayati Raj Act so that its Scheduled-Area
         provisions conform to section 4; all ten States have enacted
         compliance legislation of this kind
                |
LEVEL 3  STATE PESA RULES
         subordinate rules operationalising village definition, Gram Sabha
         convening, quorum, resolutions, recommendations and records
         -> rule regimes listed for NINE of the ten States
         -> Odisha recorded at DRAFT stage in official material through 2026
                |
LEVEL 4  SECTORAL LAW CONFORMITY
         excise | village markets | money-lending | land transfer
         minor minerals | minor forest produce | forest and revenue rules
         -> this is where section 4(m) actually becomes usable or fails
```

Caption: a State can sit at Level 2 and still deliver nothing at Level 4. "Rules notified" is a status, not an outcome.

### 1. Why the four levels matter more than any single number

Standard Polity sources record that all ten Fifth Schedule States enacted the requisite compliance legislation by amending their respective Panchayati Raj Acts. That is Level 2, and it is real.

Official central material and parliamentary replies traced to 2026, however, show **State PESA Rules** notified in nine of the ten States, with **Odisha** alone at draft stage — its draft rules published in the State Gazette and reported as still under consultation in a Rajya Sabha unstarred reply answered on **25 March 2026** — rather than as a finally notified instrument. The most recent addition is **Jharkhand**, whose **PESA Rules, 2025** were approved by the State Cabinet on **23 December 2025** and notified by its Panchayati Raj Department in the **first week of January 2026**, reported as extending to sixteen thousand-odd villages across thirteen fully and three partially scheduled districts.

Treat all of this as a **status check with a date**, not as a verdict. Three cautions belong in any answer that uses it.

- Notification of rules does not prove implementation.
- A State without separately notified rules is not necessarily without any PESA framework, because Level 2 conformity legislation may already operate.
- Numbers of this kind change; attach the date and the source type whenever quoting them.

### 2. Where section 4(m) actually succeeds or fails

Section 4(m) obliges the State Legislature to endow seven substantive powers. Each of them collides with a different existing State law, which is why Level 4 is decisive.

| Section 4(m) endowment | Law it must be reconciled with | Typical failure |
|---|---|---|
| Regulation of intoxicants | State excise law and licensing | Excise revenue interest outweighs the Gram Sabha's statutory power |
| Ownership of minor forest produce | Forest rules, transit permits, monopoly procurement arrangements | Ownership recognised in principle, transit and sale still controlled |
| Prevention of alienation and restoration | State land-transfer regulation, revenue law, limitation | No accessible forum, no tracking of restoration orders |
| Management of village markets | Agricultural produce market law, local-body rules | Market committee retains control of yards and fees |
| Control over money-lending | Money-lenders legislation and registration | Unregistered lending continues outside the regulated net |
| Control over social-sector institutions and functionaries | Departmental service rules and posting authority | Functionaries remain answerable upward, not to the Gram Sabha |
| Control over local plans and resources | Scheme guidelines and departmental budget lines | Plans arrive pre-designed; the Gram Sabha ratifies rather than decides |

The pattern is uniform: PESA confers a power, another statute holds the operative lever, and the statute wins in practice unless conformity is deliberately engineered. This is the single most useful analytical observation in the implementation half of this topic.

### 3. The capacity agenda

| Gap | Reform design | Evidence discipline |
|---|---|---|
| The law is inaccessible to the people it empowers | Local-language power cards keyed to section 4 clauses, and habitation maps | Cite the section, not a generic right |
| Meetings are thin or unrepresentative | Prescribed notice, quorum, timing, accessible venue, women's and vulnerable-group participation, independent facilitation | Attendance is not voice; record interventions, not headcount |
| Records are weak | Minute books, resolution formats, geo-referenced land and community-forest maps, public display | Protect privacy and customary knowledge while publishing decisions |
| Departments act in silos | A written convergence protocol across tribal welfare, Panchayats, forest, revenue and mining departments | Convergence coordinates delivery; it cannot waive a statutory right |
| The Tribes Advisory Council is dormant | Fixed calendar, published agenda, mandatory reference heads, action-taken statements | Reform reference practice; preserve its advisory character |
| Council laws await assent | Transparent timelines and recorded reasons | Retain the constitutional check; remove the silence |
| Responsibility without control | Activity, staff and fund mapping for every entrusted function | Autonomy requires implementable functions |
| Remedies are unavailable | Accessible appeal, legal aid, restoration tracking | A remedy must follow the violation, not the scheme cycle |

### 4. Delivery agencies: what they are and are not

Integrated tribal development agencies and projects, and current convergence programmes, are administrative implementation vehicles. Their form, mandate and territorial reach are **State-specific** and administrative rather than constitutional.

```text
Is it a constitutional institution?                    NO
Is it an administrative implementation vehicle?        OFTEN, AND STATE-SPECIFIC
Can it replace a Tribes Advisory Council,
  a Gram Sabha or an Autonomous District Council?      NO
Can a convergence programme waive PESA or
  forest-rights requirements?                          NO
```

The examination point is the third and fourth lines. A delivery agency may coordinate schemes and improve service delivery; it has no capacity to substitute for a constitutional or statutory decision-maker, and a programme cannot waive a statutory requirement.

A data discipline goes with this: do not quote agency counts, dashboard totals, expenditure figures or programme reach without a dated official source. Such figures move constantly and are the easiest way to introduce an unverifiable claim into an otherwise sound answer.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If both Schedules, two statutes and multiple programmes exist and outcomes still lag, the architecture itself must be over-engineered.

**Reply.** The layers answer genuinely different questions — territory, protection, self-government, participation, tenure, delivery. Collapsing them would not simplify governance; it would remove a safeguard. The measurable failures are concentrated at the conformity and record layers, not in the existence of the layers.

**Residual.** There is a real cost to complexity: an administrator in a Scheduled Area must simultaneously satisfy constitutional regulation, PESA verbs, forest-rights determination, land law and sectoral clearance, often with limited legal support. Simplification should therefore target **procedure and sequencing** — a single published compliance sequence per decision type — rather than the removal of any substantive layer.

### UPSC application

- Distinguish compliance legislation from notified rules; most answers conflate them.
- Use the nine-of-ten rule-status point with its date and its source type, and never as proof of implementation quality.
- Use the section 4(m) conformity table to explain *why* implementation fails; it converts a complaint into a mechanism.
- Treat delivery agencies as administrative; never describe them as constitutional bodies.
- Avoid undated figures entirely.

### Revision notes

1. Four levels: central statute, State Panchayat law conformity, State PESA Rules, sectoral law conformity.
2. All ten Fifth Schedule States enacted compliance legislation by amending their Panchayati Raj Acts.
3. Official central material through 2026 lists PESA Rules for nine of the ten States.
4. Odisha is recorded at draft-rules stage rather than final notification in that material.
5. Jharkhand's Rules of 2025 are the most recent addition to the central listing.
6. "Rules notified" is a status, not an outcome; always date the claim.
7. Section 4(m) powers collide with excise, forest, land, market, money-lending, service and scheme rules.
8. Conformity across those laws is what makes PESA usable.
9. Capacity gaps: inaccessible law, thin meetings, weak records, departmental silos, dormant councils, assent delay, functions without staff and funds, unavailable remedies.
10. Convergence coordinates delivery; it cannot waive a statutory right.
11. Tribal development agencies and projects are administrative and State-specific.
12. They cannot replace a Tribes Advisory Council, a Gram Sabha or an Autonomous District Council.
13. Do not quote agency counts or dashboard figures without a dated official source.
14. Simplify procedure and sequencing, not substantive layers.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State notifies PESA Rules and announces that implementation is complete. What would you still need to examine before accepting that claim?

**Model answer:** At least four things. Whether the Rules define the village in the sociological terms of section 4(b) rather than as a revenue village. Whether the State's excise, market, money-lending, land-transfer, minor-mineral and minor-forest-produce laws have been brought into conformity so that section 4(m) powers are actually exercisable. Whether Gram Sabha meetings, quorum, resolutions and records are prescribed and maintained in a form that can prove approval, consultation or recommendation. And whether remedies — appeal, legal aid and tracking of restoration orders — exist. Notification is Level 3 of a four-level problem.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a notification date as an implementation outcome. The statutory powers operate only where other laws stop blocking them and where records can prove that the statutory step occurred.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "PESA's implementation deficit is a problem of legal conformity, not of legislative intent." Critically examine.

#### Model solution

The claim is substantially correct, though incomplete.

Intent is not the gap. PESA was enacted under Article 243M(4)(b) with express objectives of self-rule, participatory village governance and subsidiarity, and section 4 states powers with unusual precision — approval of local plans under 4(e), certification of utilisation under 4(f), consultation before acquisition and rehabilitation under 4(i), mandatory recommendation for minor-mineral decisions under 4(k) and 4(l), and seven substantive endowments under 4(m). All ten Fifth Schedule States enacted compliance legislation amending their Panchayati Raj Acts.

The deficit appears where PESA meets other statutes. Ownership of minor forest produce confronts transit and procurement rules; regulation of intoxicants confronts excise licensing and revenue; market control confronts agricultural-market law; money-lending control confronts registration regimes; prevention and restoration of alienated land confront revenue procedure and limitation; and control over social-sector functionaries confronts departmental service rules. PESA names the power; another law holds the lever.

A second, record-level deficit compounds it. Where State rules define the PESA village as a revenue village, the Gram Sabha that exercises these powers is not the body the Act contemplated. Where minutes and resolutions are thin, the difference between consultation and formality cannot be demonstrated. Official central material through 2026 also lists rule regimes for nine of the ten States, with Odisha at draft stage.

The claim is therefore incomplete in one respect: conformity is necessary but not sufficient. Repair requires harmonised sectoral law, a faithful village definition, prescribed records and accessible remedies — in that order.

**Rubric (15):** statement of PESA's intent with named clauses 3; sectoral-conformity failures with concrete pairings 5; record and village-definition deficits 3; dated rule-status evidence used with caution 2; qualified verdict that refines the proposition 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

If the Sixth Schedule's elected model is the stronger form of agency, the obvious political question follows: should it be extended? The most prominent live demand gives us a disciplined way to answer.

---

## Lesson 20: Extension Debates: Ladakh, Institutional Fit and Reform Principles

Progress: 20/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The test for extending Sixth Schedule-style autonomy, the dated Ladakh position, alternative models and reform principles

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — Polity note recording Ladakh's demand for Sixth Schedule status as a live issue, used only as background; the current position is taken from the dated reporting set out below
CA search: "Ladakh Statehood and Sixth Schedule demand, Ministry of Home Affairs sub-committee talks, proposed Article 371 Chapter K, 2026"
CA found: *The Hindu*, 10 September 2026 — "MHA proposes new Chapter K under Article 371 for Ladakh", on the 9 September 2026 Delhi meeting; *The New Indian Express*, 14 September 2026 — "Ladakh groups dial down on demand for statehood"; *The New Indian Express*, 22 September 2026 — "New framework being worked out for Ladakh under Article 371: Chief Secretary"; read with *The New Indian Express*, 22 May 2026, on the in-principle understanding about Article 371-type safeguards. As on 1 October 2026 no constitutional amendment had been enacted and Ladakh had not been brought within the Sixth Schedule
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a six-question extension test

| Question | What it actually tests | Why population alone is not enough |
|---|---|---|
| Is the Scheduled Tribe population high? | Relevance of the demand | Demography establishes need, not institutional fit |
| Which concrete risks are present? | Land, employment, culture, resources, demographic change | A safeguard must be matched to a named risk |
| Is the territory compact and administrable? | Viability of a council unit | Scattered populations suit rights-based safeguards better than territorial councils |
| Do customary institutions exist to carry self-government? | Capacity of the proposed unit | A council without a social base becomes another department |
| What is the relationship with the State or Union territory legislature? | Constitutional fit | The Sixth Schedule's text names four States |
| What internal minority safeguards exist? | Protection inside the autonomous unit | Autonomy can reproduce domination at a smaller scale |

Caption: the examiner's reward is for applying a test, not for asserting a position. Every row generates a defensible paragraph.

### 1. The current Ladakh position, stated carefully

Ladakh became a Union territory **without a legislature** in 2019. Since then the Leh Apex Body and the Kargil Democratic Alliance have pressed two linked demands: **Statehood**, and inclusion of Ladakh in the **Sixth Schedule**. Around those demands a Union counter-proposal has taken shape, and by late September 2026 the parties were discussing something different from either original demand. Five stages are in play, and collapsing them is the commonest error in a Ladakh answer.

```text
WHAT LADAKH'S BODIES HAVE ASKED FOR
  Statehood (an elected Assembly with a Chief Minister)
        -> an ordinary law of Parliament under ARTICLE 3;
           Article 4(2) says such a law is NOT an
           Article 368 amendment
  + inclusion in the SIXTH SCHEDULE
        -> needs ARTICLE 244(2) altered, which paragraph 21
           cannot do (the route analysis is in section 2)
                        |
                        | not conceded
                        v
IN-PRINCIPLE UNDERSTANDING, reported 22 May 2026
  constitutional safeguards "on the lines of Article 371 A, F and G"
  as applied to Nagaland, Sikkim and Mizoram
        -> a model referenced, not a text enacted
                        |
                        v
MINISTRY PROPOSAL, reported 10 September 2026
  a DIRECTLY ELECTED UT-LEVEL BODY under a special provision
  in ARTICLE 371; reports label it a new "Chapter K" or
  "Article 371K" -- press shorthand, not enacted nomenclature
        -> would require an ARTICLE 368 amendment
                        |
                        v
WHERE THE PARTIES STOOD, reported 14 and 22 September 2026
  Ladakh groups willing to CONSIDER dropping Statehood IF the
  elected-body model meets popular aspirations; Statehood
  retained as the stated long-term aspiration
  Chief Secretary: the proposed constitutional amendment goes
  to Parliament AFTER consensus is reached on the framework
                        |
                        v
ENACTED LEGAL CHANGE -- none as on 1 October 2026
```

Caption: the vertical axis is not escalation but *legal force*. Only the last box changes anyone's rights.

✅ **Reported, 22 May 2026.** Following sub-committee talks in New Delhi, a joint statement by the Kargil Democratic Alliance and the Leh Apex Body said the two bodies had "reached an in-principle understanding with the Government of India on restoring democracy in Ladakh and providing constitutional safeguards on the lines of Article 371 A, F, and G to Ladakh as applied to Nagaland, Sikkim and Mizoram." The same statement recorded that both bodies reiterated the demand for Statehood and Sixth Schedule protections (*The New Indian Express*, 22 May 2026).

✅ **Reported, 10 September 2026.** Civil society members who attended a meeting with Ministry of Home Affairs officials in Delhi on **9 September 2026** said the Ministry had proposed an elected body for the Union territory by introducing a new special provision under **Article 371**, which the reporting describes as a proposed "Chapter K". Their statement recorded that "the intended UT-level body would be elected by direct election, and would have legislative powers with respect to land, culture and language, forest, environment, natural resources, and any other subject matter reserved for the UT under Article 240." Ladakh's Chief Secretary **Ashish Kundra** described the discussion as covering "the broad contours of a sui generis model of governance under Article 371." **Cherring Dorjay Lakruk** of the Leh Apex Body said he was disappointed because the meeting reiterated the 22 May discussion and **no detailed draft proposal** had been shared. The delegation asked that the Lieutenant Governor take no major decision on administration, bureaucracy or land until the safeguards are in place, and pressed for the legislation in the coming Winter Session; the Kargil Democratic Alliance sought its passage by a **two-thirds majority** (*The Hindu*, 10 September 2026).

Two cautions about that paragraph, both of which an examiner can test. First, "**Chapter K**" and "**Article 371K**" are the labels used in press reporting of a proposal. Part XXI of the Constitution contains **Article 371 and Articles 371A to 371J**, each a separate article carrying special provisions for a named State or region; there is no enacted provision for Ladakh and no settled nomenclature for one. Second, the phrase "any other subject matter reserved for the UT under Article 240" is quoted from the reported statement. **Article 240 does not distribute legislative subjects.** It empowers the **President** to make regulations for the peace, progress and good government of the Union territories it names, and provides that such a regulation may repeal or amend an Act of Parliament applicable to that territory and, once promulgated, has the same force and effect as an Act of Parliament applying there.

Then notice *which* Union territories it names, because this is the detail that is most often got wrong. ✅ **Article 240(1) enumerates the Andaman and Nicobar Islands; Lakshadweep; Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu; and Puducherry. Ladakh is not in that list.** The regulation-making power exists for Ladakh because an **ordinary law of Parliament put it there**: ✅ **section 58(2) of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019** provides that "the President may make regulations for the peace, progress and good government of the Union territory of Ladakh **under article 240** of the Constitution of India", and section 58(3) repeats the repeal-or-amend and same-force rule in the same terms. Section 58(1) places the territory under the President acting through a Lieutenant Governor appointed under Article 239.

Keep the two layers apart, and say which one you mean: **constitutional enumeration** in Article 240(1), and **statutory application** of that same power to Ladakh by section 58(2) of the 2019 Act. Quote the reported sentence as reported; do not convert it into a proposition about Article 240, and do not promote a statutory application into a constitutional listing.

✅ **Reported, 14 September 2026.** Ladakh's agitating bodies "indicated their willingness to consider dropping the statehood demand if the MHA's proposed model of a UT-level elected body meets the aspirations of the people." Kargil Democratic Alliance co-chairman **Sajjad Kargili** said: "We have agreed to discuss in principle this model of governance. If it is satisfactory, we will accept it. But our long-term aspiration will remain statehood, which is also reflected in the recent minutes of meeting with MHA" (*The New Indian Express*, 14 September 2026).

✅ **Reported, 22 September 2026.** Chief Secretary Ashish Kundra said "a new framework is being worked out under Article 371 to provide constitutional safeguards suited to the specific requirements of Ladakh," covering land, employment, culture, environment and natural resources, and that direct elections to the proposed UT-level body were among the key suggestions from the Ministry–Ladakh discussions. He added that "further steps, including consideration of the proposed constitutional amendment by Parliament, would follow after consensus is reached on the framework" (*The New Indian Express*, 22 September 2026).

✅ **Position as on 1 October 2026.** Statehood had not been granted, no constitutional amendment had been enacted, and **Ladakh had not been brought within the Sixth Schedule**. The demand for Sixth Schedule status had not been withdrawn; what the reporting records is a willingness to consider accepting an elected-body model instead, conditional on its contents.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, and why the demand set is harder than it looks.** Reporting of the two-thirds demand is intelligible once Article 368(2) is read: an amendment of the Constitution must be passed in each House by a majority of the total membership of that House **and** by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting. But notice that the three demands do not share one road. **Statehood** is reachable by an ordinary law of Parliament under Article 3, which Article 4(2) expressly declares not to be a constitutional amendment. A **new Article 371-type special provision** sits in Part XXI and could only be made under Article 368 — which is exactly what the two-thirds request, and the Chief Secretary's reported reference to a proposed constitutional amendment, point towards. **Sixth Schedule inclusion** turns on Article 244(2), and section 2 works out precisely what that takes. So the honest contrast is not "concession versus amendment" and not "cheap versus costly"; it is a question of *which text has to move*, and each demand moves a different one. An answer that treats "Sixth Schedule status" and "Article 371-type safeguards" as interchangeable has missed the mechanics; so has an answer that assumes every demand on the table needs the same majority.

Two disciplines follow. Present the position as dated and attributed. And keep five stages apart: a **demand**, a **dialogue**, an **in-principle understanding**, a **proposal**, and an **enacted legal change**. Only the last alters anyone's rights.

### 2. What would actually be required

This is where most answers become vague, and where precision earns marks. It is also where the commonest textbook error lives, so work it slowly.

Start from the text that governs the Schedule's reach. **Article 244(2)** reads: "The provisions of the Sixth Schedule shall apply to the administration of the tribal areas in the States of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram." The Schedule's own heading repeats the limit — it is subscribed "[Articles 244(2) and 275(1)]" and titled "Provisions as to the Administration of Tribal Areas in the States of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram."

Now read **paragraph 21** beside it. Paragraph 21(1) lets Parliament "from time to time by law amend by way of addition, variation or repeal any of the provisions of **this Schedule**", and 21(2) says such a law is not an amendment of the Constitution for the purposes of Article 368. The object of that power is the **Schedule**. It is not Article 244(2), which sits in Part X of the Constitution proper. That is the first boundary, and it is the one most answers miss: **paragraph 21 cannot put a new name into Article 244(2).**

The second boundary is the one textbooks get wrong in the opposite direction. It does **not** follow that Article 244(2) can be touched only by Article 368. The clause has in fact been altered three times, and only one of those was a constitutional amendment.

```text
            ARTICLE 244(2)  -- in Part X of the Constitution
            names the units to whose tribal areas
            the Sixth Schedule applies
                 |                          |
                 |  altered by an           |  altered by a
                 |  ARTICLE 368             |  reorganisation law
                 |  amendment               |  under ARTICLES 2-4
                 |  (1984, Tripura)         |  (1971 and 1986)
                 |                          |  Article 4(2): NOT an
                 |                          |  Article 368 amendment
                 +------------+-------------+
                              |
                              |  NEVER by paragraph 21
                              v
            THE SIXTH SCHEDULE  -- internal machinery
            paragraph 20 table, councils, powers, brakes
                        |
                        |  rewritten by SEVERAL KINDS of instrument
                        |  (verified families, not a closed list)
                        v
            on its OWN occasion, under PARAGRAPH 21:
              1988  Tripura and Mizoram - paragraphs 9, 10, 15 and 16
                    varied in their application, paragraph 20BB
                    inserted, and Part III serial entries 2 and 3 of
                    the paragraph 20 table substituted
              1995  Assam - paragraph 3A inserted, paragraph 3(3)
                    substituted, paragraph 12(1)(a) widened,
                    paragraph 14(2) thinned, paragraph 20BA inserted,
                    and the two Councils renamed by a proviso to 2(3)
              2003  Assam - Bodoland entry in the paragraph 20 table,
                    Council created under provisos to 2(3) and 2(1),
                    paragraph 3B inserted, paragraph 3(3) substituted
                    again, paragraph 12(1)(a) extended, paragraph
                    19(4) inserted, and paragraphs 1(2), 4, 10 and 17
                    switched off for Bodoland

            inside a LARGER OPERATION, by that instrument's own sections:
              1969  Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act (55 of 1969),
                    s. 74 and Fourth Schedule - paragraphs 2(1) and 6
                    substituted            (w.e.f. 2 April 1970)
              1971  North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act (81 of
                    1971), s. 71(i) and Eighth Schedule - paragraph 18
                    OMITTED, paragraph 12A substituted
                                           (w.e.f. 21 January 1972)
              1971  Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act
                    (83 of 1971), s. 13 - paragraph 20A substituted
                    by the present paragraphs 20A, 20B and 20C, and
                    "The Mizo District" omitted from Part III
              1984  Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, s. 4 -
                    Part IIA entry, paragraph 20(3), paragraph 12AA
                                           (w.e.f. 1 April 1985)
              2016  Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment)
                    Act, s. 16 - paragraph 8(3), clause (e) on
                    entertainment and amusements inserted
                                           (w.e.f. 16 September 2016)

            by EXECUTIVE ORDER, where the Schedule itself authorises it:
              paragraph 1(3), second proviso - a Governor's
                    reorganisation order may carry incidental and
                    consequential provisions "including any amendment
                    of paragraph 20 and of any item in any of the
                    Parts of the said table"
              paragraph 20B(1)(a) - the Administrator's order
                    directing consequential amendments in paragraph 20
                    and in Part III of its table; used in fact by the
                    Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous
                    Provisions) Order, 1972, which inserted the three
                    Part III districts (entries 2 and 3 later
                    substituted in 1988)
                                           (w.e.f. 29 April 1972)

            what NONE of these can do:
              add a unit to ARTICLE 244(2)
```

Caption: two different texts, and on the lower text a family of instruments rather than a single route. Ordinary amending laws, a law forming an autonomous State, two reorganisation-type laws, two Constitution Amendment Acts and even executive orders have each altered the Schedule's contents; nothing in the Schedule reaches the provision that decides where the Schedule applies at all.

Read that lower box as a verified inventory, not as a closed list. These are the instruments this lesson has checked against the Schedule's own amendment footnotes and against the amending Acts themselves; the Schedule has been worked on often, and the point is the *variety* of vehicles, not a count.

Three lines of it are worth pausing on, because they are routinely misattributed. First, the adaptations of 1972 — the omission of paragraph 18, the substitution of paragraph 12A — were **not** made under paragraph 21. They were made by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 through its own section 71(i) and Eighth Schedule, the same instrument and the same commencement date that rewrote Article 244(2) itself. Second, the earliest change of all came from the law that formed an autonomous State: section 74 and the Fourth Schedule of the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 substituted paragraph 2(1) — the "not more than thirty members, not more than four nominated" formula every student memorises — and paragraph 6, with effect from 2 April 1970. Third, the executive-order branch is not theoretical. The three districts that now stand in Part III of the paragraph 20 table for Mizoram were put there by the **Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972**, published in the Mizoram Gazette of 5 May 1972, with effect from 29 April 1972 — an Administrator's order under paragraph 20B(1)(a), amending a table inside the Constitution without any Act at all.

A caution on how to describe them. Each of those five parliamentary instruments is a law of Parliament, and this edition names the **instrument that carried the change**, not the enabling power it was referred to; nothing in this topic turns on whether a particular section was traced to paragraph 21 or to the Act's own enabling provision. What is examinable is the pattern: paragraph 21 is the standing power Parliament has used on its own occasions, in 1988, 1995 and 2003; a larger statutory operation carries its consequential Schedule drafting with it, which is as true of a Goods and Services Tax amendment in 2016 as of a reorganisation in 1971; and the Schedule even licenses executive orders to amend its own table. The amending instrument follows the occasion, while the one fixed limit never moves: no amendment of the Schedule, by whichever instrument, can enlarge Article 244(2).

✅ **The amendment history of Article 244(2), from the amendment footnotes to that article.**

| Effective from | Instrument | What it did to Article 244(2) | Nature of the law |
|---|---|---|---|
| 21 January 1972 | North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 (81 of 1971), section 71 | Substituted "the State of Assam", so the clause named the States of Assam and Meghalaya **and the Union territory of Mizoram** | Ordinary law under Articles 3 and 4 |
| 1 April 1985 | Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984, section 2 | Substituted "and Meghalaya", adding **Tripura** to the named units | Constitutional amendment under Article 368 |
| 20 February 1987 | State of Mizoram Act, 1986 (34 of 1986), section 39 | Substituted "Meghalaya and Tripura and the Union territory of Mizoram" with "Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram" after Mizoram became a State | Ordinary law under Articles 3 and 4 |

Caption: the clause that names where the Sixth Schedule applies has been amended more often by ordinary reorganisation law than by constitutional amendment.

Notice the division of labour inside the 1984 Act, because it is the clearest illustration of the two-text point. **Section 2** of the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 did the work in Article 244(2), adding Tripura to the named units. **Section 4** of the same Act did the consequential work inside the Sixth Schedule — the Part IIA entry in the paragraph 20 table and the related paragraph 20(3), together with paragraph 12AA, which fixes how Acts of Parliament and of the Tripura Legislature apply there. One Act, two sections, two different texts, because the Constitution proper and the Schedule are amended by different operations.

✅ The enabling text is **Article 4**. A law made under Article 2 or Article 3 "shall contain such provisions for the amendment of the First Schedule and the Fourth Schedule as may be necessary to give effect to the provisions of the law and may also contain such **supplemental, incidental and consequential provisions** … as Parliament may deem necessary"; and under Article 4(2), "No such law as aforesaid shall be deemed to be an amendment of this Constitution for the purposes of Article 368."

Why did 1971 and 1986 travel that road while 1984 did not? Because the first two were genuinely **consequential**: in each, the tribal areas concerned were already inside the Schedule's field, so the amendment to Article 244(2) kept them where they already were. But the unit they were inside was not the same unit on the two occasions, and the difference is examinable.

- In **1971** they were inside as parts of **Assam**. Carving the new units out of Assam required the clause to name them separately, so section 71 substituted "the State of Assam" with wording that named the States of Assam and Meghalaya **and the Union territory of Mizoram**.
- In **1986** the areas were already inside in their own right, **as the Union territory of Mizoram** — a unit Article 244(2) had itself named since 1972. When that Union territory became a State, section 39 simply updated the description, substituting "Meghalaya and Tripura and the Union territory of Mizoram" with "Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram".

Hold the consequence steady, because it does real work in Lesson 20's argument: for fifteen years, from 1972 to 1987, **Article 244(2) named a Union territory**. The Sixth Schedule has already operated in a Union territory, and the clause fixing its reach was adjusted for that by an ordinary reorganisation law.

Tripura is the different case, and it is the case Ladakh resembles. Tripura's tribal areas had **never** been within the Schedule's field; Tripura had already become a State in 1972 and no reorganisation was under way in 1984. For a first-time extension, with no reorganisation to be consequential upon, Parliament used **Article 368**.

Now apply all of that to Ladakh, demand by demand.

- **Statehood** does not need Article 368 at all. Article 3(a) lets Parliament by law form a new State, and **Explanation I** to that article provides that in clauses (a) to (e) the word "State" **includes a Union territory**. Ladakh is therefore within reach of an ordinary Article 3 law, which Article 4(2) declares not to be a constitutional amendment. One further detail in the proviso repays attention: the requirement that the President refer the Bill to the State Legislature for its views applies to a "State", and the proviso expressly says that there "State" does **not** include a Union territory — so for Ladakh only the President's recommendation would precede introduction.
- **Sixth Schedule inclusion** cannot be achieved by paragraph 21. It requires Article 244(2) to be altered. Outside a valid reorganisation law under Articles 2 to 4, that means **Article 368**, and the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 is the precedent for doing it that way. Either route then needs consequential Schedule work — an entry in the paragraph 20 table and adaptation of machinery that speaks of a Governor and a State Legislature. That work is not tied to paragraph 21: it is carried by whichever instrument is doing the job, either a separate amending law under paragraph 21 or the enacting Act's own sections, as happened in 1969, twice in 1971 and again in 1984.
- **An Article 371-type special provision** would sit in **Part XXI** of the Constitution, which no ordinary law can reach. That route requires Article 368. It is the shape of the reported September 2026 proposal, and it fits the Chief Secretary's reported statement that consideration of the proposed constitutional amendment by Parliament would follow consensus on the framework.

⚠️ **A bounded constitutional inference, offered as such and not as a rule.** Could a future Ladakh Statehood or reorganisation law itself amend Article 244(2), and so bring Ladakh within the Sixth Schedule without a separate Article 368 amendment? The 1971 and 1986 instruments prove that a reorganisation law *can* amend Article 244(2). But in both, the tribal areas concerned were already inside the Schedule, so the amendment preserved an existing application rather than creating a new one. Whether "supplemental, incidental and consequential" in Article 4(1) stretches to a **first-time** extension of the Schedule to territory it has never covered is not settled by those precedents, and this edition takes no position on it. Carry it as an argument that can be made and must be flagged, never as a stated route.

⚠️ **A bounded inference about the threshold.** Neither Article 244 nor the Sixth Schedule appears in the enumerated list in the proviso to Article 368(2) — the list that triggers ratification by the legislatures of not less than one-half of the States. Read on its face, an amendment of Article 244(2) would therefore need the special majority in each House but not State ratification. State this as a reading of the enumerated list, not as settled authority.

```text
DEMAND
   |
DIALOGUE / COMMITTEE PROCESS
   |
POLICY DECISION
   |
ENACTED LEGAL CHANGE  <-- the only step that changes legal status
   |
   +-- Statehood route
   |     an ordinary law of Parliament under ARTICLE 3,
   |     reaching a Union territory through Explanation I;
   |     Article 4(2) -- not an Article 368 amendment
   |
   +-- Sixth Schedule route
   |     ARTICLE 244(2) must be altered -- paragraph 21
   |     cannot do it. Outside a valid Articles 2-4
   |     reorganisation law that means ARTICLE 368.
   |     PLUS consequential Schedule amendments
   |     (paragraph 20 table entry, machinery
   |     adaptation), carried either by a separate
   |     paragraph 21 law or by the enacting Act's
   |     own sections
   |     precedent: Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act,
   |     1984, for Tripura
   |
   +-- Article 371-type route
   |     a new special provision in PART XXI, beyond the
   |     reach of ordinary law: ARTICLE 368 required
   |     this is the shape of the reported September 2026
   |     proposal
   |
   +-- Statutory route
         strengthened elected hill councils, land and employment
         legislation, cultural and language guarantees, or a
         tailored parliamentary framework -- ordinary legislation,
         therefore faster and more easily reversed
```

Two technical observations give an answer unusual authority.

First, the four routes are not graded by difficulty in the way most answers assume. Statehood and the statutory package are ordinary legislation; the Sixth Schedule and Article 371-type routes reach the Constitution proper. And between those last two the difference is substantive rather than procedural: the Sixth Schedule brings a **tested package** — an elected District Council with defined legislative, executive, judicial, revenue and fiscal powers, and defined gubernatorial brakes — whereas an Article 371-type provision is drafted from scratch and can be shaped to the territory, including in ways that give less than a council.

Second, the Schedule contains drafting technique for a **Union territory**, and it is worth knowing exactly how much that proves. Paragraph 20C provides that in its application to the then Union territory of Mizoram, references to the Governor and the Government of the State are read as references to the Administrator appointed under Article 239, references to the State as references to that Union territory, and references to the State Legislature as references to its Legislative Assembly, with consequential modifications to paragraphs 4(5), 6(2) and 13.

Read that for what it is. Paragraph 20C is an **adaptation clause, not an extension route**. It operated while the Union territory of Mizoram was itself named in Article 244(2), and it answered a drafting question — how does machinery written for a State read when the unit is a Union territory — rather than a jurisdictional one. It could not, and did not, bring any territory within Article 244(2); that had already been done by section 71 of the 1971 Act. What it does establish, usefully for Ladakh, is that the Schedule's machinery has been made to work in a Union territory at least once. What it does not supply is entitlement, and it assumed a **Legislative Assembly**, which Ladakh does not have.

### 3. Weighing the models

| Model | What it would give | What it would not solve |
|---|---|---|
| Sixth Schedule council | Elected territorial government with law-making on land, forests and custom; village courts; land revenue and specified taxes; an agreed royalty share; budget visibility under paragraph 13 | Governor or Administrator assent, subject-bounded competence, capacity and staffing, and internal minority representation |
| Article 371-type provision | A State or territory-level consent shield over custom, land and resources, entrenched by constitutional amendment | No elected territorial council, no local legislative or fiscal machinery |
| Strengthened statutory hill councils | Faster to enact; can transfer functions, staff and funds; can be tailored to two districts with different demographics | Lower entrenchment, reversible by ordinary legislation, and no constitutional floor |
| Rights-based legislation on land, employment and language | Addresses the named risks directly and operates irrespective of territorial form | Does not create democratic self-government |

The analytically strongest position is not "grant it" or "refuse it." It is that the choice should follow **institutional fit against named risks**, and that the Sixth Schedule is one instrument among several — a point that holds equally for other regions pressing comparable demands.

### 4. Reform principles for the whole topic

```text
1  TERRITORIAL ACCURACY     publish current Presidential Orders, council
                            boundaries and habitation maps in accessible form
2  SUBSIDIARITY             decide at the lowest competent level; enforce
                            PESA section 4(n) in State law
3  LEGALLY PRECISE          use the exact statutory verb at each decision
   PARTICIPATION            stage; stop treating consultation as consent
4  SECURE RECORDS           geo-referenced, privacy-sensitive land and
                            community-forest records
5  ACCOUNTABLE SUPERVISION  standardise and publish the Governor's report;
                            reasoned disposal of advice; assent timelines
6  COUNCIL CAPACITY         activity, staff and fund mapping for every
                            entrusted function
7  RIGHTS-RESPECTING        rights determination -> statutory participation ->
   SEQUENCING               appraisal -> reasoned decision -> monitored
                            rehabilitation and restoration
8  TRANSPARENT REVIEW       accessible appeal, legal aid and tracked remedies
```

The organising idea is a single sentence worth memorising: **reform should convert protection into agency without romanticising custom or weakening constitutional rights**.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Extending the Sixth Schedule to new regions would fragment administration and multiply demands for parity.

**Reply.** The Schedule is already asymmetric within its four States, through the 1988, 1995 and 2003 amendments, and asymmetry has coexisted with national integration for decades. Parity demands arise from unexplained differentiation, not from differentiation as such; a stated, revisable reason reduces rather than increases that pressure.

**Residual.** The objection retains force where a council would be created without a viable revenue base, without administrative capacity, or without safeguards for groups who would become minorities inside the new unit. Those are the conditions on which extension should turn, and they are empirical questions that a general constitutional argument cannot settle.

### UPSC application

- Always date the Ladakh position, name the outlet and date you are relying on, and label it as reported rather than enacted change.
- Use the five-stage ladder — demand, dialogue, in-principle understanding, proposal, enacted legal change — to avoid overstating the current position.
- Cite Article 244(2) to show that the Sixth Schedule route needs that clause altered and that paragraph 21 cannot do it; name the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 for Tripura as the Article 368 precedent, and the 1971 and 1986 reorganisation laws as the cases where ordinary legislation amended the same clause consequentially.
- Separate the demands by instrument: Statehood under Article 3 with Article 4(2); Sixth Schedule inclusion through Article 244(2); an Article 371-type provision through Part XXI and Article 368.
- Use paragraph 20C carefully, as adaptation technique rather than a route of entry.
- Answer "should it be extended" with a test, alternatives and a conditional verdict; never with a slogan.

### Revision notes

1. Ladakh became a Union territory without a legislature in 2019.
2. The Leh Apex Body and the Kargil Democratic Alliance couple Statehood with Sixth Schedule inclusion.
3. 22 May 2026: a joint LAB-KDA statement after sub-committee talks recorded an in-principle understanding with the Government of India on restoring democracy and on safeguards "on the lines of Article 371 A, F, and G" as applied to Nagaland, Sikkim and Mizoram, while reiterating the Statehood and Sixth Schedule demands.
4. 9 September 2026: the Ministry of Home Affairs proposed a directly elected Union-territory body under a new special provision in Article 371, which reporting labels a "Chapter K" or "Article 371K"; the civil-society statement recorded legislative powers over land, culture and language, forest, environment, natural resources "and any other subject matter reserved for the UT under Article 240".
5. "Chapter K" is press shorthand. Part XXI contains Article 371 and Articles 371A to 371J as separate articles; none exists for Ladakh. Article 240 does not distribute legislative subjects — it empowers the President to make regulations for the Union territories it enumerates, and **Ladakh is not enumerated in Article 240(1)**, which names the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu, and Puducherry. The power reaches Ladakh through **section 58(2) of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019**: a statutory application, not a constitutional listing.
6. No detailed draft was shared; the delegation asked that the Lieutenant Governor defer major decisions and sought legislation in the Winter Session by a two-thirds majority, which matches the Article 368(2) special majority.
7. 14 September 2026: the Ladakh bodies indicated willingness to consider dropping Statehood if the proposed elected-body model meets popular aspirations, with Statehood retained as the stated long-term aspiration.
8. 22 September 2026: the Chief Secretary said a new framework under Article 371 was being worked out and that consideration of the proposed constitutional amendment by Parliament would follow consensus on the framework.
9. As on 1 October 2026 Statehood was not granted, no constitutional amendment was enacted, and Ladakh had not been brought within the Sixth Schedule.
10. Distinguish five stages: demand, dialogue, in-principle understanding, proposal, enacted legal change.
11. **Article 244(2) names Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.** Paragraph 21 lets Parliament amend the Schedule by ordinary law, but its object is the Schedule alone; it cannot add a unit to Article 244(2), which sits in Part X of the Constitution.
12. Article 244(2) has itself been altered three times: by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 (81 of 1971), section 71, with effect from 21 January 1972; by the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984, **section 2**, with effect from 1 April 1985, adding Tripura — while **section 4** of that Act made the consequential Schedule insertions, Part IIA of the paragraph 20 table with paragraph 20(3), and paragraph 12AA; and by the State of Mizoram Act, 1986 (34 of 1986), section 39, with effect from 20 February 1987. The two reorganisation amendments were ordinary laws under Articles 3 and 4, which Article 4(2) declares not to be Article 368 amendments; both were consequential, because the areas were already inside the Schedule — in 1971 as parts of Assam, and in 1986 as the Union territory of Mizoram, which Article 244(2) had itself named since 1972. Tripura was a first-time extension with no reorganisation under way, and needed Article 368.
13. From 1972 to 1987 Article 244(2) named the **Union territory of Mizoram**. The Sixth Schedule has operated in a Union territory. Paragraph 20C adapted its machinery for that setting — Governor read as the Administrator under Article 239, State as the Union territory, State Legislature as its Legislative Assembly, with modifications to paragraphs 4(5), 6(2) and 13 — but paragraph 20C is adaptation, not a route of entry, and it assumed a Legislative Assembly.
14. Separate the three demands by instrument. Statehood: an ordinary law under Article 3, reaching a Union territory through Explanation I, with no reference to a State Legislature because the proviso excludes a Union territory. Sixth Schedule inclusion: Article 244(2) must be altered, which outside a valid Articles 2 to 4 law means Article 368, plus consequential Schedule work carried either by a separate paragraph 21 law or by the enacting Act's own sections. An Article 371-type provision: Part XXI, therefore Article 368. The Schedule's contents have in fact been rewritten by several kinds of instrument, not by paragraph 21 alone: paragraph 21 on its own occasions in 1988, 1995 and 2003; the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969, section 74 and Fourth Schedule; the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971, section 71(i) and Eighth Schedule; the Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971, section 13; section 4 of the Forty-ninth Amendment and section 16 of the One Hundred and First Amendment of 2016; and executive orders authorised by the second proviso to paragraph 1(3) and by paragraph 20B(1)(a), the latter used by the Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972.
15. Six-question extension test: population, named risks, territorial compactness, customary institutions, relation with the legislature, internal minority safeguards. Alternatives: an Article 371-type shield, strengthened statutory hill councils, or rights-based land, employment and language legislation. Reform principles run from territorial accuracy to transparent review with remedies, and the core sentence is to convert protection into agency without romanticising custom or weakening constitutional rights.

### Concept check

**Question:** What exactly would have to happen for a Union territory to come under the Sixth Schedule, and what does paragraph 20C add to the discussion?

**Model answer:** **Article 244(2)** would have to be altered, because that is the clause applying the Sixth Schedule to the tribal areas of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. Paragraph 21 cannot do it: it lets Parliament amend the Schedule by ordinary law, but the Schedule is its only object. Article 244(2) has been altered both ways — by **Article 368** in the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 for Tripura, and by ordinary reorganisation law under Articles 3 and 4 in 1971 and 1986, where the change was consequential on areas already inside the Schedule. For a territory the Schedule has never covered, and outside such a reorganisation law, the route is Article 368, followed by consequential Schedule amendments adding the territory to the paragraph 20 table and adapting machinery that speaks of a Governor and a State Legislature. Paragraph 20C adds technique, not entry: it told the reader how the Schedule reads for the Union territory of Mizoram, which Article 244(2) then named, and it assumed a Legislative Assembly.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that paragraph 21 alone can extend the Schedule to a new State or Union territory because it is "not an Article 368 amendment". Paragraph 21 governs the Schedule's contents, not Article 244(2), which fixes where the Schedule applies. The opposite error is just as costly — assuming Article 244(2) can move only under Article 368, when reorganisation laws amended it twice. A third error is treating a dialogue, an in-principle understanding or a ministerial proposal as a change in legal status; only an enacted law does that.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** Should Sixth Schedule-style autonomy be extended to Ladakh or other tribal regions? Critically examine, and propose a decision framework.

#### Model solution

Extension should follow institutional fit against named risks, not population alone.

The case is real. A Sixth Schedule council gives elected territorial government with law-making on land, non-reserved forests, water, cultivation, village administration, customary leadership, inheritance, marriage and social customs; village courts under paragraph 4 between Scheduled Tribe parties; listed services under paragraph 6; land revenue and taxes under paragraph 8; a royalty share under paragraph 9; and budget visibility under paragraph 13. It is not cheap: Article 244(2) names the units covered and paragraph 21 cannot add to that list, so a territory never inside the Schedule needs Article 368 plus consequential Schedule changes, as the Forty-ninth Amendment did for Tripura in 1984.

The limits are real. Competence is subject-bounded and assent-dependent. Capacity, staffing and revenue decide whether the form delivers, and council boundaries can create new minorities. Ladakh has no legislature, strategic and ecological constraints and two districts of differing demography.

Weigh alternatives on the same axes: an Article 371-type consent shield, strengthened hill councils with transferred functions, staff and funds, or targeted land, employment and language legislation.

Ask six questions: territorial compactness; customary institutions; risks addressed; consent of affected groups; fiscal and administrative viability; and safeguards for internal minorities.

As on 1 October 2026 Statehood and Sixth Schedule inclusion remained unconceded; the reported proposal was a directly elected Union-territory body under a new Article 371 provision, which lies in Part XXI and so needs Article 368, unlike Statehood. Choose by consultation and enacted design.

**Rubric (20):** accurate statement of what a Sixth Schedule council would deliver 5; Article 244(2), the amendment route and the Tripura precedent 3; limits including territorial text, capacity and internal minorities 4; alternatives weighed on common axes 3; six-question decision framework 3; dated and correctly qualified current status 2.

### Why the next lesson follows

Everything taught so far must now become usable under examination conditions. The final lesson converts the topic into traps, a paragraph method and a thesis selector.
