## Lesson 21: Prelims Trap Board, Mains Method and Thesis Selection

Progress: 21/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Consolidated reversals, the evidence-led paragraph method, demand-to-thesis mapping and the topic's closing synthesis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried — the standard trap lists in Polity chapters on this topic, consolidated and corrected against the provisions taught in Lessons 1 to 20
CA search: "examination-relevant change in Scheduled Areas or Sixth Schedule law requiring revision of standard answer positions, April–October 2026"
CA found: None in the stated window beyond the dated Ladakh dialogue recorded in Lesson 20 and the PESA rule-status check in Lesson 19, both of which are already incorporated
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the reversal board

| Tempting proposition | Accurate position |
|---|---|
| The Fifth Schedule governs the north-eastern tribal areas | Those are the Sixth Schedule's field under Article 244(2); Fifth Schedule paragraph 1 defines "State" to exclude them |
| The Governor declares Scheduled Areas | The President declares under paragraph 6(1); the Governor is consulted for enlargement and redefinition |
| The Governor notifies Scheduled Tribes | The President specifies under Article 342(1); Parliament includes or excludes by law |
| The Tribes Advisory Council legislates | It advises on matters referred to it by the Governor |
| The council has exactly 20 members | Not more than twenty, with three-fourths as nearly as may be from ST representatives in the Assembly |
| Declaring an area Scheduled suspends the State's executive reach | Paragraph 2 preserves it; paragraph 3 adds a reporting duty and a Union power of direction |
| Paragraph 3 is a power of administration in Union hands | It is a power to **direct** the State as to administration; the Schedule provides no assumption of administration |
| The Chief Minister reports on Scheduled Area administration | Paragraph 3 places the duty on the **Governor**, and the report goes to the **President** |
| A Fifth Schedule regulation needs the Governor's assent | It needs the President's assent under paragraph 5(4) |
| A Sixth Schedule council law needs the President's assent | It needs the Governor's assent under paragraph 3(3) — unless it is an Assam council law relating to a Concurrent List matter, which the Governor reserves for the President |
| Every Autonomous District Council has 30 elected members | Not more than thirty in total, with not more than four nominated |
| The Bodoland design applies to all councils | It is a special arrangement created by the 2003 amendment |
| An Autonomous District Council controls all forests | Paragraph 3 excludes reserved forests, defined in paragraph 3(2) |
| An Autonomous District Council owns the minerals | Paragraph 9 gives an agreed share of royalties, with the Governor settling disputes finally |
| Central and State laws never apply in Sixth Schedule areas unless the council agrees | Application rules differ by State under paragraphs 12, 12A, 12AA and 12B |
| PESA applies to Sixth Schedule areas | PESA applies to Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas; Article 243M(4)(b) would have permitted more |
| PESA requires consent for every project | Verbs vary: approve, identify, certify, consult, recommend, endow |
| PESA covers all minerals | Sections 4(k) and 4(l) speak of **minor** minerals |
| The Niyamgiri judgment made the Gram Sabha the final clearance authority | The Ministry retained the final Stage-II decision, taken in the light of Gram Sabha decisions |
| *Samatha* banned private mining in every Scheduled Area nationwide | It invalidated the impugned leases under the Andhra Pradesh regulation; wider reach has been debated |
| Article 371 provisions are a form of Sixth Schedule autonomy | They are distinct Part XXI techniques; Mizoram holds both |
| Article 244A describes an existing autonomous council | It is an enabling power for an autonomous State within Assam, used once for Meghalaya between 1970 and 1972 and dormant since |
| Amending either Schedule requires Article 368 | Paragraph 7 and paragraph 21 allow ordinary parliamentary law — but only over the Schedules themselves; Article 244(2) still fixes which States the Sixth Schedule reaches |
| Paragraph 21 could extend the Sixth Schedule to a new State or Union territory | Paragraph 21's object is the Schedule; Article 244(2) names the units covered and lies outside its reach. Lesson 20 sets out how that clause has actually been altered |
| Paragraph 16 operates identically in all four States | For Tripura and Mizoram the 1988 amendment removed the previous-approval requirement and the second proviso, and replaced 16(3) with a laying duty |
| Paragraph 15 works the same way everywhere | For Tripura and Mizoram the 1988 amendment put revocation in the Governor's hands instead of the State Legislature's and omitted the renewal proviso |
| Paragraph 10 always regulates "non-tribals" | That is its Assam and Meghalaya form. For Tripura and Mizoram the 1988 amendment dropped the tribal/non-tribal limitation, and paragraph 10(4) excludes the Bodoland Territorial Council altogether |
| Every Sixth Schedule council runs village courts under paragraph 4 | Paragraph 4(6) switches the whole paragraph off for the Bodoland Territorial Council |
| Council laws always take the Governor's assent | In Assam, paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) require the Governor to reserve a council law relating to a Concurrent List matter for the President |

### 1. Why these particular errors recur

Three structural features of the topic generate almost every trap.

- **Mirror institutions.** President and Governor, declaration and organisation, advice and legislation, Presidential assent and gubernatorial assent — each pair appears in both Schedules with the roles reversed. The mind retains the pair and loses the direction.
- **Qualifying adjectives.** "Reserved" forest, "minor" minerals, "not more than" thirty, "agreed" share, "as nearly as may be" three-fourths. Dropping one adjective flips the proposition.
- **Verb intensity.** Consult, recommend, approve, certify, own, restore. The statute varies the verb deliberately; summaries flatten them into "powers."

The remedy is not more memorisation. It is to rehearse each pair as a **directional sentence** — "Fifth Schedule regulation, President's assent; Sixth Schedule council law, Governor's assent, and in Assam a Concurrent-List council law back to the President" — because direction, not content, is what fails under time pressure.

### 2. The evidence-led paragraph

```text
CLAIM
  -> NAMED CONSTITUTIONAL, STATUTORY OR JUDICIAL EVIDENCE
       -> WHAT THE MECHANISM ACTUALLY DOES
            -> WHY IT MATTERS
                 -> IMPLEMENTATION OR DOCTRINAL QUALIFICATION
                      -> MINI-VERDICT
```

Worked example:

- **Claim.** PESA is stronger than generic consultation.
- **Evidence.** Section 4(e) requires Gram Sabha approval of local plans, programmes and projects before implementation; section 4(i) uses consultation for acquisition and rehabilitation; sections 4(k) and 4(l) make recommendations mandatory for specified minor-mineral decisions.
- **Mechanism.** Parliament attached a different legal consequence to each decision type.
- **Why it matters.** The community's power is strongest where the decision is wholly local and calibrated where other legal interests are engaged.
- **Qualification.** It did not enact a universal veto, and section 4(i) expressly keeps project planning and implementation coordinated at State level.
- **Mini-verdict.** Legal precision strengthens rather than weakens the case for meaningful participation.

Five or six paragraphs of that shape, plus an opening thesis and a closing verdict, is a complete 15-mark answer.

### 3. Demand-to-thesis selector

| If the demand is | Use this defensible thesis |
|---|---|
| Rationale for special administration | Differentiated administration is substantive equality, legitimate only while it converts protection into agency |
| The Fifth Schedule evaluated | Strong supervisory and protective powers are weakened by opaque reporting, controlled council agendas and implementation dormancy |
| The Sixth Schedule evaluated | Councils create genuine legislative, executive, judicial and fiscal autonomy, which remains subject-specific, assent-dependent and capacity-constrained |
| Comparison of the two | The Fifth gives a constitutional guardian; the Sixth gives a bounded territorial government — import the Sixth's agency without claiming either is complete |
| PESA | Section 4 is a calibrated code of community power, not a universal Gram Sabha veto |
| Forest Rights Act linkage | Tenure recognition and community forest governance are what make participation materially meaningful |
| Land, mining and displacement | Lawful development requires cumulative compliance; no clearance substitutes for another |
| Accountability | The architecture is rich in channels and poor in evidence; records are the binding constraint |
| Extension debates | Choose by institutional fit against named risks, through enacted design rather than analogy |
| Reform | Transparency, records, capacity and remedy are what connect constitutional text to community power |

### 4. Mark-scaled architecture

| Marks | Structure | Evidence load |
|---|---|---|
| 10 | Thesis, one institutional axis, one qualification, verdict | Three units, at least one Schedule paragraph or statutory section |
| 15 | Thesis, Article 244 map, Fifth and Sixth on named powers, PESA or Forest Rights Act layer, verdict | Five or six units, with exact provisions |
| 20 | Thesis, both Schedules in detail, rights layer, implementation critique, extension or reform paragraph, graded verdict | Six to eight units plus a dedicated protection-versus-self-government paragraph |

### 5. Closing synthesis

The topic resolves into one proposition and one caution.

**The proposition.** India chose two institutional answers to a single question. One protects through constitutional offices outside the community; the other governs through an elected institution inside it. Statute later added participation and tenure to the first, and the courts added a requirement that rights be determined before they are overridden.

**The caution.** Every institution in this topic runs on a record — a report, a resolution, a map, a title, an assent, an audit. Where the record exists, protection becomes enforceable and participation becomes provable. Where it does not, constitutional strength and statutory precision both fall silent.

That is the sentence to end an answer with, and it is also why the implementation lessons in this session matter as much as the textual ones.

### UPSC application

- Rehearse the reversal board as directional sentences, not as a list.
- Open every answer with a thesis from the selector, then prove it with named provisions.
- Attach one qualification to every major claim; the examiner reads qualification as command over the subject.
- Keep *Samatha* and the Niyamgiri judgment bounded; over-claiming on either is the fastest way to lose credibility.
- End on the record-and-remedy point; it converts description into evaluation.

### Revision notes

1. Three generators of error: mirror institutions, qualifying adjectives, verb intensity.
2. Direction, not content, is what fails under time pressure; rehearse directional sentences.
3. Fifth Schedule regulation takes the President's assent; Sixth Schedule council law takes the Governor's, except an Assam Concurrent-List council law, which is reserved for the President.
4. President declares the area; President specifies the tribe; Governor organises districts and regions.
5. Advice from the Tribes Advisory Council; law from a District or Regional Council.
6. "Not more than thirty" and "not more than four nominated" for a council; "not more than twenty" for the advisory body.
7. Reserved forests are excluded from paragraph 3; minor minerals only under PESA sections 4(k) and 4(l).
8. Application of laws varies by State across paragraphs 12, 12A, 12AA and 12B.
9. Paragraph 9 gives an agreed royalty share; the Governor's determination of a dispute is final.
10. Both Schedules are amendable by ordinary parliamentary law.
11. Evidence-led paragraph: claim, named evidence, mechanism, significance, qualification, mini-verdict.
12. Use the demand-to-thesis selector to choose a position before writing.
13. Mark-scaled architecture: three units at 10, five or six at 15, six to eight plus a dedicated paragraph at 20.
14. Closing proposition: two institutional answers to one question, with statute and courts adding participation and tenure.
15. Closing caution: every institution in this topic runs on a record.

### Concept check

**Question:** You are given fifteen marks and the directive "Examine" on the Fifth Schedule. Outline the architecture of your answer in six moves.

**Model answer:** One, thesis — the Fifth Schedule is constitutionally powerful but institutionally under-activated. Two, territory — paragraph 6 Presidential declaration in ten States, with the administrative criteria classified correctly. Three, administration — paragraph 2 continuing State executive power, paragraph 3 Governor's report to the President and Union directions. Four, voice and protective law — paragraph 4's advisory council with its agenda limit, and paragraph 5's law filter and land, allotment and money-lending regulations requiring Presidential assent. Five, support and statute — Articles 275(1) and 339, PESA and the Forest Rights Act as the agency layer. Six, verdict — activation requires published reports, reasoned disposal of advice, cross-law conformity, records and enforceable remedies.

**Misconception to avoid:** Spending the answer proving that the Schedule's powers exist. The directive "Examine" asks for their adequacy in operation, so at least a third of the answer must address use, evidence and remedy.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt:** "Scheduled and tribal area governance in India has moved from protection to participation, but not yet to power." Critically examine.

#### Model solution

The trajectory is accurate and the terminal claim largely sustainable.

**Protection.** The Fifth Schedule, applied by Article 244(1), builds safeguarding around offices outside the community: Presidential declaration under paragraph 6, continuing State administration under paragraph 2, the Governor's report and Union directions under paragraph 3, an advisory council of not more than twenty under paragraph 4, and the Governor's paragraph 5 power to disapply or modify Acts and regulate land transfer, allotment and money-lending, subject to Presidential assent.

**Participation.** PESA, enacted under Article 243M(4)(b), relocated decision-making to a habitation-based village, constituted the Gram Sabha from the electoral roll, and assigned calibrated verbs — approval of local plans, certification of utilisation, consultation before acquisition, mandatory recommendation for minor minerals, and endowment of ownership and control under section 4(m). The Forest Rights Act added Gram Sabha-initiated determination of individual, community and community forest resource rights under section 6, with conservation duties under section 5. Niyamgiri required that determination before clearance, while leaving the final decision with the Ministry.

**Why not yet power.** Statutory powers remain blocked by unreconciled excise, forest, market, money-lending and land laws; State rules sometimes mis-define the PESA village; meeting, land and community-forest records are thin; restoration orders are rarely tracked; entrusted functions arrive without staff and funds; and official material through 2026 lists rule regimes for nine of the ten PESA States.

**Qualification.** The Sixth Schedule shows real power is constitutionally possible: the deficit is implementational, not conceptual.

The last step is administrative: records, conformity, capacity and remedy.

**Rubric (20):** protection phase with exact paragraphs 5; participation phase with PESA and Forest Rights Act provisions 5; judicial contribution with its limit 2; evidence-based explanation of the power deficit 4; Sixth Schedule counter-example as qualification 2; implementation-focused conclusion 2.

### Where this leads

The constitutional and statutory architecture is now complete and internally consistent. What remains is application: verified past questions, cumulative checks across lessons, original Mains practice, remediation of the errors that persist after study, master maps for revision, and consolidated register notes.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

This section gives each question's exact demand and a way of working it, without supplying an answer or any hint of one. Objective questions keep their printed response choices as part of the question text.

## Direct objective questions on this topic

### 2019 Prelims GS-I, Question 50

> Under which Schedule of the Constitution of India can the transfer of tribal land to private parties for mining be declared null and void?
>
> A. Third Schedule
>
> B. Fifth Schedule
>
> C. Ninth Schedule
>
> D. Twelfth Schedule

**Paper:** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2019, General Studies Paper I, Question 50. No answer is given here; work it from the constitutional text.

**Mapped lessons:** Lesson 6 for the protective regulation power over land in a protected tribal territory, and Lesson 17 for the judicial treatment of such land transfers.

**How to work it:** Ask which Schedule of the Constitution carries a power to regulate or prohibit the transfer of land in a protected tribal territory, and read that power in the Schedule itself rather than from the surrounding case law. Then note that a judgment construing a particular State regulation does not travel beyond the regulatory setting it construed.

### 2022 Prelims GS-I, Question 73

**Demand:** the consequences of bringing an area under the Fifth Schedule of the Constitution.

**Wording:** the exact wording of this question could not be verified, so only its demand appears above. Nothing is reconstructed and no response choices are shown.

**Mapped lessons:** Lessons 3 and 4, with the protective-law consequence in Lesson 6 and the statutory arrangements in Lessons 14 and 15.

**How to work it:** Work through what scheduling actually changes — the authority that creates the status, what happens to State executive power, what supervision is added, which advisory institution appears, which protective law-making power becomes available, and which statutory local-government arrangement begins to apply. Then separate those consequences from arrangements that scheduling does **not** produce.

### 2023 Prelims GS-I, Question 39

> With reference to "Scheduled Areas" in India, consider the following statements:
>
> 1. Within a State, the notification of an area as Scheduled Area takes place through an Order of the President.
>
> 2. The largest administrative unit forming the Scheduled Area is the District and the lowest is the cluster of villages in the Block.
>
> 3. The Chief Ministers of the concerned States are required to submit annual reports to the Union Home Ministry on the administration of Scheduled Areas in the States.
>
> How many of the above statements are correct?
>
> A. Only one
>
> B. Only two
>
> C. All three
>
> D. None

**Paper:** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2023, General Studies Paper I, Question 39. No answer is given here, and no statement is marked as true or false.

**Mapped lessons:** Lesson 3 for the notification route and the administrative criteria, and Lesson 4 for the reporting chain.

**How to work it:** Test each statement independently against Fifth Schedule paragraph 6 for the notification instrument, the official identification criteria for the administrative-unit description, and Fifth Schedule paragraph 3 for the reporting officer and the recipient. Do not convert administrative guidance into a constitutional rule in either direction.

### 2024 Prelims GS-I, Question 84

> Consider the following statements:
>
> 1. It is the Governor of the State who recognizes and declares any community of that State as a Scheduled Tribe.
>
> 2. A community declared as a Scheduled Tribe in a State need not be so in another State.
>
> Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
>
> A. 1 only
>
> B. 2 only
>
> C. Both 1 and 2
>
> D. Neither 1 nor 2

**Paper:** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2024, General Studies Paper I, Set A, Question 84. No answer is given here.

**Mapped lesson:** Lesson 2, section on Article 342.

**How to work it:** Read Article 342(1) for the authority that specifies Scheduled Tribes and the consultation it prescribes, and Article 342(2) for the route by which the list may be altered. Then consider whether the notification operates in relation to a State or Union territory, and what that implies for a community's status across States.

### 2025 Prelims GS-I, Question 56

> Consider the following statements:
>
> With reference to the Constitution of India, if an area in a State is declared as Scheduled Area under the Fifth Schedule
>
> I. the State Government loses its executive power in such areas and a local body assumes total administration
>
> II. the Union Government can take over the total administration of such areas under certain circumstances on the recommendations of the Governor
>
> Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
>
> A. I only
>
> B. II only
>
> C. Both I and II
>
> D. Neither I nor II

**Paper:** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2025, General Studies Paper I, Set A, Question 56. No answer is given here.

**Mapped lesson:** Lesson 4.

**How to work it:** Test proposition I against Fifth Schedule paragraph 2 on the reach of State executive power, and proposition II against Fifth Schedule paragraph 3 on the Governor's report and the nature of the Union's power in relation to such administration. Decide each proposition independently on the text.

### 2026 Prelims GS-I, Question 57

> Consider the following statements about the provisions pertaining to the Scheduled Castes and the Scheduled Tribes in India:
>
> 1. Provisions regarding the administration of the Tribal Areas in the States of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram are given in the Fifth Schedule of the Constitution of India.
>
> 2. Some tribes of India are entitled to exemption from paying Income Tax on certain incomes.
>
> 3. The Constitution of India provides for reservation of seats in Panchayats for women belonging to the Scheduled Castes and the Scheduled Tribes.
>
> Which one of the following conclusions based on the above statements is correct?
>
> A. There are two correct statements, that include statement 2.
>
> B. There are two correct statements, that are statements 1 and 3.
>
> C. There is only one correct statement.
>
> D. All three statements are correct.

**Paper:** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper I, Set A, Question 57. No answer is given here. Statement 1 belongs squarely to this topic; statements 2 and 3 reach into neighbouring areas.

**Mapped lessons:** Lesson 2 for the Article 244 allocation between the Schedules, with the Panchayat-reservation element belonging to the Panchayati Raj topic and the tax element to tax law rather than to this one.

**How to work it:** Check statement 1 against Article 244(1) and 244(2) and against Fifth Schedule paragraph 1. Verify the second statement against the source of law it names rather than assuming a constitutional provision. Verify the third against the relevant Part IX provision on reservation of seats in Panchayats. Then evaluate each conclusion option as a conclusion, not as a statement.

## Necessary cross-topic questions

### 2026 GS-II, Question 6 — 10 marks, 150 words

> Does India's tribal development policy reflect the aspirations for a socially grounded and equity-based governance? Justify your answer.

**Paper:** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper II, Question 6. Tribal welfare and development policy is the question's own area; Scheduled and tribal area governance supplies its constitutional and statutory self-governance component.

**Relevance to this topic:** Articles 244, 275(1) and 339; the Fifth and Sixth Schedules as two models of differentiated governance; PESA's calibrated community powers; forest-rights recognition and community forest resource governance; and the implementation and record deficits identified in Lessons 18 and 19.

**Demand approach:** Define socially grounded governance as decision-making anchored in the community's own institutions and recognised rights, and equity-based governance as substantive rather than formal equality. Assess the policy architecture against both standards: strong constitutional and statutory design, uneven sectoral conformity, weak records and remedies. Conclude with a graded verdict rather than a binary one.

### 2026 GS-III, Question 10 — 10 marks, 150 words

> Ladakh is strategically located between China and Pakistan. As a measure to win hearts and minds of locals, discuss the Border Area Development Programmes (BADP) by the Central Government and civic actions by the Army. Also discuss demand of promulgation of provision of the Sixth Schedule of constitution for Ladakh.

**Paper:** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper III, Question 10. Border management and border-area development is the question's own area; the Sixth Schedule demand is the limb this topic supplies.

**Relevance to this topic:** Lesson 20 in full — what a Sixth Schedule council would deliver, how Article 244(2) has actually been altered and what that implies for a new unit, the paragraph 20C Union-territory adaptation, the alternative models including an Article 371-type consent shield and strengthened hill councils, and the dated position of the demand as on 1 October 2026 together with the Ministry's reported Article 371 framework.

**Demand approach:** Treat the question as three linked parts. Handle the programme and civic-action limbs within their own area, with dated claims only. For the third limb, state what the Sixth Schedule would and would not confer, identify the enacted legal step that alone would change status, weigh the alternatives on common axes, and close with a conditional judgment tied to institutional fit rather than analogy.

## How to use this index

Six direct objective questions from 2019 to 2026 fall squarely on this topic, and two 2026 Mains questions use it as a supporting area. Exact wording is reproduced only where it could be verified; where it could not, the question's demand appears alone and nothing is reconstructed. No answer is given for any question in this index. The approach notes show how to reach one from the constitutional and statutory text, which is the only method that transfers to a question you have not seen.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

## Cumulative check 1 — the provision switch

**Question:** Given a tribal region, how do you decide in one step which Schedule governs it?

**Model answer:** Identify the State. If it is Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura or Mizoram, Article 244(2) applies the Sixth Schedule to its tribal areas, and Fifth Schedule paragraph 1 confirms the exclusion by defining "State" in that Schedule to exclude those four. For any other State, Article 244(1) applies the Fifth Schedule to its Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes — but only territory covered by an operative Presidential Order under paragraph 6 is a Scheduled Area.

## Cumulative check 2 — direction of assent

**Question:** Arrange these in the correct direction: a Governor's regulation on tribal land transfer; a District Council law on inheritance; a District Council regulation levying a market tax.

**Model answer:** The Governor's regulation is made under Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(2) and, by paragraph 5(4), is submitted forthwith to the **President** and has no effect until he assents, with prior consultation of the Tribes Advisory Council required under paragraph 5(5) where one exists. The District Council's law on inheritance is made under Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(1)(h). In Meghalaya, Tripura or Mizoram it takes the **Governor's** assent under paragraph 3(3); in **Assam** the Governor must reserve it for the **President** under paragraph 3A(2) or 3B(2), because inheritance falls within Concurrent List Entry 5. The taxation regulation is made under paragraph 8 and, by paragraph 8(4), takes the **Governor's** assent in every one of the four States.

## Cumulative check 3 — who creates territory

**Question:** Contrast how territory is created and altered under the two Schedules.

**Model answer:** Under the Fifth Schedule, the **President** declares an area to be a Scheduled Area under paragraph 6(1) and may cease, increase, rectify, declare on reorganisation, or rescind and redefine under paragraph 6(2), consulting the Governor for enlargement and redefinition. Under the Sixth Schedule, the **Constitution** lists tribal areas in the paragraph 20 table, and the **Governor** organises and reorganises autonomous districts and regions under paragraph 1(3) — with creation, increase, diminution and union requiring consideration of a Commission report under paragraph 14(1).

## Cumulative check 4 — advice against legislation

**Question:** State the exact legal weight of a Tribes Advisory Council recommendation.

**Model answer:** It is advice, not law. Under paragraph 4(2) the council advises on matters pertaining to the welfare and advancement of Scheduled Tribes **as may be referred to it by the Governor**, so it controls neither the outcome nor the agenda. Its one procedurally indispensable role is under paragraph 5(5): where such a council exists, it must be consulted before the Governor makes a regulation. Non-binding advice combined with mandatory consultation is its precise constitutional weight.

## Cumulative check 5 — the PESA verb test

**Question:** For each of these, name the exact PESA verb: a village irrigation plan; acquisition of land for a highway; a minor-mineral auction concession; ownership of tendu leaves.

**Model answer:** The village plan requires **approval** of the Gram Sabha before implementation by the village Panchayat under section 4(e)(i). Acquisition requires **consultation** with the Gram Sabha or the Panchayat at the appropriate level under section 4(i), which also keeps actual planning and implementation coordinated at State level. The auction concession requires a **prior mandatory recommendation** under section 4(l). Ownership of minor forest produce is an **endowment** that State law must confer under section 4(m)(ii).

## Cumulative check 6 — reserved adjectives

**Question:** Three single words in this topic reverse an answer if dropped. Name them and their effect.

**Model answer:** **"Reserved"** — Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(1)(a) and (b) exclude land which is a reserved forest and the management of reserved forests, so a council does not control all forests. **"Minor"** — PESA sections 4(k) and 4(l) apply to minor minerals, not to major minerals. **"Agreed"** — Sixth Schedule paragraph 9 gives a share of royalties **as agreed** between the State Government and the District Council, settled finally by the Governor in case of dispute, which is neither ownership nor consent.

## Cumulative check 7 — the two judgments, bounded

**Question:** State what each leading judgment decided and what it did not.

**Model answer:** *Samatha* invalidated the impugned grants and leases of Scheduled-Area land to non-tribal private mining companies under the Andhra Pradesh Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation read with the Fifth Schedule, treating the State's position as trustee-like; it did **not** establish an automatic nationwide prohibition detached from the applicable State regulation, and its wider reach has been debated. The Niyamgiri judgment required the Gram Sabhas to determine forest-rights claims including cultural and religious claims before the clearance decision; it did **not** make the Gram Sabha the final clearance authority, which remained with the Ministry.

## Cumulative check 8 — the enabling-clause family

**Question:** Four provisions in this topic allow constitutionally significant change by ordinary parliamentary law. Name them, and name the further route by which Article 244(2) itself has twice been altered without Article 368.

**Model answer:** Fifth Schedule **paragraph 7**, allowing Parliament to amend that Schedule; Sixth Schedule **paragraph 21**, allowing Parliament to amend that Schedule; **Article 243M(4)(b)**, allowing Parliament to extend Part IX to Scheduled Areas and tribal areas with exceptions and modifications — the clause under which PESA was enacted; and **Article 244A(4)**, declaring that a law forming an autonomous State within Assam is not an Article 368 amendment even if it has the effect of amending the Constitution. Article 243ZC performs the parallel enabling function for Part IXA. The further route is **Articles 3 and 4**: a reorganisation law may carry supplemental, incidental and consequential provisions, and Article 4(2) declares such a law not to be an Article 368 amendment. Article 244(2) was altered that way by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 and by the State of Mizoram Act, 1986.

## Cumulative check 9 — exclusion architecture

**Question:** Explain in one movement why PESA exists and why it does not cover Sixth Schedule areas.

**Model answer:** Article 243M(1) withheld Part IX from both Scheduled Areas and tribal areas so that a uniform Panchayat template would not overwrite customary institutions. Article 243M(4)(b) then allowed Parliament to extend Part IX to **both** categories, subject to exceptions and modifications. Parliament exercised that power in 1996 only for Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas, because the tribal areas already possessed elected District and Regional Councils with legislative, executive, judicial and fiscal powers. The non-application is therefore a legislative choice, not a constitutional bar.

## Cumulative check 10 — the four systems

**Question:** A statutory hill council, an autonomous district council, a PESA Gram Sabha and an Article 371A consent requirement are placed before you. Classify each.

**Model answer:** A statutory hill council is created by ordinary legislation and has no Sixth Schedule status, as Article 243M(3)'s reference to the Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council illustrates. An autonomous district council exists under Sixth Schedule paragraphs 1 and 2 with constitutional powers over paragraph 3 subjects. A PESA Gram Sabha is a statutory body in a Fifth Schedule Scheduled Area, constituted from the village electoral roll under section 4(c). An Article 371A requirement is a Part XXI State-level consent shield operating through the Legislative Assembly, with no territorial council attached.

## Cumulative check 11 — implementation diagnosis

**Question:** A State has notified PESA Rules but Gram Sabhas report that they cannot stop unlicensed money-lending or control the village market. Diagnose the failure.

**Model answer:** The failure is at the sectoral-conformity layer. Section 4(m)(iv) and 4(m)(v) oblige the State Legislature to endow powers over village markets and money-lending, but the operative levers sit in the State's agricultural-market law and money-lenders legislation. Unless those statutes are amended to recognise the Gram Sabha's power, the PESA endowment exists on paper while the market committee and the registration regime continue to control the subject. Notification of rules is only the third of four layers.

## Cumulative check 12 — the closing proposition

**Question:** State in one sentence why records dominate this topic.

**Model answer:** Because every institution here operates through a documented act — the Governor's report, a council resolution, a Gram Sabha minute, a forest-rights map, an assent, an audit — so the absence of a record disables the next institution in the chain and makes protection unenforceable and participation unprovable.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

## Final 10-mark question — 150 words

**Question:** Explain the constitutional significance of Fifth Schedule paragraph 7 and Sixth Schedule paragraph 21.

**Model answer:** Both paragraphs allow Parliament by law to amend the respective Schedule by addition, variation or repeal, and both declare that such a law shall not be deemed an amendment of the Constitution for the purposes of Article 368.

Their significance is threefold. They keep tribal-area institutions adaptable, so demography, reorganisation and negotiated settlements can be accommodated without engaging constituent power. They explain the constitutional history of the Sixth Schedule: the 1988 modifications for Tripura and Mizoram, the 1995 enlargements for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, and the 2003 creation of the Bodoland Territorial Areas District and Council were all ordinary legislation. And they show that the same device recurs in Article 243M(4)(b) and Article 244A(4), making flexibility a deliberate design choice across this field.

The qualification is symmetry: a route that enables enlargement equally enables reduction, so the safeguard is political and evidentiary rather than procedural.

**Rubric (10):** exact content of both paragraphs 3; non-Article 368 character 2; historical amendments correctly attributed 3; link to the wider enabling-clause family 1; symmetry qualification 1.

## Final 15-mark question — 250 words

**Question:** Evaluate the role of the Governor across the Fifth and Sixth Schedules, and assess whether the office is well suited to the functions assigned to it.

**Model answer:** The Governor's functions differ fundamentally between the Schedules.

Under the Fifth Schedule the office is a protective supervisor. Paragraph 3 requires an annual or on-demand report to the President on the administration of Scheduled Areas. Paragraph 4(2) controls what the Tribes Advisory Council may examine, and 4(3) supplies rule-making for it. Paragraph 5(1) permits disapplying or modifying any particular Act of Parliament or the State Legislature, with retrospective effect; paragraph 5(2) permits regulations on land transfer, allotment and money-lending, which may under 5(3) repeal or amend an applicable Act, and which take effect only on Presidential assent. Paragraph 6(2) requires the Governor's consultation for enlargement and redefinition of Scheduled Areas.

Under the Sixth Schedule the office is the working supervisor of autonomous institutions. Paragraph 1(3) confers territorial reorganisation powers; paragraph 2(6) first-constitution rule-making; paragraph 3(3), 8(4) and 10(3) assent to council laws and regulations; paragraph 4(3) specification of the High Court's jurisdiction; paragraph 5 conferment of procedural-code powers; paragraph 9(2) final determination of royalty-share disputes; and paragraphs 14 to 16 inquiry, annulment, suspension and dissolution.

Suitability is mixed. Placing protective functions in a constitutional office insulates them from departmental pressure and gives the Union a reporting channel. But several functions are discretionary in their trigger, so initiative depends on an office embedded in State executive arrangements that weigh revenue, forest and mining priorities.

Reform should standardise reporting, fix reference calendars and assent timelines, and require reasons — strengthening use without disturbing the constitutional location of the power.

**Rubric (15):** Fifth Schedule functions with exact paragraphs 4; Sixth Schedule functions with exact paragraphs 5; identification of the structural difference between the two roles 2; evaluation of suitability with the discretionary-trigger point 2; reform conclusion preserving constitutional location 2.

## Final 20-mark question — 250 words

**Question:** A draft parliamentary Bill proposes to (i) empower State Governments to declare Scheduled Areas, (ii) make every Autonomous District Council law subject to the President's assent, and (iii) declare that the Gram Sabha's recommendation under PESA shall be treated as binding consent for all mining in Scheduled Areas. As constitutional adviser, assess each clause and suggest an accurate drafting route.

**Model answer:** Each clause is defective for a different reason, and each needs a different instrument.

**Clause (i).** Fifth Schedule paragraph 6(1) makes Scheduled Area status a function of Presidential order, and paragraph 6(2) confines alteration to specified routes, its closing words barring variation of the founding order otherwise. Parliament could amend the Schedule by ordinary law under paragraph 7, which is not an Article 368 amendment. But the proposal is unsound in substance: central declaration exists to insulate territorial protection from State-level revenue and project interests, and transferring it to the State inverts the safeguard. If a State role is desired, draft a mandatory consultation and reasoned-recommendation procedure, leaving the order with the President.

**Clause (ii).** Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(3) requires the Governor's assent, as do paragraphs 8(4) and 10(3) — except in Assam, where paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) already reserve Concurrent-List council laws for the President. Parliament may amend the Schedule under paragraph 21, so the clause is competent. It is retrograde: generalising the Assam exception would add delay without scrutiny, since the President acts on advice. Prefer assent timelines with recorded reasons.

**Clause (iii).** PESA sections 4(k) and 4(l) make recommendations mandatory for **minor** minerals only; section 4(i) uses consultation for acquisition. Converting a recommendation into binding consent for **all** mining would exceed the Act's calibrated scheme and intersect with mineral and environment law. If the aim is stronger community power, amend the Act clause by clause, specify the mineral category and project stage, and provide reasons and review.

**Rubric (20):** clause (i) paragraph 6 analysis with the paragraph 7 route and a substantive objection 6; clause (ii) paragraph 3(3) with the Assam paragraph 3A(2) and 3B(2) exception, paragraph 21 and a reasoned alternative 5; clause (iii) accurate PESA verb and mineral-category analysis 6; competence-versus-wisdom distinction maintained throughout 2; practical redrafting advice 1.

# REMEDIATION

## Error family 1: the Schedules reversed

**Faulty recall:** The Fifth Schedule covers Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.

**Repair:** Article 244(2) applies the **Sixth** Schedule to tribal areas in those four States, and Fifth Schedule paragraph 1 defines "State" in that Schedule so as to exclude them. Rehearse it as a directional sentence: four north-eastern States means Sixth; the other ten Scheduled-Area States mean Fifth.

## Error family 2: the wrong authority for territory or tribe

**Faulty recall:** The Governor declares Scheduled Areas and notifies Scheduled Tribes.

**Repair:** The **President** declares Scheduled Areas under Fifth Schedule paragraph 6(1), and specifies Scheduled Tribes under Article 342(1) after consulting the Governor where it is a State; **Parliament** alters the tribe list under Article 342(2). The Governor's territorial power is under the **Sixth** Schedule, paragraph 1(3), over autonomous districts and regions.

## Error family 3: consultation treated as consent

**Faulty recall:** The President cannot alter a Scheduled Area without the Governor's agreement.

**Repair:** Consultation appears textually in paragraph 6(2)(aa) for enlargement and 6(2)(d) for rescission and redefinition. It requires that views be genuinely sought and considered; it confers no veto, and it is not written into clauses (a), (b) or (c) at all.

## Error family 4: assent reversed

**Faulty recall:** Fifth Schedule regulations need the Governor's assent; Sixth Schedule council laws need the President's.

**Repair:** Almost exactly the reverse — with one real exception. Fifth Schedule paragraph 5(4): submitted forthwith to the **President**, no effect until assent. Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(3): submitted forthwith to the **Governor**, no effect until assent; paragraphs 8(4) and 10(3) also use the Governor. The exception is **Assam**, where paragraph 3(3) was substituted in 1995 and 2003 and now opens "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B": a law of the Karbi Anglong, North Cachar Hills or Bodoland Council that relates to a **Concurrent List** matter must be reserved by the Governor for the **President**. So the sentence to rehearse is "Fifth to the President, Sixth to the Governor — and in Assam, Concurrent-List council laws to the President."

## Error family 5: the advisory body inflated

**Faulty recall:** The Tribes Advisory Council is the Fifth Schedule's legislature and has 20 members with three-fourths ST MLAs.

**Repair:** Paragraph 4(2) confines it to **advising** on matters referred by the Governor. Paragraph 4(1) provides for **not more than** twenty members, of whom **as nearly as may be** three-fourths shall be representatives of the Scheduled Tribes in the Legislative Assembly, with a proviso filling any shortfall from other members of those tribes.

## Error family 6: executive power misdescribed

**Faulty recall:** Once scheduled, an area passes to a local body or to Union administration.

**Repair:** Paragraph 2 preserves the State's executive power subject to the Schedule; paragraph 3 adds a reporting duty and a Union power to give **directions** to the State. No takeover, and no local body, is created by the Fifth Schedule.

## Error family 7: council composition over-generalised

**Faulty recall:** Every Autonomous District Council has 30 members, all elected, serving five years.

**Repair:** Paragraph 2(1) provides for **not more than thirty** members, of whom **not more than four** are nominated by the Governor and the rest elected on adult suffrage; the commonly cited maximum configuration is 26 elected plus 4 nominated. Paragraph 2(6A) gives **elected** members five years from the date appointed for the first meeting, while nominated members hold office during the Governor's pleasure. The Bodoland Territorial Council, created by the 2003 amendment, is a special arrangement with its own ceiling of not more than forty-six members under the proviso to paragraph 2(1).

## Error family 8: forests and minerals over-claimed

**Faulty recall:** An Autonomous District Council controls all forests and owns the district's minerals.

**Repair:** Paragraph 3(1)(a) and (b) exclude land which is a reserved forest and the management of reserved forests, with paragraph 3(2) supplying the definition. Paragraph 9 provides a **share of royalties as agreed** between the State Government and the District Council from State-granted licences or leases, with the Governor determining disputes finally. Neither amounts to ownership.

## Error family 9: a single rule for the application of laws

**Faulty recall:** Central and State laws do not apply in Sixth Schedule areas unless the council agrees.

**Repair:** Four State-specific paragraphs govern this. In **Assam**, paragraph 12(a) gives the District Council the switch for State Acts on paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor, and 12(b) gives the Governor the switch for other Acts, including Acts of Parliament. In **Meghalaya**, paragraph 12A substitutes a repugnancy rule under which State law prevails, and gives the President the switch for Acts of Parliament. In **Tripura** and **Mizoram**, paragraphs 12AA and 12B give the Council the switch for paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor, the Governor for other State Acts, and the President for Acts of Parliament.

## Error family 10: PESA extended to the wrong territory

**Faulty recall:** PESA applies to all tribal areas, including Sixth Schedule districts.

**Repair:** PESA was enacted under Article 243M(4)(b) and extends modified Part IX only to **Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas**. The enabling clause would have permitted extension to tribal areas as well; Parliament did not do so.

## Error family 11: PESA verbs flattened

**Faulty recall:** PESA gives the Gram Sabha consent over every project in a Scheduled Area.

**Repair:** The verbs are clause-specific: **approval** of local plans under 4(e)(i); **identification** of beneficiaries under 4(e)(ii); **certification** of fund utilisation under 4(f); **consultation** before acquisition and rehabilitation under 4(i), with State-level coordination preserved in the same clause; **mandatory recommendation** for minor-mineral licences, leases and auction concessions under 4(k) and 4(l); and **endowment** of seven substantive powers under 4(m). Major minerals are outside 4(k) and 4(l) altogether.

## Error family 12: the village definition ignored

**Faulty recall:** The PESA Gram Sabha is the Gram Panchayat's general body.

**Repair:** Section 4(b) defines a village as ordinarily a habitation, a group of habitations, a hamlet or a group of hamlets **comprising a community and managing its affairs in accordance with traditions and customs**, and section 4(c) constitutes the Gram Sabha from persons on the village-level electoral roll. Equating the PESA village with a revenue village or Gram Panchayat defeats every power in section 4.

## Error family 13: forest rights reduced to individual titles

**Faulty recall:** Forest rights are settled once individual titles are issued.

**Repair:** The Act recognises individual rights, community rights and **Community Forest Resource** rights, the last carrying the right to protect, regenerate, conserve or manage the customary common forest land traditionally protected by the community. Section 6 requires the Gram Sabha to initiate, verify and map claims, the Sub-Divisional Level Committee to prepare the record, and the District Level Committee to give final approval. Section 5 attaches conservation duties, and section 4(5) bars eviction before the procedure is complete.

## Error family 14: judgments over-extended

**Faulty recall:** *Samatha* bans private mining in all Scheduled Areas, and Niyamgiri gave Gram Sabhas a veto over mining.

**Repair:** *Samatha* invalidated the impugned leases under the **Andhra Pradesh** Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation read with the Fifth Schedule; its reach beyond that regulatory setting has been debated and should be stated with the State-law boundary. The Niyamgiri judgment required Gram Sabha determination of forest-rights-linked individual, community, cultural and religious claims, with the **Ministry retaining the final Stage-II clearance decision** taken in the light of those decisions.

## Error family 15: status confused with enactment

**Faulty recall:** Ladakh has been granted Sixth Schedule status, or the Centre's Article 371 proposal is the same thing; Sixth Schedule extension needs only an ordinary law under paragraph 21, or alternatively Article 244(2) can move only under Article 368; Statehood for a Union territory needs a constitutional amendment; several States have "completed" PESA.

**Repair:** As on 1 October 2026, Statehood had not been granted and Ladakh had not been brought within the Sixth Schedule. What was reported was a 22 May 2026 in-principle understanding on safeguards "on the lines of Article 371 A, F, and G"; a 9 September 2026 Ministry proposal for a directly elected Union-territory body under a new special provision in Article 371, which reports label "Chapter K"; a 14 September 2026 indication that the Ladakh bodies would consider dropping Statehood if that model satisfied them, with Statehood retained as their long-term aspiration; and a 22 September 2026 statement by the Chief Secretary that the proposed constitutional amendment would go to Parliament after consensus on the framework. On the route, keep three instruments apart. **Statehood** needs only an ordinary law under Article 3, which reaches a Union territory through Explanation I and is not an Article 368 amendment by force of Article 4(2). A **new Article 371-type provision** lies in Part XXI and needs Article 368. **Sixth Schedule inclusion** turns on Article 244(2): paragraph 21 cannot reach it, but Article 368 is not its only key — that clause was altered by reorganisation law in 1971 and 1986 and by the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 for Tripura, the reorganisation amendments being consequential on areas already inside the Schedule. On PESA, all ten Fifth Schedule States enacted compliance legislation amending their Panchayati Raj Acts, and State PESA Rules are notified in nine of the ten with Odisha alone at draft stage — and rules in any event are not an implementation outcome. Keep demand, dialogue, in-principle understanding, proposal, enactment and outcome as separate stages.

## Error family 16: Article 244A misplaced

**Faulty recall:** Article 244A is the provision under which the Bodoland, Karbi Anglong and Dima Hasao councils exist, and it has never been used.

**Repair:** Two corrections, in opposite directions. Those councils exist under the Sixth Schedule, paragraphs 1 and 2 read with the Assam-specific enlargements of 1995 and 2003, not under Article 244A. But Article 244A is **not** an unused power either. It is an enabling power for Parliament to form an autonomous State within Assam from Part I tribal areas, with a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both; clause (3) entrenches its core specification by a two-thirds requirement in each House, and clause (4) excludes it from Article 368. It was exercised once, by the **Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969**, which formed the autonomous State of Meghalaya on 2 April 1970; that unit ceased on 21 January 1972 when Meghalaya became a full State. The accurate sentence is "used once, 1970 to 1972, dormant since", and no autonomous State exists under the article today.

## Fifth Schedule causal chain

```text
tribal preponderance + compactness + viable unit + backwardness
   -> Presidential Order under paragraph 6(1)
      -> territory becomes a Scheduled Area
         -> State continues to administer (paragraph 2)
            -> Governor reports to the President (paragraph 3)
               -> Union may direct the State (paragraph 3)
            -> Governor refers matters to the Tribes Advisory Council (4(2))
               -> advice, non-binding
            -> Governor filters Acts (5(1)) and makes regulations (5(2))
               -> consult the council (5(5)) -> President's assent (5(4))
            -> Articles 275(1) and 339 supply money and supervision
               -> PESA adds habitation village and Gram Sabha powers
                  -> Forest Rights Act determines who holds which right
                     -> enforcement depends on records, forums and remedies
```

## Sixth Schedule causal chain

```text
tribal areas listed in the paragraph 20 table
   -> each becomes an autonomous district (paragraph 1(1))
      -> different Scheduled Tribes -> autonomous regions (1(2))
         -> Governor organises and reorganises (1(3))
            -> after a paragraph 14(1) Commission report for (c)-(f)
      -> District and Regional Councils constituted (paragraph 2)
         -> legislate on the ten paragraph 3 heads
            -> Governor's assent (3(3)); in ASSAM a Concurrent-List
               council law is RESERVED FOR THE PRESIDENT (3A(2)-(3),
               3B(2)-(3)); Meghalaya repugnancy rule (12A)
         -> run village councils and courts (paragraph 4)
            -> appeal within the Council; only the High Court and
               Supreme Court beyond; Governor specifies High Court reach
            -> paragraph 4 does NOT apply to the Bodoland Council (4(6))
         -> manage listed services (paragraph 6(1))
            -> Governor may entrust more with consent (6(2))
         -> District and Regional Funds (7); land revenue and taxes (8)
            -> CAG-prescribed accounts and audit; reports to the Council
         -> agreed royalty share (9); Governor's determination final
      -> brakes: inquiry (14), annulment or suspension (15),
         dissolution or assumption (16), all before the State
         Legislature -- but for TRIPURA and MIZORAM the 1988 Act
         gave revocation under 15 to the Governor, dropped its
         renewal proviso, and reduced 16 to a laying duty
```

```text
territorial status -> applicable land-transfer regulation
   -> forest-rights determination under section 6
      -> the exact PESA verb for this decision stage
         -> forest, environment, mineral and acquisition law
            -> reasoned decision: avoid | redesign | condition |
               compensate and rehabilitate | refuse
               -> restoration tracking and rehabilitation delivery

Judicial anchors, stated with their limits
```
