
## Accountability map

```text
ELECTORAL        -> council and Panchayat elections
LEGAL            -> Constitution, Schedules, PESA, Forest Rights Act,
                    land, forest and environment law, judicial review
FISCAL           -> Article 275 grants, District and Regional Funds,
                    CAG audit, PESA section 4(f) certification
ADMINISTRATIVE   -> paragraph 3 report, Union directions, paragraph 14
                    inquiry, reports before the State Legislature
SOCIAL           -> Gram Sabha proceedings, public records, grievance
                    redress, Article 338A consultation duty

                    every channel runs on a RECORD
                    no record -> next institution is disabled
```

## One-line comparative verdicts

| Question | Verdict in one line |
|---|---|
| Why two Schedules? | One problem, two institutional theories: guardianship and self-rule |
| Which is stronger on paper? | The Sixth, because its output is law, services, judgments and revenue rather than advice |
| Which is stronger in practice? | Whichever has records, capacity and remedies; neither is strong without them |
| What did PESA add? | Calibrated community power, clause by clause, inside the Fifth Schedule |
| What did the Forest Rights Act add? | The determination of who holds which right, which makes participation substantive |
| What did the courts add? | A requirement that rights be determined before they are overridden |
| What is still missing? | Sectoral conformity, records, capacity and enforceable remedies |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Provision vocabulary and the Article 244 switch

| Rapid recall | Rule |
|---|---|
| Article 244(1) | Fifth Schedule applies to Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in States other than Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram |
| Article 244(2) | Sixth Schedule applies to tribal areas in those four States |
| Fifth Schedule paragraph 1 | "State" in that Schedule excludes the four Sixth Schedule States |
| Sixth Schedule heading | Referenced to Articles 244(2) **and** 275(1) |
| Scheduled Area | President declares and alters under paragraph 6 |
| Tribal area | Constitutionally listed in the paragraph 20 table |
| Scheduled Tribe | Article 342(1) Presidential notification; Article 342(2) parliamentary alteration |
| Autonomous district and region | Governor organises under paragraph 1 |
| PESA village | Habitation-based, section 4(b) |

```text
AREA STATUS  !=  TRIBE STATUS  !=  COUNCIL ORGANISATION
President        President, then        Governor
para 6           Parliament, Art 342    Sixth Schedule para 1
```

## Why differentiated administration exists

- Uniform rules plus unequal bargaining power produce unequal outcomes; the Schedules are devices of substantive equality.
- Four risks: land and forest dependence; living customary institutions; thin administrative presence; concentrated extractive interest.
- Colonial route: Scheduled Districts Act, 1874; excluded and partially excluded areas under the Government of India Act, 1935.
- Continuity of form, change of purpose: insulation from representative institutions gave way to rights, welfare and self-government.
- Thakkar subcommittee — areas outside Assam, leading to the Fifth Schedule; Bordoloi subcommittee — Assam and the north-eastern tribal and excluded areas, leading to the Sixth.
- Thesis: differentiation is legitimate only while it converts protection into agency.

## Article 244A, 275 and 339

- **244A(1):** Parliament may by law form an autonomous State within Assam from Part I tribal areas, with a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both.
- **244A(2):** may specify State or Concurrent List matters, define executive power, assign attributable Assam taxes, make "State" references include it.
- **244A(3):** two-thirds of members present and voting in each House to amend the (2)(a) or (2)(b) specification.
- **244A(4):** not an Article 368 amendment. **Used once** — the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 (55 of 1969) formed the autonomous State of Meghalaya within Assam on the appointed day, 2 April 1970, with a Legislative Assembly, a Council of Ministers, its own legislative field and its own Consolidated Fund, and the same Act amended the Sixth Schedule by section 74 and its Fourth Schedule. The autonomous State ended on 21 January 1972 when the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 made Meghalaya a full State. **Dormant since; no autonomous State exists under the article today.**
- **275(1) first proviso:** capital and recurring grants for GoI-approved ST welfare schemes, or for raising Scheduled Area administration to the State's general level.
- **275(1) second proviso:** Assam-specific sums for Part I tribal areas.
- **275(1-A):** apportionment once an Article 244A autonomous State is formed.
- **275(2):** President may order until Parliament provides; after a Finance Commission exists, only after considering its recommendations.
- **339(1):** Presidential commission; discretionary at any time, mandatory at ten years from commencement. Dhebar 1960, reported 1961; Bhuria 2002, reported 2004.
- **339(2):** Union directions on drawing up and executing specified essential ST-welfare schemes; not confined to Scheduled Areas.
- Distinguish paragraph 3 directions (administration of Scheduled Areas) from 339(2) directions (specified welfare schemes).

## Scheduled Area declaration and the ten-State map

```text
TEN STATES
Andhra Pradesh | Telangana | Chhattisgarh | Gujarat | Himachal Pradesh
Jharkhand | Madhya Pradesh | Maharashtra | Odisha | Rajasthan
```

- Paragraph 6(1): the expression means such areas as the President may by order declare.
- 6(2)(a) cease; (aa) increase after consulting the Governor; (b) alter only by rectification of boundaries; (c) declare territory not previously in any State on boundary change, admission or new-State establishment; (d) rescind and, consulting the Governor, redefine afresh.
- Closing words: save as aforesaid, the 6(1) order shall not be varied by a subsequent order.
- Consultation is textually confined to (aa) and (d), and is not consent.
- Administrative criteria: tribal preponderance; compactness and reasonable size; viable unit such as district, block or taluk; comparative economic backwardness. Practice-based, not constitutional.
- Only notified territory is scheduled; a State is never wholly scheduled.
- Paragraph 7: Parliament may amend the Fifth Schedule by ordinary law; the 1976 amending Act inserted 6(2)(aa) and 6(2)(d).

## Fifth Schedule institutional chain

```text
paragraph 2  State executive power continues, subject to the Schedule
     -> paragraph 3  Governor reports to the President annually or when required
        -> Union executive power extends to DIRECTIONS to the State
     -> paragraph 4  Tribes Advisory Council advises on referred matters
     -> paragraph 5  law filter and protective regulations
```

- No automatic local-body or Union takeover; direction is not substitution.
- No constitutional template, deadline, publication, tabling or consequence for the report.
- **TAC:** mandatory in each State with Scheduled Areas; permissible by Presidential direction in a State with STs but no Scheduled Areas; **not more than twenty** members; **as nearly as may be** three-fourths representatives of STs in the Assembly; proviso fills shortfall with other members of those tribes.
- TAC duty: advise on welfare and advancement matters **referred by the Governor**. Subject limit plus agenda limit.
- Paragraph 4(3): Governor's rules on strength, appointment, Chairman, staff, meetings and procedure.
- Paragraph 5(1): by public notification, a particular Act of Parliament or the State Legislature shall not apply, or shall apply with exceptions and modifications; retrospective effect permitted; no assent or consultation condition stated.
- Paragraph 5(2): regulations for the peace and good government of a Scheduled Area; illustrative heads — transfer of land by or among STs; allotment of land to STs; money-lending to STs.
- Paragraph 5(3): such a regulation may repeal or amend any Act of Parliament, State Act or existing law applicable to the area.
- Paragraph 5(5): consult the TAC where one exists. Paragraph 5(4): submitted forthwith to the President; **no effect until assent**.
- Do not assert unqualified personal gubernatorial discretion; the defensible criticism is dormancy, not textual weakness.

## Sixth Schedule territory and councils

```text
STATE
  -> autonomous district (para 1(1)), still within State executive authority
       -> autonomous regions where different STs live (1(2))
            -> Regional Council; District Council has only delegated powers there (2(5))
```

- Four States only. Ten tribal areas: Assam — North Cachar Hills, Karbi Anglong, Bodoland Territorial Areas District; Meghalaya — Khasi Hills, Jaintia Hills, Garo Hills; Tripura — Tripura Tribal Areas District; Mizoram — Chakma, Mara, Lai.
- Paragraph 20(3): the Tripura district is defined by reference to the First Schedule to the Tripura Tribal Areas Autonomous District Council Act, 1979.
- Proviso to paragraph 20(2): the Shillong municipality area is excluded from the Khasi Hills District for specified purposes.
- Paragraph 1(3): include, exclude, create, increase, diminish, unite, alter name, define boundaries — by public notification; (c) to (f) only after considering a paragraph 14(1) Commission report; consequential orders may amend paragraph 20 and its table.
- Paragraph 2(1): **not more than thirty** members; **not more than four** nominated; rest elected on adult suffrage. Standard maximum configuration 26 plus 4.
- Paragraph 2(3): body corporate. 2(4) and 2(5): vesting and delegation.
- Paragraph 2(6): Governor's first-constitution rules, made in consultation with existing tribal councils or representative tribal organisations.
- Paragraph 2(6A): elected members five years from the date appointed for the first meeting after general elections; nominated members during the Governor's pleasure; extension by not more than one year at a time during an Emergency Proclamation or where elections are impracticable; casual-vacancy member serves the remainder.
- Paragraph 2(7): Council rule-making with the Governor's approval.
- **Paragraph 1(2) and its 2003 proviso:** the Governor may divide a district inhabited by different Scheduled Tribes into autonomous regions — but nothing in that sub-paragraph applies to the **Bodoland Territorial Areas District**, so no autonomous region or Regional Council can exist there.
- **The three amending Acts, all under paragraph 21:** 1988 Act (67 of 1988) — Tripura and Mizoram: paragraph 20BB discretion in listed functions after consulting the Council of Ministers and, if he thinks it necessary, the council concerned, application of Acts, paragraph 9(3) one-year royalty time limit, paragraph 10 recast to reach every person resident in the district, paragraph 15 revocation moved to the Governor with its renewal proviso omitted, and paragraph 16 stripped of previous approval and the second proviso with 16(3) reduced to a laying duty; and, without any application limit, serial numbers 2 and 3 of Part III of the paragraph 20 table substituted, which is where the Mara and Lai District names come from. 1995 Act (42 of 1995) — Assam: Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills renamed Autonomous Councils, paragraph 3A inserted with fifteen heads and Presidential reservation of their Concurrent-List laws, paragraph 3(3) substituted, paragraph 12(1)(a) extended, the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto" omitted from paragraph 14(2), and paragraph 20BA inserted making consultation of the named Autonomous Council compulsory. 2003 Act (44 of 2003) — Assam: Bodoland Territorial Areas District added as entry 3 of Part I, Bodoland Territorial Council created, paragraph 3B inserted with forty heads and the same Presidential reservation, paragraph 3(3) substituted again, paragraph 12(1)(a) extended, paragraph 19(4) inserted, and paragraphs 1(2), 4, 10 and 17 switched off for Bodoland.
- **Paragraphs 20BA and 20BB:** the Governor acts **in his discretion** in listed functions — in Assam after compulsorily consulting the Council of Ministers and the North Cachar Hills or Karbi Anglong Autonomous Council, and in Tripura and Mizoram after consulting the Council of Ministers and, only if he thinks it necessary, the District or Regional Council. The Assam list reaches further, taking in 2(6), 2(6A), 8(4) and 10(3). There is no equivalent paragraph for Meghalaya, and none of this supports a claim of unqualified personal discretion across the Schedule.
- **Bodoland Territorial Council:** under the proviso to paragraph 2(1), **not more than forty-six** members — forty elected (thirty reserved for STs, five for non-tribal communities, five open to all) and six nominated by the Governor from unrepresented communities of the Bodoland Territorial Areas District, of whom at least two shall be women, with the same rights and privileges including voting rights.
- Paragraphs 3A and 3B carry the enlarged Assam subject-lists — fifteen heads (a) to (o) for the Karbi Anglong and North Cachar Hills Councils, forty heads (i) to (xl) with a land-rights proviso for the Bodoland Territorial Council — and each requires the Governor to reserve that Council's Concurrent-List laws for the President under its sub-paragraph (2), with the President's assent, withholding or return-with-message powers in sub-paragraph (3).
- Ten Autonomous District Councils operated across the four States on official material dated 4 August 2026.

## Sixth Schedule powers, limits and brakes

| Power | Core content | Express limit |
|---|---|---|
| Legislative, paragraph 3 | Land; non-reserved forests; canal or watercourse for agriculture; jhum; village and town committees; village and town administration including local police, public health and sanitation; Chiefs or Headmen; inheritance; marriage and divorce; social customs | Excludes reserved forest land and reserved-forest management; acquisition proviso; Governor's assent under 3(3) — but in **Assam** that clause opens "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B", so a council law relating to a **List III** matter is reserved by the Governor for the **President**, who may assent, withhold assent, or return it with a message for reconsideration within six months (3A(2)-(3), 3B(2)-(3)) |
| Judicial, paragraphs 4 and 5 | Village councils and courts where **all** parties are STs within the area; Council as appellate forum; only the High Court and Supreme Court beyond | Governor specifies High Court reach (4(3)); rules need previous approval (4(4)); paragraph 4(5) Presidential recast; paragraph 5 powers conferred and withdrawable; **paragraph 4(6): the whole paragraph does not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council** |
| Executive, paragraph 6 | Establish, construct or manage primary schools, dispensaries, markets, cattle pounds, ferries, fisheries, roads, road transport and waterways; prescribe the language and manner of primary education | Regulations need the Governor's previous approval; 6(2) functions entrusted only with the Council's consent |
| Fiscal, paragraphs 7 to 9 | District and Regional Funds; land revenue on State principles; taxes on lands and buildings and tolls on residents; District Council additionally the five clauses of paragraph 8(3) — professions, trades, callings and employments, animals, vehicles and boats, entry of goods into a market and ferry tolls, taxes for schools, dispensaries or roads, and, by clause (e) inserted in 2016, entertainment and amusements; agreed share of mineral royalties | CAG-prescribed accounts and audit; 8(4) assent; royalty dispute determined finally by the Governor |
| Regulatory, paragraph 10 | Money-lending and trading: licences, maximum interest, accounts and inspection, licence for wholesale or retail business. By **non-tribals** in Assam and Meghalaya; as amended in 1988, by **every person resident in the district** in Tripura and Mizoram | Three-fourths of total membership; pre-existing businesses protected; Governor's assent under 10(3); under 10(4) the paragraph does **not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Council** |

- Paragraph 11: publication in the State Official Gazette gives the force of law.
- Paragraph 13: district estimates placed before the Council first, then shown separately in the State budget under Article 202.
- **Application of laws:** Assam — Council switch for paragraph 3 subjects and non-distilled liquor, Governor for the rest; Meghalaya — repugnancy rule favouring State law, President for Acts of Parliament; Tripura and Mizoram — Council, Governor and President respectively.
- **Brakes:** paragraph 14 inquiry commissions with reports laid before the State Legislature; paragraph 15 annulment or suspension where an act or resolution is likely to endanger the safety of India or be prejudicial to public order, twelve months; paragraph 16(1) dissolution on a Commission recommendation, with assumption for up to twelve months requiring the State Legislature's previous approval; paragraph 16(2) assumption for up to six months, extendable; 16(3) lapse thirty days after the Legislature first sits unless approved. **Not uniform:** in its application to **Assam**, the 1995 Act omitted from paragraph 14(2) the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto", so there the report and the explanatory memorandum are laid without those recommendations; and as amended for **Tripura and Mizoram** in 1988, paragraph 15 substitutes "by him" for "by the Legislature of the State", so the **Governor** revokes, and its renewal proviso is omitted; and in paragraph 16, 16(1)(b) carries no previous-approval requirement, the second proviso is omitted, and 16(3) becomes a bare duty to lay orders under 16(1) or 16(2), with reasons, before the State Legislature.
- Paragraph 17: exclusion of areas from reserved constituencies — but a proviso inserted in 2003 provides that paragraph 17 does **not** apply to the Bodoland Territorial Areas District. Paragraph 19(1) to 19(3): transitional regime — until a District Council is constituted the Governor administers the district, no Act of Parliament or of the State Legislature applies unless he directs by public notification (with retrospective effect if he chooses), and his peace-and-good-government regulations may repeal or amend any applicable law but have no effect until the President assents. This is the **mirror image** of Fifth Schedule paragraph 5, which switches laws off rather than on, and it carries no Tribes Advisory Council consultation requirement; constituting the Council transfers the switch to the authority named for that State in paragraphs 12 to 12B rather than making laws apply automatically. Paragraph 19(4), inserted in 2003: an Interim Executive Council for the Bodoland Territorial Areas District formed by the Governor from leaders of the Bodo movement including signatories to the Memorandum of Settlement of 10 February 2003, with adequate representation for non-tribal communities; the proviso says the Interim Council shall be for a period of six months during which endeavour to hold the election to the Council shall be made — a period and a duty of effort, not a completed-election deadline. Paragraph 20C: Union-territory adaptation reading Governor as Article 239 Administrator, for a unit Article 244(2) itself named. Paragraph 21: amendment of **the Schedule** by ordinary parliamentary law; it does not reach Article 244(2).
- The President is **not** the routine assent authority for council laws — but in **Assam** paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) require the Governor to reserve for him any council law relating to a Concurrent List matter.
- **The 2003 Bodoland carve-outs:** paragraph 1(2) autonomous regions, the whole of paragraph 4, the whole of paragraph 10 and the whole of paragraph 17 do not apply to the Bodoland Territorial Areas District or Council.

## Fifth against Sixth — compressed

| Fifth Schedule | Sixth Schedule |
|---|---|
| Scheduled Areas in ten States | Tribal areas in four States |
| President creates territory | Constitution lists; Governor organises |
| Advisory council | Elected District and Regional Councils |
| Governor filters law and makes regulations | Councils legislate on specified heads |
| President assents to regulations | Governor assents to council laws; in Assam, Concurrent-List council laws go to the President |
| State administers; Union directs | Councils manage listed functions within State authority |
| No Schedule-created courts or taxes | Village courts, land revenue and specified taxes |
| PESA applies | PESA does not apply |
| Supervision by President and Union | Supervision by Governor and State Legislature |

> **Memory:** the Fifth gives a constitutional guardian plus a statutory village; the Sixth gives a bounded territorial government.

## The four systems, and the exclusions that separate them

- **Article 243M(1):** Part IX does not apply to Scheduled Areas or tribal areas.
- **243M(2):** Part IX does not apply to Nagaland, Meghalaya and Mizoram, or to Manipur's hill areas with District Councils.
- **243M(3):** two limbs — (a) district-level Panchayat provisions do not apply to Darjeeling's hill areas for which the Gorkha Hill Council exists, and (b) nothing in Part IX is to be construed to affect that Council's functions and powers.
- **243M(3A):** inserted by the Constitution (Eighty-third Amendment) Act, 2000 — Article 243D's reservation of Panchayat seats for the Scheduled Castes does not apply to Arunachal Pradesh. This withholds one article, not Part IX.
- **243M(4)(a):** a named State's Assembly may extend Part IX by a total-membership majority and two-thirds of members present and voting.
- **243M(4)(b):** Parliament may extend Part IX to Scheduled Areas **and** tribal areas with exceptions and modifications; not an Article 368 amendment.
- **Article 243ZC:** parallel exclusion and enabling clause for municipalities under Part IXA; no PESA equivalent has been enacted.
- Ordinary Panchayats, PESA Gram Sabhas, Sixth Schedule councils and Article 371 provisions are four distinct systems.
- Articles 371A and 371G are State-level consent shields through the Legislative Assembly; Mizoram holds Article 371G **and** three autonomous district councils.
- A statutory hill council is not a constitutional Autonomous District Council.

## PESA power code

```text
APPROVE    4(e)(i)   local plans, programmes and projects before implementation
IDENTIFY   4(e)(ii)  beneficiaries under poverty alleviation and other programmes
CERTIFY    4(f)      utilisation of funds, obtained by the village Panchayat
RESERVE    4(g)      seats in proportion to community population; ST floor
                     of not less than one-half; all chairperson seats
NOMINATE   4(h)      unrepresented STs, up to one-tenth of members to be elected
CONSULT    4(i)      land acquisition and resettlement or rehabilitation;
                     actual planning and implementation coordinated at State level
MANAGE     4(j)      planning and management of minor water bodies
RECOMMEND  4(k)      prospecting licence or mining lease for MINOR minerals
RECOMMEND  4(l)      concession for exploitation of minor minerals by auction
ENDOW      4(m)      intoxicants; ownership of minor forest produce; prevention of
                     alienation and restoration of unlawfully alienated land; village
                     markets; money-lending control; control over social-sector
                     institutions and functionaries; control over local plans and
                     resources including tribal sub-plans
SUBSIDIARITY 4(n)    higher Panchayats must not assume lower or Gram Sabha powers
PATTERN    4(o)      endeavour to follow the Sixth Schedule pattern at district level
```

- **4(a):** State Panchayat law in Scheduled Areas shall be in consonance with customary law, social and religious practices and traditional management of community resources — a conformity standard, subject to the Constitution.
- **4(b):** village is ordinarily a habitation, group of habitations, hamlet or group of hamlets comprising a community managing its affairs by traditions and customs.
- **4(c):** Gram Sabha consists of persons on the village-level electoral roll.
- **4(d):** competent to safeguard traditions and customs, cultural identity, community resources and the customary mode of dispute resolution.
- **Section 5:** inconsistent State provisions cease one year after Presidential assent; existing Panchayats continue till their term ends.
- Intensity ladder: consultation < mandatory prior recommendation < approval < endowment.
- PESA applies to Fifth Schedule areas only; minor minerals only; no universal veto.

## Forest Rights Act interface

- Section 6(1): the Gram Sabha initiates determination of individual or community forest rights — receiving, consolidating and verifying claims, mapping each recommended claim and passing a resolution.
- Petitions by aggrieved persons go to the Sub-Divisional Level Committee, which prepares the record of forest rights.
- The District Level Committee hears appeals and gives **final** approval. The chain is administrative with statutory appeals.
- Community Forest Resource right: to protect, regenerate, conserve or manage the customary common forest land traditionally protected and conserved by the community.
- Section 5: conservation duties on rights holders, the Gram Sabha and village-level institutions — wildlife, forest and biodiversity; catchments and ecologically sensitive areas; habitat and heritage; compliance with Gram Sabha access decisions.
- Section 4(5): no eviction until the recognition and verification procedure is complete.
- Three categories to keep distinct: individual rights, community rights, Community Forest Resource rights.
- PESA asks who participates and with what power; the Act asks who holds which forest right. Neither substitutes for the other, and the Act is not confined to Scheduled Areas.

## Land, mining and case law

```text
record weakness -> debt -> transfer -> administrative conversion -> project change
      answered by
Fifth Schedule para 5(2)  +  PESA 4(m)(iii) prevent AND restore
      +  Sixth Schedule para 10  +  acquisition and rehabilitation law
      made effective only by
records  +  accessible forum and legal aid  +  tracked restoration
```

- *Samatha* (1997): under the Andhra Pradesh Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation read with the Fifth Schedule, the impugned grants and leases of Scheduled-Area land to non-tribal private mining companies were invalid; the State's position was read as trustee-like. Always state the State-law setting and the debated wider reach.
- Niyamgiri (2013): Gram Sabhas were to consider and decide forest-rights claims including cultural and religious claims, with State and ministry assistance and a judicial observer; decisions went through the State to the Ministry, which retained the **final Stage-II clearance decision**. Reported sequel: twelve Gram Sabhas rejected the proposal in 2013 and the Ministry is reported to have refused clearance in January 2014.
- Never write "Niyamgiri equals universal Gram Sabha veto." Royalty share is not consent; compensation is not restoration; one clearance is not all clearances.

## Rights, commissions and accountability

- Applicable: Article 14 non-arbitrariness; Article 19 subject to valid protective restrictions; Article 21 dignity, livelihood, procedure and cultural conditions; Articles 25 and 26 religious and sacred-landscape claims; Article 29(1) distinct language, script or culture; Article 46 special care and protection from exploitation; Article 300A deprivation only by authority of law.
- Article 338A: National Commission for Scheduled Tribes, with a duty on the Union and every State to consult it on all major policy matters affecting Scheduled Tribes. Institutional detail belongs to the commissions topic.
- Custom under PESA section 4(a) and Sixth Schedule paragraph 3(1)(j) is constitutionally relevant but not immune; autonomy protects against external domination, not internal discrimination.
- Five channels: electoral, legal, fiscal, administrative, social.
- Characteristic failures: controlled TAC agenda; unpublished and unstandardised reports; assent delay; entrusted functions without staff and funds; thin Gram Sabha records; incomplete land and forest-rights records; untracked restoration.
- Several channels are discretionary in their trigger, which is the design-level criticism.

## Implementation status and reform

- Four levels: central statute; State Panchayat law conformity; State PESA Rules; sectoral law conformity.
- All ten Fifth Schedule States enacted compliance legislation amending their Panchayati Raj Acts.
- Official central material and parliamentary replies traced to 2026 show State PESA Rules notified in **nine of the ten** States, with **Odisha alone at draft stage** (Rajya Sabha unstarred reply answered 25 March 2026) and the **Jharkhand PESA Rules, 2025** — Cabinet-approved 23 December 2025, notified in the first week of January 2026 — the most recent addition. Quote with the date; rules are a status, not an outcome.
- Section 4(m) powers are blocked where excise, forest transit, market, money-lending, land, service and scheme rules retain the operative lever.
- Capacity agenda: local-language power cards; meeting notice, quorum and inclusion; geo-referenced privacy-sensitive records; a written convergence protocol; TAC calendar and action-taken statements; assent timelines with reasons; activity, staff and fund mapping; accessible appeal, legal aid and restoration tracking.
- Tribal development agencies and convergence programmes are administrative and State-specific; they cannot replace a TAC, a Gram Sabha or an Autonomous District Council, and cannot waive a statutory requirement. Avoid undated figures.

## Extension debates and reform compass

- Ladakh: a Union territory without a legislature since 2019; the Leh Apex Body and the Kargil Democratic Alliance couple Statehood with Sixth Schedule inclusion. Reported 22 May 2026 — an in-principle understanding on safeguards "on the lines of Article 371 A, F, and G" as applied to Nagaland, Sikkim and Mizoram. Reported 9 September 2026 — a Ministry of Home Affairs proposal for a directly elected Union-territory body with legislative powers over land, culture and language, forest, environment and natural resources, under a new special provision in **Article 371** which reporting labels "Chapter K" or "Article 371K"; no detailed draft shared. Those are press labels: Part XXI contains Article 371 and Articles 371A to 371J as separate articles, and Article 240 — quoted in the reported statement — empowers the President to make regulations for the Union territories it enumerates rather than distributing legislative subjects. Ladakh is **not** among those enumerated in Article 240(1); the power reaches it through section 58(2) of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019, which is a statutory application of Article 240 and not a constitutional listing. Reported 14 September 2026 — the Ladakh bodies indicated willingness to consider dropping Statehood if the elected-body model meets popular aspirations, Statehood remaining their stated long-term aspiration. Reported 22 September 2026 — the Chief Secretary said the proposed constitutional amendment would be considered by Parliament after consensus on the framework. **As on 1 October 2026 Statehood was not granted, no constitutional amendment was enacted and Ladakh had not been brought within the Sixth Schedule.**
- Stages to keep distinct: demand, dialogue, in-principle understanding, proposal, enacted legal change.
- Route: Article 244(2) must be altered to bring a new unit within the Schedule's field; paragraph 21 cannot do it. That clause has been altered by constitutional amendment — the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984, **section 2**, effective 1 April 1985, adding Tripura, with **section 4** of the same Act inserting Part IIA of the paragraph 20 table, paragraph 20(3) and paragraph 12AA — and by ordinary reorganisation law under Articles 3 and 4, which Article 4(2) declares not to be an Article 368 amendment: the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 (81 of 1971), section 71, effective 21 January 1972, and the State of Mizoram Act, 1986 (34 of 1986), section 39, effective 20 February 1987. Those two were consequential on areas already inside the Schedule — in 1971 as parts of Assam, in 1986 as the Union territory of Mizoram, named in Article 244(2) from 1972 to 1987; Tripura, a first-time extension, needed Article 368. Either way, consequential Schedule changes follow — a paragraph 20 table entry and machinery adaptation — carried by whichever instrument is being used: under paragraph 21 where it is an ordinary amending law, by the reorganisation law's own sections as the 1971 Act did through section 71(i) and its Eighth Schedule, or by the amending Act itself as section 4 of the Forty-ninth Amendment did in 1984. Statehood is a separate question: an ordinary law under Article 3, which reaches a Union territory through Explanation I and is not an Article 368 amendment by force of Article 4(2). Paragraph 20C shows only the Union-territory adaptation technique, for a unit Article 244(2) already named, and it assumed a Legislative Assembly.
- A new special provision in Part XXI would need an Article 368 amendment, because no ordinary law reaches Part XXI. Statehood would not. The Sixth Schedule route depends on how Article 244(2) is altered; a first-time extension outside a reorganisation law means Article 368.
- Six-question test: ST population; named risks; territorial compactness; customary institutions; relation with the legislature; internal minority safeguards.
- Eight reform principles: territorial accuracy; subsidiarity; legally precise participation; secure records; accountable supervision; council capacity; rights-respecting sequencing; transparent review with remedies.

## Last-minute PYQ routes and traps

| Year and question | Demand | How to work it |
|---|---|---|
| 2019 Prelims Q50 | Which Schedule carries a power that can invalidate a transfer of tribal land for mining | Lesson 6 for the protective regulation power over land in a protected tribal territory; Lesson 17 for how far a judgment on one State's regulation travels |
| 2022 Prelims Q73 | Consequences of bringing an area under the Fifth Schedule | Demand only; exact wording unverified, nothing reconstructed |
| 2023 Prelims Q39 | Notification route, administrative units and the reporting chain for a Scheduled Area | Lesson 3 for the instrument and the identification criteria; Lesson 4 for the reporting duty and who owes it to whom |
| 2024 Prelims Q84 | Who specifies a Scheduled Tribe, and whether the status travels across States | Lesson 2, the Article 342 section |
| 2025 Prelims Q56 | What declaring a Scheduled Area does to State executive power and to Union involvement | Lesson 4, which teaches both limbs of the Fifth Schedule executive chain |
| 2026 Prelims Q57 | Schedule allocation, a tax entitlement and a Panchayat reservation, then a conclusion about the set | Lesson 2 for the Schedule allocation; the other two limbs belong to neighbouring topics; then read each conclusion as a conclusion |
| 2026 GS-II Q6 | Tribal development policy and equity-based governance | Cross-topic; this topic supplies the self-governance component |
| 2026 GS-III Q10 | Border-area development and the Ladakh Sixth Schedule demand | Cross-topic; this topic supplies the third limb |

1. Fifth is Article 244(1); Sixth is Article 244(2).
2. President declares the Scheduled Area; Governor organises the autonomous district.
3. The Governor does not notify the Scheduled Tribe list.
4. State executive power continues; the Union directs.
5. The Tribes Advisory Council advises on referred matters.
6. Fifth Schedule regulation goes to the President; Sixth Schedule council law goes to the Governor — except in Assam, where a Concurrent-List council law is reserved for the President.
7. "Not more than thirty" and "not more than four nominated"; Bodoland is special.
8. Reserved forests are outside paragraph 3; minor minerals only under PESA.
9. Application of laws varies by State across paragraphs 12, 12A, 12AA and 12B.
10. PESA applies to the Fifth, not the Sixth.
11. Consultation is not consent; recommendation is not approval.
12. The Ministry retained the final Stage-II decision at Niyamgiri.

## Rapid Mains spines

**Fifth Schedule**

> Article 244(1) -> Presidential territory under paragraph 6 -> State administers under paragraph 2 -> Governor reports and the Union directs under paragraph 3 -> advisory council under paragraph 4 -> law filter and protective regulations under paragraph 5 -> Articles 275 and 339 -> PESA and forest rights -> dormancy and record failures -> qualified protective-governance verdict.

**Sixth Schedule**

> Article 244(2) -> listed tribal areas and gubernatorial organisation under paragraph 1 -> councils under paragraph 2 with the 1995 and 2003 Assam enlargements -> legislative, executive, judicial and fiscal powers under paragraphs 3 to 10 -> State-specific application of laws under paragraphs 12 to 12B -> inquiry, annulment and dissolution under paragraphs 14 to 16 -> capacity and internal representation -> bounded-self-government verdict.

**PESA**

> Article 243M(1) exclusion -> 243M(4)(b) enabling -> habitation village under section 4(b) -> electoral-roll Gram Sabha under 4(c) -> exact verbs across 4(e) to 4(m) -> sectoral-law conformity -> forest-rights linkage -> records, capacity and remedy -> calibrated-community-power verdict.

**Land and mining**

> Territorial status -> applicable State land-transfer regulation -> forest-rights determination -> correct PESA verb for the stage -> environment, mineral and acquisition law -> *Samatha* and Niyamgiri with their limits -> cumulative-compliance verdict.

## Twenty-second recall

```text
FIFTH : President area | State administers | Governor reports and regulates
        TAC advises | President assents | PESA and forest rights add agency

SIXTH : Constitution lists | Governor organises | elected councils legislate
        village courts | land revenue and taxes | royalty share
        Governor assents (President for Assam Concurrent-List
        council laws) | State Legislature supervises

BOTH  : amendable by ordinary parliamentary law
        bounded by Part III
        enforceable only through records, capacity and remedies
```

| Article 342 | Clause (1) Presidential notification with Governor consultation for a State; clause (2) parliamentary alteration and the bar on later variation |
| Fifth Schedule | Paragraphs 1 to 7 in full, including the paragraph 4(1) proviso, the illustrative heads in 5(2), the repeal-or-amend power in 5(3), the assent condition in 5(4), the conditional consultation in 5(5), the sub-clause structure of 6(2) with its closing words, and paragraph 7 |
| Sixth Schedule | Paragraphs 1 to 11, 12, 12A, 12AA, 12B, 13 to 17, 19, 20, 20A, 20B, **20BA, 20BB**, 20C and 21, including the eight lettered clauses of paragraph 1(3) — (a) to (f), (ff) and (g) — and its two provisos, the proviso to paragraph 2(1) governing the Bodoland Territorial Council, paragraph 2(6A) term and extension rules, the paragraph 3 heads with the reserved-forest exclusion and acquisition proviso, the paragraph 4 and 5 justice provisions, paragraph 7 audit arrangements, **the five lettered clauses of the paragraph 8(3) tax list including clause (e) on entertainment and amusements**, the paragraph 9 royalty mechanism, the paragraph 10 three-fourths majority, the complete paragraph 16 scheme with the thirty-day rule in 16(3) computed from the State Legislature's first sitting **and the 1988 variation of paragraph 16 in its application to Tripura and Mizoram**, the complete transitional scheme in paragraph 19(1)(a), 19(1)(b), 19(2), 19(3) **and 19(4) including the operative words of its proviso**, and the paragraph 20 table |
| Sixth Schedule, State-specific overlays verified this pass | **Assam:** paragraph 2(3) proviso renaming the North Cachar Hills and Karbi Anglong Councils; **paragraph 3(3) as substituted in 1995 and again in 2003**, with its opening words "Save as otherwise provided in sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3A or sub-paragraph (2) of paragraph 3B"; **paragraph 3A(1) heads (a) to (o), 3A(2) and 3A(3)** including the President's return-with-message proviso and six-month reconsideration; **paragraph 3B(1) heads (i) to (xl) with its land-rights proviso, 3B(2) and 3B(3)**; the paragraph 12(1)(a) extensions of 1995 and 2003; **the omission from paragraph 14(2) of the words "with the recommendations of the Governor with respect thereto"**; **paragraph 20BA with its list of functions and its compulsory consultation of the Council of Ministers and the named Autonomous Council**; and the four 2003 carve-outs — the proviso to paragraph 1(2), paragraph 4(6), paragraph 10(4) and the proviso to paragraph 17. **Tripura and Mizoram, 1988:** paragraph 9(3) one-year royalty direction; paragraph 10 with the heading words "by non-tribals" and the words "other than Scheduled tribes" omitted and clause (2)(d) substituted to reach every person resident in the district; paragraph 15 with "by him" substituted for "by the Legislature of the State" and its proviso omitted; **and paragraph 20BB with its shorter list of functions and its optional consultation of the District or Regional Council** |
| Articles of the Constitution | Articles 3 with Explanation I, Explanation II and the proviso, and Article 4(1) and 4(2); Articles 243M and 243ZC exclusions and extension clauses, **Article 244(1) and the full text of Article 244(2) together with the three amendment footnotes recording how that clause has been altered**, Article 244A including its two-thirds and non-Article-368 clauses, Article 275(1) with its provisos, Articles 339(1) and 339(2), Article 342(1) and 342(2), the amending mechanism in Article 368(2) and its proviso, and **Article 240(1) and 240(2)**, read to establish both what the regulation-making power is and that **Ladakh is not among the Union territories Article 240(1) enumerates**, together with **section 58 of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019**, whose sub-section (2) applies that power to the Union territory of Ladakh "under article 240" and whose sub-section (3) carries the repeal-or-amend and same-force rule. **Seventh Schedule, List III, Entry 5** was used only to explain why inheritance and marriage and divorce engage the Assam reservation requirement |
| Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 (55 of 1969) | Long title; section 1 commencement by notification, with the Act brought into force from 12 January 1970; section 2(a) and 2(c) definitions of "appointed day" and "autonomous State"; section 3(1) forming the autonomous State of Meghalaya within Assam from the United Khasi-Jaintia Hills and Garo Hills Districts, and 3(2) on accession by two-thirds District Council resolution; the section headings establishing a Council of Ministers (ss. 6 and 7), an Advocate-General (s. 8), a Legislative Assembly (ss. 11 to 32), the extent of legislative power (s. 33) and a Consolidated Fund (s. 51); and **section 74 with the Fourth Schedule amending the Sixth Schedule**. Used to establish that Article 244A has been exercised once |
| North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 (81 of 1971) and Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971 (83 of 1971) | Section 71 of the first, amending Article 244(1) and 244(2) with effect from 21 January 1972, and its section 71(i) with the Eighth Schedule omitting paragraph 18 and substituting paragraph 12A; section 13 of the second, omitting "The Mizo District" from Part III of the paragraph 20 table and substituting paragraph 20A with the present paragraphs 20A, 20B and 20C. **Published renderings differ on the commencement attributed to that section 13.** The rendering relied on here carries "w.e.f. 29-4-1972" against both footnotes, while the official apparatus is reported to carry 16 February 1972; in the same rendering the date 29 April 1972 is attached to the **Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972**, published in the Mizoram Gazette of 5 May 1972, Volume I, Part II, page 17, which inserted the three Part III districts for Mizoram. Serial numbers 2 and 3 of that Part, and the entries relating to them, were substituted later, by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1988 (67 of 1988), section 2, so the **Mara** and **Lai** names now shown there are of 1988 and not of 1972. Because the conflict is unresolved on the sources available, no commencement date is asserted for section 13 anywhere in the lessons; the 29 April 1972 date is used only where it is independently anchored, namely for the 1972 Order, which the lesson uses as the worked example of the paragraph 20B(1)(a) executive-order route |
| Provisions of the Panchayats (Extension to the Scheduled Areas) Act, 1996 | Objectives and the full sequence of section 4 clauses (a) to (o), including the main limb of 4(g) requiring reservation in proportion to community population with its two provisos, the one-tenth nomination ceiling in 4(h), the State-coordination limb of 4(i) and the seven endowments in 4(m); section 5 transition |
| Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006 | Gram Sabha initiation and the Sub-Divisional and District Level Committee chain under section 6; the community forest resource management right; conservation duties under section 5; the eviction bar under section 4(5). Full forest-law doctrine is outside this topic's scope and was not reproduced |

Where a freely accessible rendering of the Schedules showed footnote-marker artifacts in paragraph numbering, the standard paragraph numbers were used and cross-checked against the two OCR books and the canonical package. The proviso to paragraph 2(1) on the Bodoland Territorial Council and the main limb of PESA section 4(g) were each verified against a separate published rendering of the amended instrument because the primary rendering used elsewhere did not carry them.

The State-specific overlays were verified differently, and it is worth recording how. The base rendering of the Sixth Schedule used elsewhere in this edition prints the Schedule in its general form and carries the State-specific amendments in its **amendment footnotes**, which reproduce the amending words verbatim. Paragraphs 3A, 3(3) as substituted for Assam, 9(3), 10 and 15 as amended for Tripura and Mizoram, and 16 as amended for those two States were each taken from those footnotes. The complete **Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 2003 (44 of 2003)**, dated 7 September 2003, was read separately from a published rendering of the amending Act itself, which supplies paragraph 3B, the further substitution of paragraph 3(3), paragraph 4(6), paragraph 10(4), the proviso to paragraph 1(2), the proviso to paragraph 17, the paragraph 12(1)(a) extension, paragraph 19(4) and the paragraph 20 table entry. The **Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969** was read from a bare-act rendering current as on 1 October 2026 for its long title, sections 1, 2 and 3 and its section list; the appointed day of 2 April 1970 is corroborated by the Sixth Schedule's own footnotes, which record section 74 and the Fourth Schedule of that Act as taking effect with effect from 2 April 1970.

The colonial chronology in Lesson 1 — "scheduled districts" under the Scheduled Districts Act, 1874; "backward tracts" under the Government of India Act, 1919; and "excluded" and "partially excluded" areas created by sections 91 and 92 of the Government of India Act, 1935, with their notification-based application of laws and peace-and-good-government regulation power — was verified through a bounded live check, because the canonical package records the sequence without assigning each category to its creating enactment.

## Judicial sources and their stated limits

| Source | Use and limit |
|---|---|
| *Samatha v. State of Andhra Pradesh* (1997) | Taught as invalidating the impugned grants and leases of Scheduled-Area land to non-tribal private mining companies under the Andhra Pradesh Scheduled Areas land-transfer regulation read with the Fifth Schedule, with the State's position read as trustee-like. The wider reach beyond that regulatory setting is expressly recorded as debated, consistent with the canonical package and the Core owner's caution |
| *Orissa Mining Corporation Ltd. v. Ministry of Environment and Forest* (2013) | Taught as requiring Gram Sabha determination of forest-rights-linked individual, community, cultural and religious claims with State and ministry assistance and a judicial observer, while the Ministry retained the final Stage-II forest-clearance decision taken in the light of those decisions. The reported sequel — twelve Gram Sabhas rejecting the proposal in 2013 and the Ministry refusing Stage-II clearance in January 2014 — is presented as contemporary reporting rather than as part of the ratio |

The three alterations of Article 244(2) are verified from the amendment footnotes to that article: "Subs. by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 (81 of 1971), s. 71, for 'the State of Assam' (w.e.f. 21-1-1972)"; "Subs. by the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984, s. 2, for 'and Meghalaya' (w.e.f. 1-4-1985)"; and "Subs. by the State of Mizoram Act, 1986 (34 of 1986), s. 39, for 'Meghalaya and Tripura and the Union territory of Mizoram' (w.e.f. 20-2-1987)". The last of those footnotes is also the evidence that Article 244(2) named the Union territory of Mizoram between 1972 and 1987. The Forty-ninth Amendment's consequential Schedule insertions are verified from the Sixth Schedule's own footnote recording insertion "by the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984, s. 4 (w.e.f. 1-4-1985)", which carries both paragraph 20(3) and the Part IIA entry in the paragraph 20 table. That the 1971 reorganisation law also rewrote the Schedule's own text by its own sections, rather than under paragraph 21, is verified from two further Sixth Schedule footnotes: "Paragraph 18 omitted by s. 71(i) and Eighth Sch., ibid. (w.e.f. 21-1-1972)" and "Subs. by s. 71(i) and Eighth Sch., ibid. for paragraph 12A (w.e.f. 21-1-1972)". Three further instrument families were added this pass from the same footnote apparatus: "Subs. by the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969 (55 of 1969), s.74 and Fourth Sch., for sub-paragraph (1) (w.e.f. 2-4-1970)" against paragraph 2(1) and "Subs. by s. 74 and Fourth Sch., ibid., for paragraph 6 (w.e.f. 2-4-1970)" against paragraph 6; "The words 'The Mizo District.' omitted by the Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971 (83 of 1971), s. 13 (w.e.f. 29-4-1972)" and "Subs. by the Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971 (83 of 1971), s. 13, for paragraph 20A (w.e.f. 29-4-1972)" — with the commencement in those two footnotes treated as **unresolved**, because the official apparatus is reported to carry 16 February 1972 for the same section, so no date for section 13 is asserted in the lessons; and the two express executive-order powers read from the Schedule's own text, the second proviso to paragraph 1(3) permitting "any amendment of paragraph 20 and of any item in any of the Parts of the said table" and paragraph 20B(1)(a) permitting the Administrator to direct consequential amendments in paragraph 20 and Part III, the latter corroborated as actually exercised by the adjacent footnote "Ins. by the Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972, published in the Mizoram Gazette, 1972, dated the 5th May, 1972, Vol. I, Pt. II, p. 17 (w.e.f. 29-4-1972)", which inserted the three Part III districts, serial numbers 2 and 3 of which were substituted later by the 1988 Act. A sixth family instance was added this pass from the amending Act itself: section 16 of the Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016, which inserted clause (e) in paragraph 8(3) with effect from 16 September 2016, alongside that Act's section 17 substituting Entry 62 of the State List. Paragraph 12AA's own amendment footnote did not render in the rendering consulted; its attribution to section 4 of the Forty-ninth Amendment rests on the adjacent section-4 footnote of the same Act and date and on the published accounts of that Act, and the lesson states only that the same Act and section carried the Schedule work | Taught in Lesson 20 section 2 with its own visual, instrument map and amendment-history table, in the Lesson 20 concept check, revision note 14 and Mains model, in Lesson 12 section 6 as the boundary of paragraph 21, in Lesson 9's table of the three amending Acts, and in remediation error family 15. This corrects five earlier formulations: one treating paragraph 21 alone as sufficient, one treating Article 368 as the only means of altering Article 244(2), one attributing the 1972 Schedule adaptations to paragraph 21 instead of to the reorganisation law's own section 71(i) and Eighth Schedule, one presenting the instrument families as a closed set of three, and one asserting a single commencement date for section 13 of Act 83 of 1971 while attributing the Part III insertion of the three Mizoram districts to that section rather than to the 1972 Administrator's Order. Whether a future reorganisation law could carry a **first-time** extension is stated in the lesson as an open question, not as a route, and no section is assigned to a particular enabling power |

## Remaining uncertainty, disclosed

1. **Paragraph numbering of the Assam, Tripura and Mizoram discretion provisions.** Now verified and closed. The provisions are **paragraph 20BA**, inserted after paragraph 20B in its application to the State of Assam by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1995 (42 of 1995), section 2, and **paragraph 20BB**, which stands after paragraph 20BA in the Schedule as it now reads and applies to the States of Tripura and Mizoram, inserted by the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1988 (67 of 1988), section 2. Two things have to be kept apart there. The ordering is the **present position** of the paragraphs in the Schedule; the dates are the **insertions**. Paragraph 20BB is the earlier insertion, of 1988, and paragraph 20BA the later one, of 1995, so the 1988 operation cannot have placed paragraph 20BB after an already-existing paragraph 20BA. By what renumbering or repositioning the present order came about is not stated in the amendment footnotes consulted, and is therefore neither asserted nor reconstructed here. Both are reproduced verbatim in the Schedule's amendment footnotes, and both are now taught by number, with their operative formulas quoted, in Lesson 12. One caveat is recorded rather than resolved: the rendering of paragraph 20BA lists "sub-paragraph (3) of paragraph 9" among the functions it covers, although sub-paragraph (3) of paragraph 9 is itself an insertion made in 1988 for Tripura and Mizoram. Because that cannot be reconciled from the sources consulted, the lesson introduces the Assam list with "which includes" and leaves that item out of the enumeration rather than asserting an Assam paragraph 9(3).
2. **Exact text of paragraphs 3A and 3B.** Now verified. Paragraph 3A, inserted for Assam by the 1995 amendment, is taken from the amendment footnote to paragraph 3, which reproduces the inserted paragraph verbatim: fifteen law-making heads lettered (a) to (o) for the North Cachar Hills and Karbi Anglong Autonomous Councils, the duty in 3A(2) to submit Concurrent-List laws to the Governor "who shall reserve the same for the consideration of the President", and the 3A(3) scheme of assent, withholding and return with a message for reconsideration within six months. Paragraph 3B, inserted for Assam by the 2003 amendment, is taken from a published rendering of that amending Act: forty heads numbered (i) to (xl) for the Bodoland Territorial Council, a proviso protecting existing land rights, and sub-paragraphs (2) and (3) in the same terms as 3A(2) and 3A(3). The full enumerated lists are summarised rather than reproduced head by head, and no individual head is attributed beyond what those renderings carry.
3. **Bodoland Territorial Council composition.** Now verified against the proviso to paragraph 2(1) of the Sixth Schedule as amended in 2003: **not more than forty-six** members, of whom forty are elected on adult suffrage with thirty reserved for Scheduled Tribes, five for non-tribal communities and five open to all communities, and six nominated by the Governor from the unrepresented communities of the Bodoland Territorial Areas District, of whom at least two shall be women, with the same rights and privileges including voting rights. It is taught as a special constitutional arrangement that must not be generalised, and the ceiling is stated as a ceiling rather than as a fixed strength.
4. **PESA Act number and date of assent.** Not asserted anywhere in this edition, because they were not verified against a primary instrument in this pass. The statute is referred to by its full title and year.
5. **State-level PESA rule status.** Rule listings and draft-stage positions change. The nine-of-ten position is stated with the parliamentary replies that carry it, with the Jharkhand notification dated from contemporary reporting rather than from the gazette itself, and with an explicit caution that notification is not an implementation outcome.
6. **Ladakh position.** The May and September 2026 developments rest on contemporary newspaper reporting — *The New Indian Express* of 22 May, 14 September and 22 September 2026 and *The Hindu* of 10 September 2026 — rather than on a published official record of the meetings or a released draft. No draft text had been shared with the Ladakh delegations on the reporting relied on. "Chapter K" and "Article 371K" are press labels for a proposal and are identified as such in Lesson 20; Part XXI contains Article 371 and Articles 371A to 371J, and none of them concerns Ladakh. The reported phrase "any other subject matter reserved for the UT under Article 240" is quoted, not adopted: Article 240 empowers the President to make regulations for the Union territories enumerated in Article 240(1) and does not distribute legislative subjects. Ladakh is not enumerated there — the four entries are the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu, and Puducherry — and the power reaches Ladakh only through section 58(2) of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019, verified from a published rendering of that section. One qualification on the enumeration itself: the rendering of Article 240(1) consulted in this pass still printed the pre-merger entries "Dadra and Nagar Haveli" and "Daman and Diu" separately and the pre-2006 name "Pondicherry"; the current names are used in the lesson, and Ladakh appears in neither the older nor the current form of the clause, which is the point the lesson rests on. Statehood, Sixth Schedule inclusion and any constitutional amendment are each recorded as not enacted as on 1 October 2026, and the reported willingness to consider an elected-body model instead of Statehood is stated as conditional and not as withdrawal of the demand.
7. **Niyamgiri sequel.** The 2013 Gram Sabha outcomes and the January 2014 clearance refusal rest on contemporary reporting and are labelled as such; the judgment's own ratio stops at the requirement that the Ministry decide in the light of the Gram Sabha decisions.
8. **Land-transfer regulations.** Specific State regulation titles, section numbers, limitation periods and remedies are deliberately not quoted, because they vary across the ten States and were not individually verified in this pass.
9. **Whether a reorganisation law could carry a first-time Sixth Schedule extension.** The amendment footnotes to Article 244 establish as fact that the clause was altered by the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971 and by the State of Mizoram Act, 1986, both ordinary laws under Articles 3 and 4, and by the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 under Article 368. In both reorganisation cases the areas concerned were already inside the Schedule — in 1971 as parts of Assam, and in 1986 as the Union territory of Mizoram, which Article 244(2) had named since 1972 — so each amendment preserved an existing application rather than creating a new one. Whether "supplemental, incidental and consequential" in Article 4(1) would stretch to bringing territory the Schedule has never covered within Article 244(2) was not resolved by any authority consulted in this pass. Lesson 20 presents it as a bounded constitutional inference and takes no position on it.
10. **The enabling power behind each instrument that amended the Sixth Schedule.** Six families of instrument are taught, each verified from the Schedule's own amendment footnotes or from the amending Act: paragraph 21 in 1988, 1995 and 2003; section 74 and the Fourth Schedule of the Assam Reorganisation (Meghalaya) Act, 1969; section 71(i) and the Eighth Schedule of the North-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act, 1971; section 13 of the Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971; section 4 of the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 and section 16 of the Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016; and executive orders authorised by the second proviso to paragraph 1(3) and by paragraph 20B(1)(a), the second of which was exercised by the Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972. Each of the parliamentary instruments is a law of Parliament, and no authority consulted in this pass decides whether a particular section is referable to paragraph 21 or to the Act's own enabling provision. The lessons therefore name the **instrument** that carried each change and do not assign it to a power. The list is presented as the verified set, not as an exhaustive inventory of every amendment the Schedule has received.
11. **Date of insertion of Article 244A.** Now verified. The Constitution (Twenty-second Amendment) Act, 1969 bears the date **25 September 1969** in the published rendering consulted, and its only commencement-related provision is the short-title section, so Article 244A is taken to have operated from that date. The rendering also confirms that section 2 of that Act inserted Article 244A, section 3 inserted Article 275(1-A) and section 4 inserted Article 371B, and that clause (1) as enacted referred to "Part A" of the paragraph 20 table before the 1971 reorganisation law substituted "Part I". The other dates stated in Lesson 2 — 29 December 1969 for the enactment of Act 55 of 1969, 12 January 1970 for its first commencement, 2 April 1970 for the appointed day and 21 January 1972 for Meghalaya's Statehood — each rest on the sources recorded above.
12. **Whether a given council law "relates to" a Concurrent List matter.** Paragraphs 3A(2) and 3B(2) turn on that relation, and the Schedule leaves the judgment to the reserving authority in the first instance. The lessons state the rule, the trigger and the clearest illustration — Entry 5 of List III covering inheritance and marriage and divorce — and expressly decline to predict the outcome for any particular law.
13. **Commencement of section 13 of the Government of Union Territories (Amendment) Act, 1971, and the later history of paragraph 12AA.** The rendering of the Sixth Schedule relied on here records both the omission of "The Mizo District." from Part III and the substitution of paragraph 20A as made by section 13 of Act 83 of 1971 "w.e.f. 29-4-1972", while the official apparatus is reported to carry 16 February 1972 for that same section; the same rendering attaches 29 April 1972 to the Mizoram District Councils (Miscellaneous Provisions) Order, 1972, which inserted the three Part III districts; serial numbers 2 and 3 of that Part were substituted later, by section 2 of the 1988 Act, so the names now shown against them are of 1988. The conflict could not be resolved from any source reachable in this pass, so **no commencement date is asserted for section 13 anywhere in the lessons**, and the 29 April 1972 date is used only where it is independently anchored, for the 1972 Order. Separately, the amendment footnote attached to paragraph 12AA did not render at all in the source consulted; the lessons therefore say only that section 4 of the Constitution (Forty-ninth Amendment) Act, 1984 carried that Act's Schedule work, and a reported later substitution of paragraph 12AA by the 1988 Act is neither asserted nor denied.
